Non-deterministic, probabilistic, and quantum effects through the lens of event structures (Technical report)
Vítor Fernandes, Marc de Visme, Benoît Valiron

Abstract




1
Introduction

Concurrency is pervasive in modern computer architecture. Starting in the early 1960s, the study of
its semantics, both operational and denotational, and within different paradigms (from interleaving
to the so-called true concurrency) became a highly active research area with concrete implications
in language design.

In the interleaving paradigm, saying that two atomic actions
a
and
b
are in parallel is
interpreted as
a
then
b
or
b
then
a
. On the other hand, from a true concurrent point of
view, the same command is interpreted as
a
and
b
, which are causally unrelated. We focus on the
latter interpretation for which event structures
[
16
,
18
]
are a known model.

An event structure is a partial order with a conflict relation on events. If
a
and
b
are in
conflict, then they are incompatible events,
i.e.
they cannot be performed in the same
computation. Furthermore, event structures are very flexible, and proof of that is the fact that
they have been used to study several computational effects: parallelism
[
18
]
,
probabilities
[
13
,
15
,
6
]
, quantum effects
[
5
,
19
]
,
shared weak memory
[
3
]
, etc.

Despite all the work around event structures on different computational effects, when the goal is to
provide denotational semantics to a programming language, they seem to play a secondary role. More
often than not, they serve as the backbone of some much more complex models, such as games and
strategies
[
4
,
10
,
5
]
. Some exceptions are the works of
Winskel
[
18
,
16
]
, in which he used event structures to give denotational semantics
to CCS
[
9
]
, and Marc de Visme
[
6
]
, in which two notions of conflict are used
in order to accommodate both probabilistic and non-deterministic choices in a probabilistic
extension of CCS
[
2
]
, who have used event structures as the primary model.

Contribution.

In this paper, we aim at giving event structures the leading role as a computational model. Our
work combines parallelism with three different algebraic effects: non-determinism, probabilities,
and quantum. For each algebraic effect, we propose a small imperative-style programming language
together with suitable operational semantics, wherein for the non-deterministic and quantum cases,
we used a simple labeled transition system – or, in the probabilistic case, a labeled Segala
automaton
[
11
,
12
]
.

We rely on different flavors of event structures. For the non-deterministic case, we use the event
structures defined by Winskel
[
18
]
as a base model. For the probabilistic case, we use
probabilistic event structures
[
19
]
. For the quantum case, we consider a restriction of
the definition in
[
19
]
, which we call Unitary event structures. This modification allows
us to extend
[
19
, Theorem 3]
, which states that quantum event structures without events
in conflict are probabilistic event structures when given an initial state, by dropping the
necessity of having an empty conflict relation.

We also show that the operational and denotational semantics are sound and adequate for the three
different algebraic effects considered. We do it by checking that the words created by the
operational semantics and the covering chains in event structures, which are essentially finite
sequences of events, coincide.

Remark.

The majority of the proofs are put in appendix to improve readability.

2
Event Structures

In the imperative setting, the evaluation of a program is commonly accompanied by a memory that
changes accordingly the execution of said program, where each step performed by the computation is
not labeled. On the other side we have a process algebra approach, in which states are dropped and
each step of the computation is labeled by the action that caused the occurrence of the computation.
Although we intend to model an imperative language, our approach is similar to the latter. This
decision comes from the use of event structures. By dropping the state we can use the usual
definitions of event structures
[
17
,
16
]
. Since we want to model an imperative
language, we need to have the notion of state. Well, since we label the transitions we perform, we
can create a trace of the actions that were performed. By doing this, we can apply each instruction
in the trace to a given state.

Informally, an event structure
[
18
]
is composed of a set of events, together with a
notion of causality given by a partial order on events: if
e\leq e^{\prime}
then
e^{\prime}
depends on
e

(another way of interpreting
e\leq e^{\prime}
is
e^{\prime}
occurs after
e
), and a notion of conflict between
events: if
e\#e^{\prime}
then either
e
occurs or
e^{\prime}
occurs, which is a behavior similar to a
non-deterministic choice.

Definition 2.1

(Event Structures)
.

Define an
event structure
to be a structure
\mathrm{E}=(E,\,\leq,\,\#)
consisting of a
set
E
of events, which are partially ordered by
\leq
, the
causal dependency relation
,
and a binary, symmetric, irreflexive relation
\#\subseteq E\times E
, the
conflict
relation
, satisfying:

•

\{e^{\prime}\mid e^{\prime}\leq e\}
is finite

•

e\#e^{\prime}\leq e^{\prime\prime}\Rightarrow e\#e^{\prime\prime}

for all
e,e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime}\in E
.

Summing up, the first condition tells us that the downward closure of an event
e
must be finite,

i.e.
the set of events that
e
causally depends on needs to be finite, and the second condition
tells us that the conflict relation is hereditary.

Definition 2.2

(Concurrent Event)
.

Two events,
e,e^{\prime}
are said
concurrent
iff

\neg(e\leq e^{\prime})\wedge\neg(e^{\prime}\leq e)\wedge\neg(e\#e^{\prime})
. In other words, two events are
concurrent when they are not causally dependent and are not in conflict.

Definition 2.3

(Configuration)
.

A configuration is a subset of the set of events,
x\subseteq E
, that are

\displaystyle\text{conflict-free: }\forall e,e^{\prime}\in x\ .\ \neg(e\#e^{\prime})

\displaystyle\text{down-closed: }\forall e,e^{\prime}\ .\ e^{\prime}\leq e\wedge e\in x\Rightarrow e^{\prime}\in x

We then denote by
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathrm{E})
the set of all configurations and by

\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{E})
the set of finite configurations.

Definition 2.4

(Covering chain)
.

Let
\mathrm{E}=(E,\,\leq,\,\#)
be a event structure,
e\in E
, and

x\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{E})
. Denote by
x\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}\ }}x\cup\{e\}
if
e\not\in x

and
(x\cup\{e\})\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{E})
. A
covering chain
on a configuration

x\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{E})
is a finite sequence of events
e_{1}e_{2}\dots e_{n}
such that

\emptyset\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e_{1}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}\ }}x_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e_{2}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}\ }}x_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e_{3}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}\ }}\dots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e_{n}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}\ }}x_{n+1}=x

Definition 2.5

(Cover)
.

Let
\mathrm{E}
be a event structure and
x,y\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{E})
. Say that
y
covers

x
, pictured as
x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}\ y
, if
x\subset y
with nothing in between
(
\not\exists z\ .\ x\subset z\subset y
).

Later on we may find useful to say
y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e_{1},\dots e_{n}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ x_{1},\dots x_{n}
when

y\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}\ x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
.

Definition 2.6

(Maximal configuration)
.

Let
\mathrm{E}
be a event structure and
x\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{E})
. Say that
x
is a
maximal configuration iff
\not\exists y\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{E})
such that
x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}\ y
.
Denote by
\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\mathrm{E})
the set of maximal configurations.

An interesting property of maximal configurations is that two distinct maximal configurations have
events in conflict. This is useful when showing the validity of sequential composition’s definition
in the probabilistic realm (Lemma
3.6
).

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
\mathrm{E}
be an event structure and
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\mathrm{E})
. If
x\neq x^{\prime}
then

\exists e\in x,e^{\prime}\in x^{\prime}
such that
e\#e^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\mathrm{E})
such that
x\neq x^{\prime}
.

Pick
e\in x\backslash x^{\prime}=\{e\in x\mid e\not\in x^{\prime}\}
and let

M=\{a\in E\mid a\leq e\text{ and }a\not\in x^{\prime}\}
be the set of events that
e
causally
depends on and not in
x^{\prime}
. Clearly we have
M\neq\emptyset
, since
e\in M
. By
Definition
2.1
,
\{e^{\prime}\mid e^{\prime}\leq e\}
is finite. Thus we can choose
a
minimal in
M

with respect to
\leq
,
i.e.

\forall a^{\prime}<a\ .\ a^{\prime}\not\in M
(in other words, choose the first
event
a
that
e
causally depends on that is not in
x^{\prime}
). Then every
a^{\prime}\in E
such that

a^{\prime}<a
is in
x^{\prime}
,
i.e.

a^{\prime}\in x^{\prime}
. Thus,
x^{\prime}\cup\{a\}
is downward closed. Since
x^{\prime}
is
maximal,
x^{\prime}\cup\{a\}
cannot be a configuration, hence it must fail conflict-freeness:

\exists e^{\prime}\in x^{\prime}
such that
a\#e^{\prime}
. By hereditary of conflict,
a\leq e
and
a\#e^{\prime}
, we
have
e\#e^{\prime}
.
∎

Later on we shall find useful to simplify how covering chains are represented.
We then let
\omega=e_{1}e_{2}\dots e_{n}
and denote

\emptyset\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e_{1}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}\ }}x_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e_{2}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}\ }}x_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e_{3}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}\ }}\dots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e_{n}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}\ }}x_{n+1}=x
simply by
\emptyset\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}\ }}x
.

Since the causal relation is a partial order we know that it is transitive. Furthermore, the
conflict relation is hereditary over events. Hence if we want to draw an event structure using these
two relations we would have to add a lot of redundant information, which would make the event
structure hard to understand. To ease such task, we find it useful to use the notions of

immediate causality
, pictured by
\rightarrowtriangle
, and
minimal conflict
,
represented by
\mathrel{\!\!\!\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}{\hbox{\kern 17.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\!\!\!\!}
. Let
\mathrm{E}
be an event structure such that
e_{1},e_{2}\in E
. We
say
e_{1}\rightarrowtriangle e_{2}
iff
e_{1}<e_{2}
and
\not\exists e_{3}\in E\,.\,e_{1}<e_{3}<e_{2}
.
We say
e_{1}\mathrel{\!\!\!\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}{\hbox{\kern 17.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\!\!\!\!}e_{2}
iff
e_{1}\#e_{2}
and whenever
e^{\prime}_{1}\leq e_{1}
,
e^{\prime}_{1}\#e^{\prime}_{2}
, and

e^{\prime}_{2}\leq e_{2}
we have
e_{1}=e^{\prime}_{1}
and
e_{2}=e^{\prime}_{2}
. Note that it is possible to deduce the causal
and conflict relations from the immediate causality and minimal conflict relations.

Example
2.8
aims to get the reader familiarized with event structures.

Example 2.8
.

Figure
1
shows an event structure with four events,
a
,
b
,
c
, and
d
,
where:
b
causally depends on
a
,
c
and
d
are concurrent events which are in conflict
with
a
, and consequently also with
b
. Furthermore, note that
a
is in minimal conflict
with
c
and
d
. The set of configurations,
i.e.
the set of possible computations, is

\set{\emptyset,\set{a},\set{c},\set{d},\set{a,b},\set{c,d}}
. Furthermore note that the
configuration
\set{c,d}
, which is composed of two concurrent events, has two possible
covering chains:
\emptyset\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ \set{d}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ \set{c,d}
and

\emptyset\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ \set{c}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ \set{c,d}
.

a
b
c
d

Figure 1
:
Example of an event structure

The last concept we will introduce in this section is that of a map of event structures, which can
be total or partial. Its usefulness will be demonstrated when showing that unitary event structures
with an initial state correspond to probabilistic event structures. Intuitively, a map of event
structures
f:\mathrm{E}\rightarrow\mathrm{E}^{\prime}
tells how the occurrence of an event
e
in
\mathrm{E}

implies the occurrence of
f(e)=e^{\prime}
in
\mathrm{E}^{\prime}
. To be more precise, if
x
in
\mathrm{E}
,
i.e.

x\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ \ x\cup\{e\}
then
f[x]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f(e)}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ \ f[x\cup\{e\}]
in
\mathrm{E}^{\prime}
, whenever

f(e)
is defined. In words, this means that if an event
e
can be added to a configuration
x
to
form a new configuration
x\cup\{e\}
in
\mathrm{E}
, then the same behavior holds in
\mathrm{E}^{\prime}
when

f(e)
is defined. Formally, a map of event structures is defined as follows:

Definition 2.9

(Map event structures)
.

Let
\mathrm{E}=(E,\,\leq,\,\#),\mathrm{E}^{\prime}=(E^{\prime},\,\leq^{\prime},\,\#^{\prime})
be event structures. A
partial/total map
f
from
\mathrm{E}
to
\mathrm{E}^{\prime}
is a partial/total function

f:E\rightharpoonup E^{\prime}
such that:

(Configuration Preserving)

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{E})\Rightarrow f(x)\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{E}^{\prime})

(Locally injective)

\displaystyle\forall(a\neq b)\in x\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{E}),\text{ if $f$ is defined in both then }f(a)\neq f(b)

where
f[x]=\set{f(e)\mid e\in x,\ f(e)\text{ defined}}

The preserving configuration condition is self-explanatory. It suffices to recall what was
previously said,
i.e.
that if an event
e
is added to a configuration
x
to form a new
configuration
x\cup\{e\}
in
\mathrm{E}
, then the same behavior holds in
\mathrm{E}^{\prime}
, when
f(e)
is
defined. The locally injective condition assures that no two distinct events, under the same
configuration, are mapped to the same image event. This prevents concurrent events, or causally
dependent events from being mapped to the same event. To understand why this condition is
necessary, let us explore an example illustrating a spurious map between event structures.

Example 2.10
.

Consider the event structures in Figure
2
linked by a broken map of event
structures.

a
c
b
a
b
f

Figure 2
:
Broken map of event structures

The function
f
maps
c
to
a
and applies the identity on
a
and
b
. Let us denote the event
structure on the left by
\mathrm{E}
and the one on the right by
\mathrm{E}^{\prime}
. The set of configurations
of
\mathrm{E}
is
\{\emptyset,\{a\},\{b\},\{a,c\},\{a,b\},\allowbreak\{a,b,c\}\}
and the set of
configurations of
\mathrm{E}^{\prime}
is
\{\emptyset,\{a\},\{b\},\{a,b\}\}
.

It is not difficult to check that the configurations of
\mathrm{E}
are preserved. However, the map
becomes invalid because it does not respect the local injective condition. Let us consider two
specific configurations from
\mathrm{E}
:
\{a,b\}
and
\{a,c\}
. The local injective condition is
satisfied for
\{a,b\}
, since
a
and
b
belong to
\{a,b\}
and are mapped to themselves. On
the other hand, the configuration
\{a,c\}
fails to satisfy the local injective condition, since

a
and
c
are mapped to
a
,
i.e.
causally related events are being mapped to the same image
event. Thus, different events are mapped to the same event, which contradicts the locally
injective condition.

Another way to understand the local injective condition is by looking at the size of the
configurations, especially when the map is total. If the map is total, the size of the
configurations must remain the same. In other words, if a configuration has
n
events in

\mathrm{E}
, the corresponding configuration in
\mathrm{E}^{\prime}
must also have
n
events.

We now develop an example that shows a valid map of event structures.

Example 2.11
.

In Figure
3
, we have a map of event structures
f
that maps
a
to itself and the
conflicting events
c
and
b
to
d
. The map
f
is total, and it is valid because it preserves
configurations and obeys local injectivity. Each configuration in
\mathrm{E}
is mapped to a
configuration in
\mathrm{E}^{\prime}
, and distinct events within the same configuration are mapped to
distinct events. Furthermore, since
f
is total, we notice that the size of covering chains are
preserved. Consider the covering chain
\emptyset\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ \set{c}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ \set{a,c}
.
The respective covering chain after applying
f
is

\emptyset\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ \set{d}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ \set{a,d}
. This is only possible because the local
injective condition ensures that different events in the same configuration must map to distinct
events.

a
b
c
a
d
e
f

Figure 3
:
Map event structures

Although the event structure on the right has an event
e
that is not in the image of
f
, this
does not invalidate the map. The reason is that
e
is not causally required by any event in the
image of
f
.

With these definitions presented, we are now prepared to advance to the next stage, where we present
the language that we intend to model with event structures,
i.e.
its syntax and respective
operational semantics in terms of a small-step and n-step. After presenting the language, we
present the constructions made on event structures to capture the behavior of the language
operator’s. Then we show how to interpret commands of the language using event structures and, at
last, we show that both semantics are sound and adequate.

2.1
Language

To present the language we consider a set of atomic actions
Act
ranged over by
a
(examples of
atomic actions are assignments, or unitary application, etc…).

The set of commands allowed by the language are given by the following grammar:

C::=skip\mid a\in Act\mid C\,;\,C\mid C\,\square\,C\mid C\,||\,C

To define the operational semantics, we add a new command to the language, denoted by
\checkmark
,
that indicates the end of a computation.

We denote by
L=Act\cup\set{sk}
the set of labels, which is ranged by
l
, and we consider a

terminal command
, denoted by
\checkmark
, representing the end of a computation. The
small-step semantics is then defined as the smallest relation

\xlongrightarrow{l}\subseteq C\times L\times(C\cup\set{\checkmark})
obeying the rules in
Figure
4
.

\displaystyle skip\xrightarrow{sk}\checkmark\hskip 28.45274pta\xrightarrow{a}\checkmark\hskip 28.45274ptC_{1}\,;\,C_{2}\xrightarrow{l}C_{2}C_{1}\xrightarrow{l}\checkmark\hskip 28.45274ptC_{1}\,;\,C_{2}\xrightarrow{l}C^{\prime}_{1}\,;\,C_{2}C_{1}\xrightarrow{l}C^{\prime}_{1}

\displaystyle C_{1}\,\square\,C_{2}\xrightarrow{l}\checkmark C_{1}\xrightarrow{l}\checkmark\hskip 28.45274ptC_{1}\,\square\,C_{2}\xrightarrow{l}C^{\prime}_{1}C_{1}\xrightarrow{l}C^{\prime}_{1}\hskip 28.45274ptC_{1}\,\square\,C_{2}\xrightarrow{l}\checkmark C_{2}\xrightarrow{l}\checkmark\hskip 28.45274ptC_{1}\,\square\,C_{2}\xrightarrow{l}C^{\prime}_{2}C_{2}\xrightarrow{l}C^{\prime}_{2}

\displaystyle C_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\xrightarrow{l}C_{2}C_{1}\xrightarrow{l}\checkmark\hskip 28.45274ptC_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\xrightarrow{l}C^{\prime}_{1}\,||\,C_{2}C_{1}\xrightarrow{l}C^{\prime}_{1}\hskip 28.45274ptC_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\xrightarrow{l}C_{1}C_{2}\xrightarrow{l}\checkmark\hskip 28.45274ptC_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\xrightarrow{l}C_{1}\,||\,C^{\prime}_{2}C_{2}\xrightarrow{l}C^{\prime}_{2}

Figure 4
:
Rules of the small-step operational semantics

Define a word to be a sequence of labels:

\omega::=l\mid l:\omega

where
l:\omega
appends
l
to the beginning of
\omega
. A word can also be seen as an element
of
L^{+}
,
i.e.
a possibly infinite sequence of labels without the empty sequence. Despite
L^{+}

allows the possibility of having infinite words, by now we focus only on the finite words.

Define the
n
-step transition,

\xrightarrow{\omega}\subseteq C\times L^{+}\times(C\cup\{\checkmark\})
, where
n
is the length of the words, as follows:

\displaystyle C\xrightarrow{l}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow C^{\prime}C\xrightarrow{l}C^{\prime}\hskip 56.9055ptC\xrightarrow{l:\omega^{\prime}}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow C^{\prime}C\xrightarrow{l}C^{\prime\prime}\qquad C^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{\omega^{\prime}}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow C^{\prime}

Figure 5
:
Rules of the n-step operational semantics

Example 2.12
.

The initial program is
a\,;\,b\,\square\,c\,||\,d
, from which we have
three possible transitions: by
a
,
c
or
d
. If we transit by
a
, we reach the command

b
, which we execute to finish the computation. Otherwise, we could either transit via
c

and then execute
d
, or transit via
d
and then execute
c
, in order to finish the computation.

With the support of Figure
7
together with the above explanation, we can
straightforwardly deduce the words that can be formed by the n-step semantics:
a
,

,

,

,

, and

.

.

The initial program is
(a\,;\,b)\,||\,c
, from which we have two possible

or by

. If we transit by

we go to the command

a\,;\,b
, where we execute
a
followed by

to complete the computation. On

, we reach the command

, which

b
and then

or first

.

2.2
Constructions on Event Structures

Having defined the language,
i.e.
its syntax and operational semantics, we now focus on event
structures. We need to define the constructions on event structures that captures the effects of
sequential composition, non-deterministic choice, and parallel composition.

To capture the behavior of the language’s operators, we need to define them in terms of event
structures.

Let us begin with sequential composition. Consider
a\,;\,b
to be the sequential
combination of two actions,
a
and
b
. According to the rules in Figure
4
we
execute
b
after
a
has been executed, which with an event structure view means that

b
causally depends on
a
. As a first attempt to define sequential composition of two events
structures,
\mathrm{E}_{1}\,;\,\mathrm{E}_{2}
, one might try to connect every event of
\mathrm{E}_{1}
with
every event of
\mathrm{E}_{2}
. However, this approach fails to interpret programs like

(a\,\square\,b)\,;\,c
, as show in Figure
8(a)
. This failure arises
because there are two ways to reach event
a
, which come from conflicting events. According to
the definition of event structures, the conflict relation is hereditary, and an event is not in
conflict with itself. Thus, we would end up with an invalid event structure. To address this
issue, we introduce a ‘copy’ for each event of
\mathrm{E}_{2}
regarding the different ways it can be
reached. For example, in the aforementioned program, we create two copies of
a
: one indicating
it was reached by executing event
b
, and another indicating it was reached by executing event

c
, as can be seen in Figure
8(b)
.

a
b
c

(a)

Unwanted sequential composition

a
b
c_{a}
c_{b}

(b)

Good sequential composition

To capture the intended behavior in event structures, we make use of maximal configurations, as
shown in the sequential definition of event structures.

Definition 2.14

(Sequential Composition)
.

Let
\mathrm{E_{1}}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1})
and
\mathrm{E_{2}}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2})
be event
structures. Define
\mathrm{E_{1}}\,;\,\mathrm{E_{2}}=(E,\,\leq,\,\#)
as:

\displaystyle E=E_{1}\uplus(E_{2}\times\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\mathrm{E}_{1}))

\displaystyle\leq\ =\set{e_{1}\leq e^{\prime}_{1}\mid e_{1}\leq_{1}e^{\prime}_{1}}\cup\set{(e_{2},x)\leq(e^{\prime}_{2},x)\mid\ e_{2}\leq_{2}e^{\prime}_{2}}\cup\set{e_{1}\leq(e_{2},x)\mid e_{1}\in x}

\displaystyle\#\ =\set{e\#e^{\prime}\mid\exists(e_{1}\leq e,e^{\prime}_{1}\leq e^{\prime})\ .\ e_{1}\#_{1}e^{\prime}_{1}}\cup\set{(e_{2},x)\#(e^{\prime}_{2},x)\mid e_{2}\#_{2}e^{\prime}_{2}}

where
E_{2}\times\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\mathrm{E}_{1})=\set{(e,x)\mid e\in E_{2},\ x\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\mathrm{E}_{1})}
and

\uplus
denotes the disjoint union
1
1

1

The proper definition of the disjoint union is

A\uplus B=\{(0,a)|a\in A\}\cup\{(1,b)|b\in B\}
. For
R,S\in A\times B
, the
disjoint union extends to a relation as
(i,e)R\uplus S(i^{\prime},e^{\prime})
whenever
i=0=i^{\prime}
and

eRe^{\prime}
or
i=1=i^{\prime}
and
eSe^{\prime}
. For the sake of keeping the notations readable, we will keep
the
0
s and
1
s implicit.
.

Note that we multiplied
E_{2}
with the maximal configurations of
\mathrm{E_{1}}
. That is due maximal
configurations representing finished computations. In other words, the set of maximal configurations
gives all the possible ways to reach the first event of
\mathrm{E_{2}}
.

Lemma 2.15
.

Let
\mathrm{E}_{1}
and
\mathrm{E}_{2}
be event structures.
\mathrm{E_{1}}\,;\,\mathrm{E_{2}}
is an event
structure.

The absence of communication in the language considered simplifies the definition of parallel
composition in event structures, when compared to
[
18
]
, since we do not need a mechanism
of synchronization. In our case, we simply place ‘side-by-side’ the two event structures.

Definition 2.16

(Parallel Composition)
.

Let
\mathrm{E_{1}}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1})
and
\mathrm{E_{2}}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2})
be event
structures. Define
\mathrm{E_{1}}\,||\,\mathrm{E_{2}}=(E,\,\leq,\,\#)
as:

\displaystyle E=E_{1}\uplus E_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\ =\leq_{1}\uplus\leq_{2}

\displaystyle\#=\#_{1}\uplus\#_{2}

Lemma 2.17
.

Let
\mathrm{E}_{1}
and
\mathrm{E}_{2}
be event structures.
\mathrm{E_{1}}\,||\,\mathrm{E_{2}}
is an event
structure.

In Definition
2.16
we use the disjoint union to ensure that whenever we interpret the
same command in parallel,
i.e.

C\,||\,C
, we have two copies of
C
within the
event structure, as each action of
C
can occur twice.

At last we have the non-deterministic composition of event structures. Let us use the rules in
Figure
4
to give the intuition behind the definition. Consider the command

a\,\square\,b
. According to the operational semantics, if we execute
a
then we cannot
execute
b
and if we execute
b
we cannot execute
a
. If we abstract ourselves and instead of

a\,\square\,b
we consider
C_{1}\,\square\,C_{2}
, we notice that if we execute an
action from
C_{1}
, then it is no longer possible to execute any action of
C_{2}
and
vice-versa. To capture this behavior in event structures, we need to put all the events
corresponding to
C_{1}
in conflict with all the events corresponding to
C_{2}
. Formally:

Definition 2.18

(Non-deterministic Composition)
.

Let
\mathrm{E_{1}}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1})
and
\mathrm{E_{2}}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2})
be event
structures.
Define
\mathrm{E_{1}}\,\square\,\mathrm{E_{2}}=(E,\,\leq,\,\#)
as:

\displaystyle E=E_{1}\uplus E_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\ =\leq_{1}\uplus\leq_{2}

\displaystyle\#=\#_{1}\uplus\#_{2}\cup\set{e_{1}\#e_{2}\mid e_{1}\in E_{1},\,e_{2}\in E_{2}}\cup\set{e_{2}\#e_{1}\mid e_{1}\in E_{1},\,e_{2}\in E_{2}}

Equivalently, we can define the partial order in Definition
2.18
as follows:

e\leq e^{\prime}=\begin{cases}e\leq_{1}e^{\prime}&\text{if }e,e^{\prime}\in E_{1}\\
e\leq_{2}e^{\prime}&\text{if }e,e^{\prime}\in E_{2}\end{cases}

Lemma 2.19
.

Let
\mathrm{E}_{1}
and
\mathrm{E}_{2}
be event structures.
\mathrm{E_{1}}\,\square\,\mathrm{E_{2}}
is an event
structure.

We now have everything we need to interpret the language presented in Section
2.1

using event structures.

Definition 2.20
.

We interpret commands as event structures as follows:

\displaystyle\llbracket skip\rrbracket=(\set{sk},\set{sk\leq sk},\emptyset)

\displaystyle\llbracket a\rrbracket=(\set{a},\set{a\leq a},\emptyset)

\displaystyle\llbracket C_{1}\,;\,C_{2}\rrbracket=\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\,;\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket C_{1}\,\square\,C_{2}\rrbracket=\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\,\square\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket C_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\rrbracket=\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\,||\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket

When the goal is to show the equivalence between the operational and the denotational semantics,
Definition
2.39
is not suitable, since sequential composition is not
left-monotone. This happens because the inclusion on the set of events is too restrict,
i.e.
the
copies made by Definition
2.14
are distinct. Hence, we need to loose the inclusion on
the set of events, which originates
sub-similar event structures
. We must first be explicit
about the form of the events we consider. Events can either be pairs or not. If they are not pairs
we call them
plain events
. If they are pairs we call them
composite events
. We shall
denote both plain and composite events by
a,\dots,e
. Thus, examples of composite events are:

(e,x_{1})
,
((e,x_{1}),x_{2})
,
(e,1)
,
((e,x),1)
. Examples of plain events are:
e
,
e_{1}
,

e^{1}
. Composite events have an underlying plain event. To extract a plain event from a composite
event, we recursively extract the left side until it is not a pair. Formally,
\pi(a,b)=\pi(a)
,
recursively. An equivalent, explicit definition of
\pi
is as follows:

\displaystyle\pi:

\displaystyle E\rightarrow E

\displaystyle e\mapsto\begin{cases}e&\text{ if }e\text{ is a plain event}\\
\pi(e^{\prime})&\text{ if }e=(e^{\prime},x)\text{ for some }e^{\prime}\end{cases}

For example,

\pi(a)=a,\qquad\pi(a,x_{1})=a,\qquad\pi((a,x_{1}),x_{2})=a.

Returning to the notion of sub-similar event structure. Intuitively, an event structure
\mathrm{E}_{1}

is a sub-similar event structure of
\mathrm{E}_{2}
if plain events, the causal order and the conflict
relation of
\mathrm{E}_{1}
are preserved in
\mathrm{E}_{2}
. Note that the idea of ignoring the “copies”
created by sequential composition is captured by the condition on plain events. We say that two
event structures
\mathrm{E}_{1}
and
\mathrm{E}_{2}
are
similar
if each is a sub-similar event
structure of the other.

A sub-similar event structure is formally defined as follows:

Definition 2.21
.

Let
\mathrm{E_{1}}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1})
and
\mathrm{E_{2}}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2})
be event
structures. Say
\mathrm{E_{1}}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{E_{2}}
whenever exists an injective function

f:E_{1}\rightarrow E_{2}
such that
\forall e,e^{\prime}\in E_{1}
:

\displaystyle\pi(f(e))=\pi(e)

\displaystyle e\leq_{1}e^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow f(e)\leq_{2}f(e^{\prime})

\displaystyle e\#_{1}e^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow f(e)\#_{2}f(e^{\prime})

We say that two event structures
\mathrm{E}_{1},\mathrm{E}_{2}
are similar, denoted

\mathrm{E}_{1}\equiv\mathrm{E}_{2}
, iff
\mathrm{E}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{E}_{2}
and

\mathrm{E}_{2}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{E}_{1}
.

Note that in Definition
2.21
, when comparing the set of events, we ignore the
“copies” of events. This comes as a consequence of Definition
2.14
in which we make
“copies” of the same event to distinguish the different ways an event can be reached. However, the
“copies” denote the same event. Thus we want to forget the different ways they can be reached and
just focus on the event itself. Case we have not done that, sequential composition would not be
monotone,
i.e.
if
\mathrm{E_{1}}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{E^{\prime}_{1}}
and
\mathrm{E_{2}}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{E^{\prime}_{2}}
then

\mathrm{E_{1}}\,;\,\mathrm{E_{2}}\not\sqsubseteq\mathrm{E^{\prime}_{1}}\,;\,\mathrm{E^{\prime}_{2}}
. That is easily seen when the
number of maximal configurations of
\mathrm{E_{1}}
is greater than that of
\mathrm{E^{\prime}_{1}}
.

Remark 1
.

It is clear that if
\mathrm{E}_{1}=\mathrm{E}_{2}
then
\mathrm{E}_{1}\equiv\mathrm{E}_{2}
.

To finish this section of definitions, we define the set of initial events and the removal of an
initial event from a event structure.

Definition 2.22

(Set of initial events)
.

Let
\mathrm{E}=(E,\,\leq,\,\#)
be a event structure.
Define the set of initial events as follows:

\mathcal{I}(\mathrm{E})=\set{e^{\prime}\mid\not\exists e\in E\ .\ e\leq e^{\prime}\wedge e\neq e^{\prime}}

When removing an initial event from an event structure, not only the event itself but also all
conflicting events are eliminated. This decision aims to mimic, within event structures, what
happens in a transition using the small-step semantics. In small-step semantics, once an action
triggers a transition, that same action cannot be executed again. Furthermore, if the transition
occurs within a non-deterministic program, only the program associated with the triggering action
continues, while the others are discarded.

Definition 2.23

(Remove initial event)
.

Let
\mathrm{E}=(E,\,\leq,\,\#)
be a event structure and
a\in\mathcal{I}(\mathrm{E})
.
Define
\mathrm{E}\backslash a=(E^{\prime},\,\leq^{\prime},\,\#^{\prime})
as

\displaystyle E^{\prime}=\set{e\in E\mid\neg(e\#a),\,e\neq a}

\displaystyle\leq^{\prime}\,=\,\set{e\leq e^{\prime}\mid e,e^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\#^{\prime}\,=\,\set{e\#e^{\prime}\mid e,e^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}}

Lemma 2.24
.

Let
\mathrm{E}
be an event structure and
a\in\mathcal{I}(\mathrm{E})
.
\mathrm{E}\backslash a
is a event
structure.

2.3
Results

In this section we present the results obtained. For that, we interpret
\checkmark
as the empty
event structure,
i.e.

\llbracket\checkmark\rrbracket=(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset)=\emptyset
.

The following lemmas show that the sequential, parallel, and non-deterministic compositions are
monotonic w.r.t Definition
2.21
.

Lemma 2.25
.

Let
\mathrm{E_{1}},\mathrm{E}^{\prime}_{1},\mathrm{E_{2}},\mathrm{E}^{\prime}_{2}
be event structures. If

\mathrm{E_{1}}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{E^{\prime}_{1}}
and
\mathrm{E_{2}}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{E^{\prime}_{2}}
then

\mathrm{E_{1}}\,;\,\mathrm{E_{2}}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{E^{\prime}_{1}}\,;\,\mathrm{E^{\prime}_{2}}
.

Lemma 2.26
.

Let
\mathrm{E_{1}},\mathrm{E}^{\prime}_{1},\mathrm{E_{2}},\mathrm{E}^{\prime}_{2}
be event structures. If

\mathrm{E_{1}}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{E^{\prime}_{1}}
and
\mathrm{E_{2}}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{E^{\prime}_{2}}
then

\mathrm{E_{1}}\,\square\,\mathrm{E_{2}}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{E^{\prime}_{1}}\,\square\,\mathrm{E^{\prime}_{2}}
.

Lemma 2.27
.

Let
\mathrm{E_{1}},\mathrm{E}^{\prime}_{1},\mathrm{E_{2}},\mathrm{E}^{\prime}_{2}
be event structures. If

\mathrm{E_{1}}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{E^{\prime}_{1}}
and
\mathrm{E_{2}}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{E^{\prime}_{2}}
then

\mathrm{E_{1}}\,||\,\mathrm{E_{2}}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{E^{\prime}_{1}}\,||\,\mathrm{E^{\prime}_{2}}
.

Now, Lemma
2.28
tells us that whenever we have similar event structures,
i.e.

\mathrm{E}_{1}\equiv\mathrm{E}_{1}^{\prime}
and
\mathrm{E}_{2}\equiv\mathrm{E}_{2}^{\prime}
, they will still be similar when
composed of sequentially, parallelly, and non-deterministically.

Lemma 2.28
.

Let
op\in\set{;\,,\,}{,\,+}
and
\mathrm{E}_{1}
,
\mathrm{E}_{2}
,
\mathrm{E}_{1}^{\prime}
and
\mathrm{E}_{2}^{\prime}
be
event structures. If
\mathrm{E}_{1}\equiv\mathrm{E}_{1}^{\prime}
and
\mathrm{E}_{2}\equiv\mathrm{E}_{2}^{\prime}
then

\mathrm{E}_{1}\,op\,\mathrm{E}_{2}\equiv\mathrm{E}_{1}^{\prime}\,op\,\mathrm{E}_{2}^{\prime}
.

Lastly, we show how the removal of an initial event interacts with the operations associated with
sequential composition, concurrent composition, and non-deterministic composition.

Lemma 2.29
.

Let
\mathrm{E_{1}},\mathrm{E_{2}}
be event structures. Consider
\mathrm{E_{1}}\,;\,\mathrm{E_{2}}
such that

l\in\mathcal{I}(\mathrm{E_{1}}\,;\,\mathrm{E_{2}})
. Then

(\mathrm{E_{1}}\,;\,\mathrm{E_{2}})\backslash l\equiv(\mathrm{E_{1}}\backslash l)\,;\,\mathrm{E_{2}}
.

Lemma 2.30
.

Let
\mathrm{E_{1}},\mathrm{E_{2}}
be event structures. Consider
\mathrm{E_{1}}\,\square\,\mathrm{E_{2}}
such that

l\in\mathcal{I}(\mathrm{E_{1}}\,\square\,\mathrm{E_{2}})
. Then

(\mathrm{E_{1}}\,\square\,\mathrm{E_{2}})\backslash l\equiv\begin{cases}\mathrm{E_{1}}\backslash l&\text{ if }l\in\mathcal{I}(\mathrm{E_{1}})\\
\mathrm{E_{2}}\backslash l&\text{ if }l\in\mathcal{I}(\mathrm{E_{2}})\end{cases}

Lemma 2.31
.

Let
\mathrm{E_{1}},\mathrm{E_{2}}
be event structures. Consider
\mathrm{E_{1}}\,||\,\mathrm{E_{2}}
such that

l\in\mathcal{I}(\mathrm{E_{1}}\,||\,\mathrm{E_{2}})
. Then

(\mathrm{E_{1}}\,||\,\mathrm{E_{2}})\backslash l\equiv(\mathrm{E_{1}}\backslash l)\,||\,(\mathrm{E_{2}}\backslash l)
.

It is straightforward to check that:

Lemma 2.32
.

Let
\mathrm{E}_{1},\mathrm{E}_{2}
be event structures. Then

\mathrm{E}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{E}_{2}=\mathrm{E}_{2}\,||\,\mathrm{E}_{1}
.

We now show how the operational and denotational semantics are related via a soundness and adequacy
theorem. Recall that
\llbracket\checkmark\rrbracket=(\emptyset,\,\emptyset,\,\emptyset)
.

The intuition behind Lemma
2.33
is that if the action
l
triggers a transition from

C
to
C^{\prime}
, then removing
l
from
\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\gamma}
is similar to have

\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\gamma}
. This can be further clarified if we recall the intuition given when
defining the removal of an initial event from an event structure (Definition
2.23
).

Lemma 2.33

(Soundness I)
.

If
C\xrightarrow{l}C^{\prime}
then
\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket\equiv\llbracket C\rrbracket\backslash l
.

With a result that establishes a relation between the small-step and denotational semantics, we now
focus on the big-step semantics. Recall that the big-step semantics creates words, which are
sequences of labels. On the other hand, event structures have the notion of covering chains, which
are finite sequences representing the execution of events. We can exploit this similarity to
formulate an equivalence between the big-step and denotational semantics.

Theorem 2.34

(Soundness II)
.

If
C\xrightarrow{\omega}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow C^{\prime}
then
\exists x\in\mathcal{C}(\llbracket C\rrbracket)

such that
\emptyset\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\omega}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}\ }}x
.

Since adequacy is the reverse of soundness, following a similar procedure to prove it makes
sense. Hence, we first need to establish a relation between denotational and small-step
semantics. To do this, we take advantage once again of the intuition behind removing an initial
event from an event structure.

Lemma 2.35

(Adequacy I)
.

Let
l\in\mathcal{I}(\llbracket C\rrbracket)
. Then
\exists C^{\prime}\in(C\cup\{\checkmark\})
s.t

C\xrightarrow{l}C^{\prime}
and
\llbracket C\rrbracket\backslash l\equiv\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket
.

With Lemma
2.35
, we can turn our efforts to relate the denotational semantics and the
big-step semantics. To formulate the adequacy theorem, we reverse the reasoning used for
Theorem
2.34
. However, we need to be careful when selecting the
configurations. Specifically, we avoid the empty configuration because the big-step semantics does
not allow empty words. The adequacy theorem is then formulated as follows:

Theorem 2.36

(Adequacy II)
.

If
\emptyset\neq x\in\mathcal{C}(\llbracket C\rrbracket)
s.t.

\emptyset\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\omega}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}\ }}x
then
\exists C^{\prime}
s.t.

C\xrightarrow{\omega}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow C^{\prime}
.

Theorem
2.34
states that every word
\omega
derived from
the n-step semantics corresponds to a covering chain, and consequently
to a configuration. Conversely, Theorem
2.36
indicates that if
we have a non-empty covering chain
\omega
, then there exists a
command
C^{\prime}
reachable from
C
by executing
\omega
.

2.4
Introducing cyclic behavior

We now introduce cyclic behavior to the language in Section
2.1
. In order to avoid
the introduction of the notion of state in the language, the cyclic behavior will be given by
recursion. In that way, we do not need to associate the notion of state to a command in the
operational semantics. We can just keep recording the actions that are being made by the program.

Another thing to have in mind is that with cyclic behavior we open the door to infinite
computations. However, covering chains are only defined in finite sequence of words and infinite
configurations are odd, because we would need to define precisely what it means to be an infinite
configuration. Hence, the words that we formed with the n-step will be always finite, despite the
possibility of them being infinite. We can justify this by saying that we are only concerned on the
‘interesting words’,
i.e.
those who are finite.

To introduce recursion we need to add some restrictions when forming programs, since we do not want
to allow commands like:
\mu X.X\,;\,a
and
\mu X.a\,;\,X\,;\,b
.

Let
X\subseteq Var
, with
Var
a set of variables. The syntax is now given by:

C::=skip\mid a\in Act\mid C\,;\,C\mid C\,\square\,C\mid C\,||\,C\mid\mu X.C\mid X

where
skip
is a command that does nothing;
a
is an atomic action from a
pre-determined set of atomic actions, denoted as
Act
;
C\,;\,C
is the usual
sequential composition of programs;
C\,||\,C
is the parallel composition of
commands;
C\,\square\,C
represents the non-deterministic choice;
\mu X.C
is the
recursive command; and
X\in Var
with
Var
a set of variables. Furthermore, we only consider
closed commands,
i.e.
commands in which every variable
X
is bound by a recursion
\mu X
and in
sequential composition we only allow recursion to occur at right.

We define the set of free-variables and bound-variables as follows:

FV(skip)=\emptyset

BV(skip)=\emptyset

FV(a)=\emptyset

BV(a)=\emptyset

FV(C_{1}\,;\,C_{2})=FV(C_{1})\cup FV(C_{2})

BV(C_{1}\,;\,C_{2})=BV(C_{1})\cup BV(C_{2})

FV(C_{1}\,||\,C_{2})=FV(C_{1})\cup FV(C_{2})

BV(C_{1}\,||\,C_{2})=BV(C_{1})\cup BV(C_{2})

FV(C_{1}\,\square\,C_{2})=FV(C_{1})\cup FV(C_{2})

BV(C_{1}\,\square\,C_{2})=BV(C_{1})\cup BV(C_{2})

FV(X)=\set{X}

BV(X)=\emptyset

FV(\mu X.C)=FV(C)\backslash\set{X}

BV(\mu X.C)=\set{X}\cup BV(C)

We restrict the sequential composition to those whose free-variables and bound-variables on the left
are empty,
i.e.

C_{1}\,;\,C_{2}
if
FV(C_{1})=\emptyset=BV(C_{1})
.
With this restriction we forbid program like
\mu X.X\,;\,a
,

\mu X.a\,;\,X\,;\,b
(with the condition
FV(C_{1})=\emptyset
) and

(\mu X.a\,;\,X)\,;\,b
(with the condition
BV(C_{1})=\emptyset
).
We want to forbid these kind of programs in sequential composition, because if
C_{1}
never
terminates then the sequential composition never terminates. This is also a restriction that comes
from the fact that covering chains are only defined in finite sequences and that infinite
configurations are odd in event structures. Note however that we allow programs like

\mu X.X\,||\,a
and
\mu X.X\,\square\,a
, since they do not block the computation.

Before adding a rule for the recursion command to Figure
4
, we need to define what
it means to substitute a variable
X
by a command
C^{\prime}
. Inspired by
[
7
]
, we
define substitution as follows:

Definition 2.37
.

Let
X\in Var
and
C,C^{\prime}
be commands. Define
C[X\leftarrow C^{\prime}]
,
where we substitute every free occurrence of
X
in
C
by
C^{\prime}
(while changing bound
variables to avoid clashes) by induction on
C
as follows:

\displaystyle skip[X\leftarrow C^{\prime}]=skip

\displaystyle a[X\leftarrow C^{\prime}]=a

\displaystyle(C_{1}\,;\,C_{2})[X\leftarrow C^{\prime}]=C_{1}\,;\,(C_{2}[X\leftarrow C^{\prime}])

\displaystyle(C_{1}\,||\,C_{2})[X\leftarrow C^{\prime}]=C_{1}[X\leftarrow C^{\prime}]\,||\,C_{2}[X\leftarrow C^{\prime}]

\displaystyle(C_{1}\,\square\,C_{2})[X\leftarrow C^{\prime}]=C_{1}[X\leftarrow C^{\prime}]\,\square\,C_{2}[X\leftarrow C^{\prime}]

\displaystyle X[X\leftarrow C]=C

\displaystyle(\mu X.C)[X\leftarrow C^{\prime}]=\mu X.C

\displaystyle(\mu Y.C)[X\leftarrow C^{\prime}]=\mu Y.C[X\leftarrow C^{\prime}]\text{ if }X\neq Y\text{ and }Y\not\in FV(C^{\prime})

We then add to Figure
4
the following rule for the recursion command, inspired
by
[
8
, Fig. 1]
:

\mu X.C\xlongrightarrow{l}C^{\prime}[X\leftarrow\mu X.C]C\xlongrightarrow{l}C^{\prime}

This rule tells us that if an action
l
triggers a transition from
C
to
C^{\prime}
, then
from
\mu X.C
we transit with the same label to
C^{\prime}[X\leftarrow\mu X.C]
,
where we substitute in
C^{\prime}
all the occurrences of
X
by
\mu X.C
.

Example 2.38
.

Figure
9
illustrates the behavior of a non-deterministic toss coin, which
produces a possibly empty sequence of
a
’s that finishes with
sk
. To understand this we observe
that the initial program has two possible transitions: (1) we execute
sk
that terminates the
computation; (2) we execute
a
, and we transit to a command equal to the initial one in which we
have two possible transitions again.

\mu X.(skip\,\square\,a\,;\,X)
\checkmark
\mu X.(skip\,\square\,a\,;\,X)
\checkmark
\mu X.(skip\,\square\,a\,;\,X)
\checkmark
\mu X.(skip\,\square\,a\,;\,X)
\checkmark
\ddots
sk
a
sk
a
sk
a
sk
a

Figure 9
:
Unrolling the execution of
\mu X.(skip\,\square\,a\,;\,X)

On the event structure side, we want to use the Knaster-Tarski Theorem to build the least-fix point.
To define it, we will use an order that does not ignore copies, differently from what happens with
Definition
2.21
.

Definition 2.39
.

Let
\mathrm{E_{1}}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1})
and
\mathrm{E_{2}}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2})
be event
structures. Say
\mathrm{E_{1}}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{E_{2}}
if:

\displaystyle E_{1}\subseteq E_{2}

\displaystyle\forall e,e^{\prime}\ .\ e\leq_{1}e^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow e,e^{\prime}\in E_{1}\wedge e\leq_{2}e^{\prime}

\displaystyle\forall e,e^{\prime}\ .\ e\#_{1}e^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow e,e^{\prime}\in E_{1}\wedge e\#_{2}e^{\prime}

We now start to check the conditions of the Knaster-Tarski theorem. We first verify that
Definition
2.39
is a partial order with a least element.

Lemma 2.40
.

\trianglelefteq
is a partial order.

Lemma 2.41
.

Define
\bot=(\emptyset,\,\emptyset,\,\emptyset)
.
\bot
is the least element of

\trianglelefteq
.

We then define what it means to be a least upper bound in terms of event structures, demonstrate
that it is an event structure, and prove that it is indeed a least upper bound of an
\omega
-chain
of event structures.

Definition 2.42
.

Let
\mathrm{E}_{1}\trianglelefteq\dots\trianglelefteq\mathrm{E}_{n}\trianglelefteq\dots
be a

\omega
-chain. Let
\mathrm{E}^{\omega}=(E^{\omega},\,\leq^{\omega},\,\#^{\omega})
be its least upper
bound where:

•

E^{\omega}=\cup_{n\in\omega}E_{n}

•

\leq^{\omega}=\cup_{n\in\omega}\leq_{n}

•

\#^{\omega}=\cup_{n\in\omega}\#_{n}

Lemma 2.43
.

\mathrm{E}^{\omega}
is an event structure.

Lemma 2.44
.

Let
\mathrm{E}_{1}\trianglelefteq\dots\trianglelefteq\mathrm{E}_{n}\trianglelefteq\dots
be a

\omega
-chain. Then
\mathrm{E}^{\omega}
is its least upper bound.

Now we define what it means for an operator in event structures to be monotone and continuous with
respect to Definition
2.39
. Intuitively, an operator is monotone if it
preserves the ordering. For example, for the parallel composition we say that it is monotone if

\mathrm{E}_{1}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{E}^{\prime}_{1}
and
\mathrm{E}_{2}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{E}^{\prime}_{2}
then

\mathrm{E}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{E}_{2}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{E}^{\prime}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{E}^{\prime}_{2}
. An operator is continuous
if the action of the operator on the least upper bounds of event structures is the same as the least
upper bound of the action of the operator on the event structures. For example, for the parallel
composition we say that it is continuous if

\bigsqcup_{n}\mathrm{E}_{n}\,||\,\bigsqcup_{m}\mathrm{E}_{m}=\bigsqcup_{n,m}(\mathrm{E}_{n}\,||\,\mathrm{E}_{m})
. Formally
[
16
, Definition 2.8]
:

Definition 2.45
.

Let
op
be an
n
-ary operation on the class of event structures
\mathbb{E}
. Say
op
is

monotonic
iff when for event structures we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}_{1}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{E}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{E}_{n}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{E}^{\prime}_{n}\text{ then }op(\mathrm{E}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{E}_{n})\trianglelefteq op(\mathrm{E}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{E}^{\prime}_{n})

Say
op
is continuous iff for all countable chains

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}_{11}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{E}_{12}\trianglelefteq\dots\trianglelefteq\mathrm{E}_{1i}\trianglelefteq\dots

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}_{n1}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{E}_{n2}\trianglelefteq\dots\trianglelefteq\mathrm{E}_{ni}\trianglelefteq\dots

we have

\displaystyle op\left(\bigsqcup_{i}\mathrm{E}_{1i},\dots,\bigsqcup_{i}\mathrm{E}_{ni}\right)=\bigsqcup_{i}op(\mathrm{E}_{1i},\dots,\mathrm{E}_{ni})

where
\bigsqcup
denotes the least upper bound w.r.t
\trianglelefteq
.

To the previous definition, we add that an operation is continuous iff it is continuous in each
argument separately
[
16
]
.

We now show that the operators of the language are monotone with respect to
Definition
2.39
. Recall that the sequential composition is only right monotone
because of the restriction imposed in the syntax, which requires the free-variables and
bounded-variables of the left command to be empty.

Lemma 2.46
.

Let
\mathrm{E},\mathrm{E}_{1},\mathrm{E}_{2}
be event structures. If
\mathrm{E}_{1}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{E}_{2}
then

\mathrm{E}\,;\,\mathrm{E}_{1}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{E}\,;\,\mathrm{E}_{2}
.

Lemma 2.47
.

Let
\mathrm{E_{1}},\mathrm{E}^{\prime}_{1},\mathrm{E_{2}},\mathrm{E}^{\prime}_{2}
be event structures. If

\mathrm{E_{1}}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{E^{\prime}_{1}}
and
\mathrm{E_{2}}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{E^{\prime}_{2}}
then

\mathrm{E_{1}}\,||\,\mathrm{E_{2}}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{E^{\prime}_{1}}\,||\,\mathrm{E^{\prime}_{2}}
.

Lemma 2.48
.

Let
\mathrm{E_{1}},\mathrm{E}^{\prime}_{1},\mathrm{E_{2}},\mathrm{E}^{\prime}_{2}
be event structures. If

\mathrm{E_{1}}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{E^{\prime}_{1}}
and
\mathrm{E_{2}}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{E^{\prime}_{2}}
then

\mathrm{E_{1}}\,\square\,\mathrm{E_{2}}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{E^{\prime}_{1}}\,\square\,\mathrm{E^{\prime}_{2}}
.

The following lemma from
[
16
, Lemma 2.9]
is helpful to show that the sequential, the
concurrent, and the non-deterministic operators are continuous. Intuitively, a function is
continuous on event structures if it is monotonic and act continuously on the component sets of
events ordered by inclusion.

Lemma 2.49
.

Let
op
be a unary operation on the class of event structures
\mathbb{E}
. Then
op
is
continuous iff

1.

op
is monotonic

2.

if
\mathrm{E}_{1}\trianglelefteq\dots\trianglelefteq\mathrm{E}_{n}\trianglelefteq\dots
is an

\omega
-chain then each event of
op(\bigsqcup_{n}\mathrm{E}_{n})
is an event of

\bigsqcup_{n}op(\mathrm{E}_{n})
.

We proceed to show that the operators are continuous. Note that since the sequential composition is
only right-monotone, then it will only be right-continuous.

Lemma 2.50
.

\bigsqcup_{m}(\mathrm{E}\,;\,\mathrm{E}_{m})=\mathrm{E}\,;\,\bigsqcup_{m}\mathrm{E}_{m}
.

Lemma 2.51
.

\bigsqcup_{n,m}(\mathrm{E}_{n}\,||\,\mathrm{E}_{m})=\bigsqcup_{n}\mathrm{E}_{n}\,||\,\bigsqcup_{m}\mathrm{E}_{m}
.

Lemma 2.52
.

\bigsqcup_{n,m}(\mathrm{E}_{n}\,\square\,\mathrm{E}_{m})=\bigsqcup_{n}\mathrm{E}_{n}\,\square\,\bigsqcup_{m}\mathrm{E}_{m}
.

Lemma
2.53
is a version of the Kleene fixed-point theorem for the case of event
structures. Intuitively, it tells us how to build a least fixed-point for a continuous operator.

Lemma 2.53
.

Let
\Gamma
be a continuous operation on the class of event structures
\mathbb{E}
. Let

\bot=(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset)\in\mathbb{E}
. Define
fix(\Gamma)
to be the least
upper bound of the chain

\bot\trianglelefteq\Gamma(\bot)\trianglelefteq\dots\trianglelefteq\Gamma^{n}(\bot)\trianglelefteq\dots
. Then
\Gamma(fix(\Gamma))=fix(\Gamma)
.

We now update Definition
2.20
to accommodate recursion.

Definition 2.54
.

Define an environment to be a function
\gamma:Var\rightarrow\mathbb{E}
from variables to
event structures. For a command
C
and an environment
\gamma
define

\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\gamma}

2
2

2

whenever a command is not recursive, for simplicity we may drop
the environment
\gamma
from
\llbracket-\rrbracket_{\gamma}
as follows:

\displaystyle\llbracket skip\rrbracket_{\gamma}=(\set{sk},\set{sk\leq sk},\emptyset)

\displaystyle\llbracket a\rrbracket_{\gamma}=(\set{a},\set{a\leq a},\emptyset)

\displaystyle\llbracket C_{1}\,;\,C_{2}\rrbracket_{\gamma}=\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{\gamma}\,;\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{\gamma}

\displaystyle\llbracket C_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\rrbracket_{\gamma}=\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{\gamma}\,||\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{\gamma}

\displaystyle\llbracket C_{1}\,\square\,C_{2}\rrbracket_{\gamma}=\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{\gamma}\,\square\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{\gamma}

\displaystyle\llbracket X\rrbracket_{\gamma}=\gamma(X)

\displaystyle\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma}=fix(\Gamma^{C,\gamma})

where
\Gamma^{C,\gamma}:\mathbb{E}\rightarrow\mathbb{E}
is given by

\Gamma^{C,\gamma}(\mathrm{E})=\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\gamma(X\leftarrow\mathrm{E})}
.

Since
\Gamma^{C,\gamma}
is a new operator, we need to show that it is continuous. To do
this, it is useful to know that
fix
is continuous
[
1
]
.

Lemma 2.55
.

\Gamma^{C,\gamma}
is continuous.

With the introduction of the recursive command, we need the two following lemmas for
Lemma
2.33
.

Lemma 2.56

(Substitution lemma)
.

\llbracket C^{\prime}[X\leftarrow\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma}]\rrbracket_{\gamma}=\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\gamma{(X\leftarrow\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma}})}

Lemma 2.57
.

\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma}=\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\gamma(X\leftarrow\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma})}

We present an example where we illustrate a fragment of the event structure that is obtained by
interpreting a recursive command.

.

To show the equivalence between the operational and the denotational semantics, we reuse what was
done in Section
2.3
. The only lemmas in which we need to add the proof for the
recursion case are the following:

Lemma 2.59

(Soundness I)
.

If
C\xrightarrow{l}C^{\prime}
then

\forall\gamma,\ \llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\gamma}\equiv\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\gamma}\backslash l
.

Lemma 2.60

(Adequacy I)
.

Let
l\in\mathcal{I}(\llbracket C\rrbracket)
. Then
\exists C^{\prime}\in(C\cup\{\checkmark\})
s.t

C\xrightarrow{l}C^{\prime}
and
\llbracket C\rrbracket\backslash l\equiv\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket
.

For Theorem
2.34
and Theorem
2.36
we only need to adapt
\llbracket-\rrbracket
to

\llbracket-\rrbracket_{\gamma}
.

We now delve into examples that show soundness and adequacy in practice.

Example 2.61

The event structure in Example

.

To see how the semantics relate, recall the configurations in Example

Let us select the words

\emptyset\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ \set{d}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ \set{d,c}
and

, respectively. Both covering chains

\set{d,c}

. It is straightforward to see that each

and

.

’s that finishes with

\mu X.(skip\,\square\,a\,;\,X)\xrightarrow{\omega}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow\checkmark
. From

x=\{a_{0},a_{1},sk_{2}\}\in\mathcal{C}(\llbracket\mu X.(skip\,\square\,a\,;\,X)\rrbracket_{\gamma})

such that
\emptyset\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\omega}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ \ x

\mu X.(skip\,\square\,a\,;\,X)\xrightarrow{\omega}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow\checkmark

\omega=a^{n}\ sk
, with

being a

\mu X.(skip\,\square\,a\,;\,X)\xrightarrow{\omega}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow\checkmark

with

.

3
Probabilistic Event Structures

This section describes how to endow event structures with a probabilistic behavior. Probabilistic
event structures
[
19
]
are event structures together with a function on configurations,

v:\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{E})\rightarrow[0,1]
, which assigns
1
for the empty set, jointly with a

monotonicity
condition. The intuition behind
v(x)
is the probability of reaching at least
configuration
x
.

The monotonicity condition in
[
19
, Definition 1]
is described in terms of
drop
functions
, which will be necessary when showing that unitary event structures with an initial
state corresponds to probabilistic event structures. Define drop functions

d^{(n)}_{v}[y\,;\,x_{1},\dots x_{n}]\in\mathbb{R}
for
y,x_{1},\dots x_{n}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{E})
with

y\subseteq x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
, by induction, taking

\displaystyle d^{(0)}_{v}[y\,;\,]=v(y)

\displaystyle d^{(n)}_{v}[y\,;\,x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]=d^{(n-1)}_{v}[y\,;\,x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1}]-d^{(n-1)}_{v}[x_{n}\,;\,x_{1}\cup x_{n},\dots,x_{n-1}\cup x_{n}]

for
n>0
. Intuitively, a drop function
d^{(n)}_{v}[y\,;\,x_{1},\dots x_{n}]
indicates the probability
of reaching at least configuration
y
without reaching any of the
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
, with

y\subseteq x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
.

Accordingly to
[
19
, Proposition 1]
, the drop function can be described based on the
inclusion-exclusion principle for sets
3
3

3

The inclusion-exclusion principle for sets is a
method to count the number of elements in the union of possibly overlapping finite sets

(Equation
1
). We make use of such result to present the definition of
probabilistic event structures.

Definition 3.1

(Probabilistic event structure)
.

Let
\mathrm{E}=(E,\,\leq,\,\#)
be an event structure. A configuration-valuation on
\mathrm{E}
is a
function
v:\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{E})\rightarrow[0,1]
such that
v(\emptyset)=1
and

\forall y,x_{1},\dots x_{n}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{E})
such that
y\subseteq x_{1},\dots,x_{n}

\displaystyle v(y)-\sum_{\emptyset\neq I\subseteq\set{1,\dots,n}}(-1)^{|I|+1}v\left(\bigcup_{i\in I}x_{i}\right)\geq 0

(1)

where
v(x)=0
whenever
x\not\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{E})
.

A probabilistic event structure,
\mathrm{P}=(\mathrm{E},\,v)
, comprises an event structure

\mathrm{E}=(E,\,\leq,\,\#)
together with a configuration-valuation

v:\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{E})\rightarrow[0,1]
.

From Equation
1
, there are some conclusions that we can
make
[
6
]
:

•

The configuration-valuation is decreasing:

x\subseteq y\implies v(x)\geq v(y)

•

Events in conflict have a conditional probability whose sum is less than or equal to one:

\forall 1\leq i\leq n,\ x\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e_{i}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ x_{i}\text{ and }\forall 1\leq i<j\leq n,\ e_{i}\#e_{j}\implies\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{v(x\cup\set{e_{i}})}{v(x)}\leq 1

The first conclusion taken from Equation
1
is trivial. Intuitively it says
that as further we advance in the computation, the probabilities either stay the same or decrease.
The second conclusion can be illustrated by considering two conflicting events
e
and
e^{\prime}
. Now
consider
x\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ \ x\cup\{e\}
and
x\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e^{\prime}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ \ x\cup\{e^{\prime}\}
. Clearly we have

x\subseteq x\cup\{e\},x\cup\{e^{\prime}\}
. From Equation
1
we then derive
the following:

\displaystyle v(x)-v(x\cup\{e\})-v(x\cup\{e^{\prime}\})\geq 0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow v(x)\geq v(x\cup\{e\})+v(x\cup\{e^{\prime}\})

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow 1\geq\dfrac{v(x\cup\{e\})+v(x\cup\{e^{\prime}\})}{v(x)}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\dfrac{v(x\cup\{e\})+v(x\cup\{e^{\prime}\})}{v(x)}\leq 1

This can be interpreted as a conditional probability: given that configuration
x
has occurred, the
probability of subsequently executing either
e
or
e^{\prime}
cannot exceed
1
.

Example
3.2
illustrates probabilistic event structures.

Example 3.2
.

Figure
12
shows a probabilistic event structure very similar to the event structure
in Figure
1
, the only difference being the addition of a new event
\tau
, for which
the events
a
,
c
, and
d
are causally dependent. The event
\tau
is used to indicate that the
events that are causally immediate to it,
i.e.

\tau\rightarrowtriangle a
,

\tau\rightarrowtriangle c
, and
\tau\rightarrowtriangle d
arose from a probabilistic
choice. Consequently, their probabilities are not
1
, as can be seen by the
configuration-valuation. Furthermore, in the drawing of the event structure, we subscript the
probability of each event to make it easier to understand where probabilities came from.

The set of configurations is composed of

\set{\emptyset,\set{\tau},\set{\tau,a},\set{\tau,c}\set{\tau,d},\set{\tau,a,b},\set{\tau,c,d}}
, where
\set{\tau,a,b}
and
\set{\tau,c,d}
are maximal configurations
with probability
p
and
1-p
, respectively.

a_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}
b_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1}}
c_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(1-p)}}
d_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(1-p)}}
\tau_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1}}

v(x)=\begin{cases}p&\text{ if }a\in x\\
1-p&\text{ if }c\in x\text{ or }d\in x\\
1&\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

Figure 12
:
Example of a probabilistic event structure

3.1
Language

The set of commands allowed by the language are given by the following grammar (where
p\in]0,1[
):

C::=skip\mid a\in Act\mid C\,;\,C\mid C\,+_{p}\,C\mid C\,||\,C

In the design of this language we made two choices: the first was to substitute the
non-deterministic operator by the probabilistic operator and the second concerns the intervals for
which
p
ranges. The justification for the former is related with the chosen probabilistic event
structure. In sum, Winskel probabilistic event structures are not suitable to model a language that
posses both non-deterministic and probabilistic operators, as explained in
[
6
]
.
Regarding the latter, the intervals chosen are influenced by Definition
3.13
, since
it is no reasonable to remove an initial event when its probability is zero.

We extend the set of labels with a new label
\tau
,
i.e.

L^{\prime}=L\cup\set{\tau}
and let it be
ranged by
l^{\prime}
. Similarly to process algebra,
\tau
will be used to denote an invisible
transition.

We fix

\mathrm{D}(X)=\set{\psi:X\rightarrow[0,1]\mid sup(X)\text{ finite},\sum_{x\in X}\psi(x)=1}

as being the probabilistic finite support functor and we We define the small-step transition step
(labeled Segala automaton),

\rightarrow\subseteq C\times\mathrm{D}(L^{\prime}\times(C\cup\{\checkmark\}))
, as the
smallest relation obeying the following rules:

\displaystyle skip\rightarrow 1\cdot(sk,\checkmark)\hskip 28.45274pta\rightarrow 1\cdot(a,\checkmark)\hskip 28.45274ptC_{1}\,+_{p}\,C_{2}\rightarrow p\cdot(\tau,C_{1})+(1-p)\cdot(\tau,C_{2})

\displaystyle C_{1}\,;\,C_{2}\rightarrow 1\cdot(l,C_{2})C_{1}\rightarrow 1\cdot(l,\checkmark)\hskip 28.45274ptC_{1}\,;\,C_{2}\rightarrow 1\cdot(l,C^{\prime}_{1}\,;\,C_{2})C_{1}\rightarrow 1\cdot(l,C^{\prime}_{1})\hskip 28.45274ptC_{1}\,;\,C_{2}\rightarrow\sum_{i}p_{i}\cdot(\tau,C_{i}\,;\,C_{2})C_{1}\rightarrow\sum_{i}p_{i}\cdot(\tau,C_{i})

\displaystyle C_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\rightarrow 1\cdot(l,C_{2})C_{1}\rightarrow 1\cdot(l,\checkmark)\hskip 28.45274ptC_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\rightarrow 1\cdot(l,C^{\prime}_{1}\,||\,C_{2})C_{1}\rightarrow 1\cdot(l,C^{\prime}_{1})\hskip 28.45274ptC_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\rightarrow\sum_{i}p_{i}\cdot(\tau,C_{i}\,||\,C_{2})C_{1}\rightarrow\sum_{i}p_{i}\cdot(\tau,C_{i})

\displaystyle C_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\rightarrow 1\cdot(l,C_{1})C_{2}\rightarrow 1\cdot(l,\checkmark)\hskip 28.45274ptC_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\rightarrow 1\cdot(l,C_{1}\,||\,C^{\prime}_{2})C_{2}\rightarrow 1\cdot(l,C^{\prime}_{2})\hskip 28.45274ptC_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\rightarrow\sum_{j}p_{j}\cdot(\tau,C_{1}\,||\,C_{j})C_{2}\rightarrow\sum_{j}p_{j}\cdot(\tau,C_{j})

Figure 13
:
Rules of the probabilistic small-step operational semantics

Define a word to be a sequence of labels:

\omega::=l^{\prime}\mid l^{\prime}:\omega

where
l^{\prime}:\omega
appends
l^{\prime}
to the beginning of
\omega
. A word can also be seen as an
element of
(L^{\prime})^{+}
,
i.e.
a possibly infinite sequence of labels without the empty sequence. Despite

(L^{\prime})^{+}
allows the possibility of having infinite words, by now we focus only on the finite words.

Define the
n
-step transition,

\xrightarrow{\omega_{p}}\subseteq C\times\mathrm{D}((L^{\prime})^{+}\times(C\cup\{\checkmark\}))
,
where
n
is the length of the words, as follows:

\displaystyle C\twoheadrightarrow\sum_{i}p_{i}(l^{\prime},C_{i})C\rightarrow\sum_{i}p_{i}(l^{\prime},C_{i})\hskip 56.9055ptC\twoheadrightarrow\sum_{i}p_{i}\left(\sum_{j}p_{j}\cdot(l^{\prime}:\omega_{ij},C_{ij})\right)C\rightarrow\sum_{i}p_{i}(l^{\prime},C_{i})\qquad\forall i\,C_{i}\twoheadrightarrow\sum_{j}p_{j}\cdot(\omega_{ij},C_{ij})

Figure 14
:
Rules of the n-step operational semantics

The left rule represents the execution of a single step in a computation, while the right rule
represents multiple steps of the computation. The latter rule can be understood as follows: if

C
transits to
\sum_{i}p_{i}\cdot(l^{\prime},C_{i})
and for each
C_{i}
we transit to

\sum_{j}p_{j}\cdot(\omega_{ij},C_{ij})
, then by appending
l^{\prime}
to each
\omega_{ij}
, we can
transit from
C
to

\sum_{i}p_{i}\left(\sum_{j}p_{j}\cdot(l^{\prime}:\omega_{ij},C_{ij})\right)
. In this transition,
for each
i
, we multiply the probabilities obtained from the small-step transition with the
probabilities obtained from the n-step transition.

Example 3.3
.

we use straight arrows to denote a transition from a command to a

\bullet
, labeled by the triggering action and wiggly arrows to
represent a transition from a distribution to a command labeled by the associated probability.

From
(a\,;\,b)\,+_{p}\,(c\,||\,d)
we transit with
\tau
to

, which
transits with probability

to
a\,;\,b
and with probability
1-p
to

c\,||\,d
. For the former, by executing first
a
and then

we reach the end of
the computation. For the latter, since it is a concurrent program, to finish the computation we
can either execute first
c
and then

or we can execute first
d
and then
c
.

, we can

with the words

and

.

3.2
Constructions on Probabilistic Event Structures

The constructions on probabilistic event structures are an extension of the ones defined previously.
Hence, the explanation of the sequential and parallel composition will be focused on the valuation
and we detail more the probabilistic choice.

Let
\mathrm{P}_{1}
and
\mathrm{P}_{2}
be two probabilistic event structures. For the valuation of the
sequential composition we note the following: either the configuration belongs to

\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{P}_{1})
and in that case the valuation of the sequential composition equals the
valuation of
\mathrm{P}_{1}
, or the configuration has elements of both probabilistic event
structures. In that case, we multiply valuation of a maximal configuration in
\mathrm{P}_{1}
with the
valuation of a configuration in
\mathrm{P}_{2}
whose events are reached by the maximal configuration of

\mathrm{P}_{1}
.

Definition 3.5

(Sequential Composition)
.

Let
\mathrm{P_{1}}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1},\,v_{1})
and
\mathrm{P_{2}}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2},\,v_{2})
be
probabilistic event structures. Define
\mathrm{P_{1}}\,;\,\mathrm{P_{2}}=(E,\,\leq,\,\#,\,v)
as:

\displaystyle E=E_{1}\uplus(E_{2}\times\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\mathrm{P}_{1}))

\displaystyle\leq\ =\set{e_{1}\leq e^{\prime}_{1}\mid e\leq_{1}e^{\prime}}\cup\set{(e_{2},x)\leq(e^{\prime}_{2},x)\mid\ e_{2}\leq_{2}e^{\prime}_{2}}\cup\set{e_{1}\leq(e_{2},x)\mid e_{1}\in x}

\displaystyle\#\ =\set{e\#e^{\prime}\mid\exists(e_{1}\leq e,e^{\prime}_{1}\leq e^{\prime})\ .\ e_{1}\#_{1}e^{\prime}_{1}}\cup\set{(e_{2},x)\#(e^{\prime}_{2},x)\mid e_{2}\#_{2}e^{\prime}_{2}}

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{P}_{1}\,;\,\mathrm{P}_{2})\,.\,v(x)=\begin{cases}v_{1}(x)&\text{ if }x\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{P}_{1})\\
v_{1}(x_{1})\cdot v_{2}(x_{2})&\text{ if }x=x_{1}\cup(x_{2}\times\{x_{1}\})\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt\text{ with }x_{1}\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\mathrm{P}_{1}),\,x_{2}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{P}_{2})\end{cases}

where
E_{2}\times\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\mathrm{P}_{1})=\set{(e,x)\mid e\in E_{2},\ x\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\mathrm{P}_{1})}
and

x_{2}\times\{x_{1}\}=\{(e_{2},x_{1})\mid e_{2}\in x_{2}\}
.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
\mathrm{P_{1}}
and
\mathrm{P_{2}}
be probabilistic event structures.
\mathrm{P}_{1}\,;\,\mathrm{P}_{2}
is a
probabilistic event structure.

We now turn our attention to the probabilistic choice. As it can be deduced, the probabilistic
choice of two probabilistic event structures is going to be very similar to the non-deterministic
choice of event structures in Definition
2.18
. Indeed when composing two probabilistic
events structures
\mathrm{P}_{1}
and
\mathrm{P}_{2}
under the probabilistic operator, the events of

\mathrm{P}_{1}
will all be in conflict with the events of
\mathrm{P}_{2}
(recall that the meaning of

C_{1}\,+_{p}\,C_{2}
is to execute
C_{1}
with probability
p
or execute

C_{2}
with probability
1-p
). Furthermore, each event of
\mathrm{P}_{1}
and
\mathrm{P}_{2}
will
causally depend on an event representing the invisible action
\tau
. In other words, the invisible
action
\tau
should be the initial event. Regarding the valuations, if the configuration obtained
by removing
\tau
belongs to
\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{P}_{1})
, then we multiply by
p
the valuation in

\mathrm{P}_{1}
. If the configuration obtained by removing
\tau
belongs to
\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{P}_{2})
, then we
multiply by
(1-p)
. Otherwise, the value of the valuation is
1
. Formally:

Definition 3.7

(Probabilistic choice)
.

Let
\mathrm{P}_{1}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1},\,v_{1})
and
\mathrm{P}_{2}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2},\,v_{2})
be
probabilistic event structures. Define
\mathrm{P}_{1}\,+_{p}\,\mathrm{P}_{2}=(E,\,\leq,\,\#,\,v)

as:

\displaystyle E=\set{\tau}\uplus(E_{1}\uplus E_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\ =\set{\tau\leq e\mid e\in E}\cup\leq_{1}\uplus\leq_{2}

\displaystyle\#=\#_{1}\uplus\#_{2}\cup\set{e_{1}\#e_{2}\mid e_{1}\in E_{1},\,e_{2}\in E_{2}}\cup\set{e_{2}\#e_{1}\mid e_{1}\in E_{1},\,e_{2}\in E_{2}}

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{P}_{1}\,+_{p}\,\mathrm{P}_{2})\,.\,v(x)=\begin{cases}p\cdot v_{1}(x\backslash\tau)&\text{ if }x\backslash\tau\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{P}_{1})\\
(1-p)\cdot v_{2}(x\backslash\tau)&\text{ if }x\backslash\tau\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{P}_{2})\\
1&\text{ if }x=\set{\tau}\vee x=\emptyset\end{cases}

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
\mathrm{P_{1}}
and
\mathrm{P_{2}}
be probabilistic event structures.
\mathrm{P}_{1}\,+_{p}\,\mathrm{P}_{2}

is a probabilistic event structure.

Note that, in the valuation of Definition
3.7
), the configurations considered for

\mathrm{P}_{1}
and
\mathrm{P}_{2}
are disjoint. This follows from the fact that, in

\mathrm{P}_{1}\,+_{p}\,\mathrm{P}_{2}
, every event in
\mathrm{P}_{1}
is in conflict with every event in

\mathrm{P}_{2}
. Since configurations are conflict-free by definition, no configuration

x\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{P}_{1}\,+_{p}\,\mathrm{P}_{2})
can contain events from both
\mathrm{P}_{1}
and

\mathrm{P}_{2}
. Hence, the configurations
x\backslash\tau
used in the valuation for
\mathrm{P}_{1}
and

\mathrm{P}_{2}
are disjoint.

Furthermore, from Definition
3.7
, it is also possible to notice that

\mathrm{P}\,+_{p}\,\mathrm{P}\neq\mathrm{P}
. This is a consequence of introducing the
\tau
event in

\mathrm{P}\,+_{p}\,\mathrm{P}
.

Remark 2
.

Another way of representing
\mathrm{P}_{1}\,+_{p}\,\mathrm{P}_{2}
is by putting the probabilities
explicit on both sides,
i.e.

p\cdot\mathrm{P}_{1}+(1-p)\cdot\mathrm{P}_{2}
. That leaves us with

\mathrm{P}_{1}\,+_{p}\,\mathrm{P}_{2}=p\cdot\mathrm{P}_{1}+(1-p)\cdot\mathrm{P}_{2}

Remark 3
.

Definition
3.7
can be generalized to allow the probabilistic composition of
n

probabilistic event structures. Consider we have a finite number
n
of probabilistic event
structures. Let
1\leq i\leq n
and define
\sum_{i}p_{i}\cdot\mathrm{P}_{i}
, with
\sum_{i}p_{i}=1
,
as follows:

\displaystyle E=\set{\tau}\uplus\biguplus_{i}E_{i}

\displaystyle\leq\ =\set{\tau\leq e\mid e\in E}\cup\biguplus_{i}\leq_{i}

\displaystyle\#=\biguplus_{i}\#_{i}\cup\set{e_{i}\#e_{j}\mid e_{i}\in E_{i},\,e_{j}\in E_{j},1\leq i\neq j\leq n}

\displaystyle\hskip 46.94687pt\cup\set{e_{j}\#e_{i}\mid e_{i}\in E_{i},\,e_{j}\in E_{j},1\leq i\neq j\leq n}

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathcal{C}\left(\sum_{i}p_{i}\cdot\mathrm{P}_{i}\right)\,.\,v(x)=\begin{cases}p_{i}\cdot v_{i}(x\backslash\tau)&\text{ if }x\backslash\tau\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{P}_{i})\\
1&\text{ if }x=\set{\tau}\vee x=\emptyset\end{cases}

This will be useful when showing the equivalence between the operational and the denotational
semantics.

For the parallel composition, and by considering the intuition that the parallel composition places
“side-by-side” the two event structures, the valuation is determined by multiplying the valuations
obtained from projecting the configuration in
\mathrm{P}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{P}_{2}
onto the corresponding
configurations of
\mathrm{P}_{1}
and
\mathrm{P}_{2}
.

Definition 3.9

(Parallel Composition)
.

Let
\mathrm{P}_{1}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1},\,v_{1})
and
\mathrm{P}_{2}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2},\,v_{2})
be
probabilistic event structures. Define
\mathrm{P}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{P}_{2}=(E,\,\leq,\,\#,\,v)
as:

\displaystyle E=E_{1}\uplus E_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\ =\leq_{1}\uplus\leq_{2}

\displaystyle\#=\#_{1}\uplus\#_{2}

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{P}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{P}_{2})\,.\,v(x)=v_{1}(x\cap E_{1})\cdot v_{2}(x\cap E_{2})

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
\mathrm{P_{1}}
and
\mathrm{P_{2}}
be probabilistic event structures.
\mathrm{P}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{P}_{2}
is a
probabilistic event structure.

We can now interpret the language shown in Section
3.1
as probabilistic event
structures.

Definition 3.11
.

We interpret commands as probabilistic event structures as follows:

\displaystyle\llbracket skip\rrbracket=(\set{sk},\set{sk\leq sk},\emptyset,v(\set{sk})=1)

\displaystyle\llbracket a\rrbracket=(\set{a},\set{a\leq a},\emptyset,v(\set{a})=1)

\displaystyle\llbracket C_{1}\,+_{p}\,C_{2}\rrbracket=\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\,+_{p}\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket C_{1}\,;\,C_{2}\rrbracket=\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\,;\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket C_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\rrbracket=\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\,||\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket

From Section
2
, we can deduce that an extension of Definition
2.39

to the probabilistic setting is unsuitable for relating the operational and the denotational
semantics. Hence, we extend Definition
2.21
to the probabilistic realm. Recall what
was said regarding plain and composite events in Section
2.2
above
Definition
2.21
.

Definition 3.12
.

Let
\mathrm{P}_{1}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1},\,v_{1})
and
\mathrm{P}_{2}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2},\,v_{2})
be
probabilistic event structures. Say
\mathrm{P}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{P}_{2}
whenever exists an injective
function
f:E_{1}\rightarrow E_{2}
such that
\forall e,e^{\prime}\in E_{1}
:

\displaystyle\pi(f(e))=\pi(e)

\displaystyle e\leq_{1}e^{\prime}\implies f(e)\leq_{2}f(e^{\prime})

\displaystyle e\#_{1}e^{\prime}\implies f(e)\#_{2}f(e^{\prime})

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{P}_{1}),y\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{P}_{2})\,.\,f[x]\subseteq y\Rightarrow v_{1}(x)\geq v_{2}(y)

We say that two probabilistic event structures
\mathrm{P}_{1},\mathrm{P}_{2}
are similar, denoted

\mathrm{P}_{1}\equiv\mathrm{P}_{2}
, iff
\mathrm{P}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{P}_{2}
and

\mathrm{P}_{2}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{P}_{1}
.

To define
\sqsubseteq
in the probabilistic setting, we based ourselves on the fact that, given two
configurations
x
and
y
such that
x\subseteq y
the probability of
x
must be greater or equal
to the probability of
y
,
i.e.

v(x)\geq v(y)
. This comes from the intuition that the
probability tends to decrease as long as the computation continues.

The valuation condition in Definition
3.12
uses
v_{1}(x)\geq v_{2}(y)
instead of

v_{1}(x)\geq v_{2}(f[x])
, because
f[x]
may not be a configuration in
\mathrm{P}_{2}
. For instance, let

\mathrm{P}_{1}=a
and
\mathrm{P}_{2}=b\rightarrowtriangle a
. It is straightforward to see that

a\sqsubseteq b\rightarrowtriangle a
, via the identity function. However,

id[\{a\}]\not\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{P}_{2})
, since in
\mathrm{P}_{2}
the event
a
causally depends on
b
,
and therefore cannot occur on its own.

Furthermore, note that in Definition
3.12
, the conditions on causal and conflict
relations use an implication rather than an equivalence (recall that in the classical case, the
definition of sub-similar event structures used an equivalence – Definition
2.21
).
This adjustment is necessary because the interaction between probabilistic choice and parallel
