composition can duplicate events, introducing new causal and conflict dependencies. For example, in
Figure
17
, the event
c
is concurrent with all others, while in
Figure
18
its copies
c^{\prime}
and
c^{\prime\prime}
now causally depend on
\tau
and are each
in conflict with events from the opposite branch (
i.e.

c^{\prime}
is in conflict with
b
and
c^{\prime\prime}
is in
conflict with
a
). Hence, if in Definition
3.12
we kept the “equivalence”, then
Lemma
3.23
would not hold and, consequently we would not be able to obtain
soundness neither adequacy.

c_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1}}
a_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}
b_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(1-p)}}
\tau_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1}}

\displaystyle v(x)=\begin{cases}p&\text{ if }a\in x\\
1-p&\text{ if }b\in x\\
1&\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

Figure 17
:
Probabilistic event structure of

(a\,+_{p}\,b)\,||\,c

\tau_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1}}
a_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}
b_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(1-p)}}
c^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}}
c^{\prime\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(1-p)}}

\displaystyle v(x)=\begin{cases}p&\text{if }a\text{ or }c^{\prime}\in x,\\
1-p&\text{if }b\text{ or }c^{\prime\prime}\in x,\\
1&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Figure 18
:
Probabilistic event structure of
(a\,||\,c)\,+_{p}\,(b\,||\,c)

To remove the initial event of a probabilistic event structure, we need to guarantee that the
probability of said event is not zero. Because if the event had probability zero, removing it would
lead to a division by zero. Furthermore, this is the reason why
p\in]0,1[
in the probabilistic
operator.

Definition 3.13

(Remove initial event)
.

Let
\mathrm{P}=(E,\,\leq,\,\#,\,v)
be a probabilistic event structure and
a\in\mathcal{I}(\mathrm{P})
,
s.t
v(\set{a})\neq 0
. Define
\mathrm{P}\backslash a=(E^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},\#^{\prime},v^{\prime})
as

\displaystyle E^{\prime}=\set{e\in E\mid\neg(e\#a),\,e\neq a}

\displaystyle\leq^{\prime}\,=\,\set{e\leq e^{\prime}\mid e,e^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\#^{\prime}\,=\,\set{e\#e^{\prime}\mid e,e^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{P}\backslash a)\,.\,v^{\prime}(x)=\dfrac{v(x\cup\set{a})}{v(\set{a})}

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
\mathrm{P}
be a probabilistic event structure.
\mathrm{P}\backslash a
is a probabilistic event
structure.

3.3
Results

In this section we present the results obtained in the probabilistic realm. For this, we interpret
the special command
\checkmark
as the empty probabilistic event structure whose valuation of the
empty configuration is
1
,
i.e.

\llbracket\checkmark\rrbracket=(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,v(\emptyset)=1)
.

We begin by showing that the sequential, parallel, and probabilistic operators are monotonic with
respect to Definition
3.12
.

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
\mathrm{P}_{1},\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{1},\mathrm{P}_{2},\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{2}
be probabilistic event structures. If

\mathrm{P}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{1}
and
\mathrm{P}_{2}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{2}
then

\mathrm{P}_{1}\,;\,\mathrm{P}_{2}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{1}\,;\,\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{2}
.

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
\mathrm{P}_{1},\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{1},\mathrm{P}_{2},\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{2}
be probabilistic event structures. If

\mathrm{P}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{1}
and
\mathrm{P}_{2}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{2}
then

\mathrm{P}_{1}\,+_{p}\,\mathrm{P}_{2}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{1}\,+_{p}\,\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{2}
.

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
\mathrm{P}_{1},\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{1},\mathrm{P}_{2},\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{2}
be probabilistic event structures. If

\mathrm{P}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{1}
and
\mathrm{P}_{2}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{2}
then

\mathrm{P}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{P}_{2}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{2}
.

We now show how probabilistic event structures composed by the sequential and concurrent operators
interact with the removal of initial events.

Lemma 3.18
.

Let
\mathrm{P}_{1}
and
\mathrm{P}_{2}
be probabilistic event structures. Consider

\mathrm{P}_{1}\,;\,\mathrm{P}_{2}
such that
l\in\mathcal{I}(\mathrm{P}_{1}\,;\,\mathrm{P}_{2})
. Then

(\mathrm{P}_{1}\,;\,\mathrm{P}_{2})\backslash l\equiv(\mathrm{P}_{1}\backslash l)\,;\,\mathrm{P}_{2}
.

Lemma 3.19
.

Let
\mathrm{P}_{1}
and
\mathrm{P}_{2}
be probabilistic event structures. Consider

\mathrm{P}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{P}_{2}
such that
l\in\mathcal{I}(\mathrm{P}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{P}_{2})
. Then

(\mathrm{P}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{P}_{2})\backslash l\equiv\begin{cases}(\mathrm{P}_{1}\backslash l)\,||\,\mathrm{P}_{2}&\text{ if }l\in\mathcal{I}(\mathrm{P}_{1})\\
\mathrm{P}_{1}\,||\,(\mathrm{P}_{2}\backslash l)&\text{ if }l\in\mathcal{I}(\mathrm{P}_{2})\end{cases}

It is straightforward to see that:

Lemma 3.20
.

Let
\mathrm{P}_{1},\mathrm{P}_{2}
be probabilistic event structures. Then

\mathrm{P}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{P}_{2}=\mathrm{P}_{2}\,||\,\mathrm{P}_{1}
.

From Definition
2.22
, we can infer that, in a probabilistic event structure
interpreting a command, any configuration consisting solely of an initial event has valuation

1
. The following lemma confirms this intuition.

Lemma 3.21
.

Let
C
be a command and
l\in\mathcal{I}(\llbracket C\rrbracket)
. Then
v(\set{l})=1
.

We start by presenting two useful lemmas to prove small-soundness (Lemma
3.26
). The
first lemma states that sequentially composing a sum of probabilistic event structures with another
probabilistic event structure is equal to first sequentially composing the probabilistic event
structures and then using the probabilistic operator.

Lemma 3.22
.

Let
\mathrm{P},\sum_{i}p_{i}\cdot\mathrm{P}_{i}
be probabilistic event structures. Then

\left(\sum_{i}p_{i}\cdot\mathrm{P}_{i}\right)\,;\,\mathrm{P}=\sum_{i}p_{i}\cdot(\mathrm{P}_{i}\,;\,\mathrm{P})

The second lemma expresses a certain “lax” linearity condition of concurrent composition.

Lemma 3.23
.

Let
\mathrm{P},\sum_{i}p_{i}\cdot\mathrm{P}_{i}
be probabilistic event structures.

1.

\left(\sum_{i}p_{i}\cdot\mathrm{P}_{i}\right)\,||\,\mathrm{P}\sqsubseteq\sum_{i}p_{i}\cdot(\mathrm{P}_{i}\,||\,\mathrm{P})

2.

x\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\left(\sum_{i}p_{i}\cdot\mathrm{P}_{i}\right)\,||\,\mathrm{P})
iff

x\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\sum_{i}p_{i}\cdot(\mathrm{P}_{i}\,||\,\mathrm{P}))

The following lemma helps proving soundness (Theorem
3.27
) and adequacy
(Theorem
3.29
). Intuitively it states that if the invisible action
\tau
triggers a
transition from
C
, which is sequentially or concurrently composed, to

\sum_{i}p_{i}C_{i}
, then any maximal configuration of
\llbracket C\rrbracket
is a maximal configuration
in
\sum_{i}p_{i}\llbracket C_{i}\rrbracket
. Furthermore, there exists a command
\llbracket C_{i}\rrbracket
in

\sum_{i}p_{i}\llbracket C_{i}\rrbracket
where the valuation of the maximal configuration matches the valuation
of the maximal configuration in
\llbracket C\rrbracket
.

Lemma 3.24
.

Let
C=C_{1}\,;\,C_{2}
or
C=C_{1}\,||\,C_{2}
.
If
C\rightarrow\sum_{i}p_{i}\cdot(\tau,C_{i})
then
x\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\llbracket C\rrbracket)
and

x\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\sum_{i}p_{i}\cdot\llbracket C_{i}\rrbracket)
such that
\exists\llbracket C_{i}\rrbracket\,.\,v(x)=v_{i}(x)
.

The following lemma tells us that the big-step semantics always progresses,
i.e.
the system always
has at least one valid transition to make.

Lemma 3.25
.

For any
C
, exists
\sum_{i}p_{i}(\omega_{i},C_{i})
such that

C\twoheadrightarrow\sum_{i}p_{i}(\omega_{i},C_{i})
.

We can now establish a relation between the small-step and denotational semantics. Recall that in
Figure
13
, transitions are labeled either by an atomic or invisible action. We can
use that to establish a relation between the small-step and denotational semantics. Intuitively, if
a transition from
C
is triggered by an atomic action to
C^{\prime}
, then we should observe
the same behavior as in Lemma
2.33
. Otherwise, if the invisible action triggers the
transition, we transition from
C
to
\sum_{i}p_{i}C_{i}
. Having in mind
Lemma
3.23
, we expect
\llbracket C\rrbracket
to be a sub-probabilistic event structure
of
\sum_{i}p_{i}\llbracket C_{i}\rrbracket
.

Lemma 3.26

(Soundness I)
.

•

If
C\rightarrow 1\cdot(l,C^{\prime})
then

\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket\equiv\llbracket C\rrbracket\backslash l

•

If
C\rightarrow\sum_{i}p_{i}(\tau,C_{i})
then

\llbracket C\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\sum_{i}p_{i}\llbracket C_{i}\rrbracket

At this point, we can prove soundness, establishing a relation between the big-step and the
denotational semantics. Recall that the big-step semantics of a command
C
returns a
probability distribution of possible outcomes. Each outcome is a pair, formed by a word
\omega

(describing the sequence of events that occurred), and either a command
C^{\prime}
, with
instructions yet to execute, or the special symbol
\checkmark
, to indicate termination,
i.e.

C\twoheadrightarrow\sum_{i}p_{i}(\omega_{i},\checkmark)+\sum_{j}p_{j}(\omega_{j},C_{j})

where, unlike Figure
14
, we separate computations that terminate from those that do
not. Having in mind our experience with soundness in the non-deterministic case
(Theorem
2.34
), if a word leads to a terminal computation then there exists a maximal
configuration that matches the word. Consequently, the probability associated with that terminal
command equals the valuation of the maximal configuration.

Theorem 3.27

(Soundness II)
.

If
C\twoheadrightarrow p_{0}(\omega_{0},\checkmark)+\sum_{k}p_{k}(\omega_{k},C_{k})

then exists
x_{0}\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\llbracket C\rrbracket)
such that

\emptyset\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\omega_{0}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}\ }}x_{0}
and
p_{0}=v(x_{0})
.

We now center our attention to show adequacy. Similar to what was done previously, we check if a
relation exists between the denotational and small-step semantics. To do this, we will take
advantage of the initial events. If an initial event represents an atomic action, we should be in a
similar situation as in the small-adequacy lemma in the non-deterministic case
(Lemma
2.35
). Otherwise, if the initial event represents an invisible action, we deal
with a transition triggered by a probabilistic command. In this case, we need to adapt the previous
situation to the probabilistic case, considering Lemma
3.23
and the similar
case in small-soundness (Lemma
3.26
).

Lemma 3.28

(Adequacy I)
.

Let
l^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}(\llbracket C\rrbracket)
.

1.

If
l^{\prime}\neq\tau
then

\exists C^{\prime}\in(C\cup\set{\checkmark})\,.\,C\rightarrow 1\cdot(l^{\prime},C^{\prime})
and
\llbracket C\rrbracket\backslash l^{\prime}\equiv\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket
.

2.

If
l^{\prime}=\tau
then

\exists C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime}\,.\,\exists e^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}(\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket)\,.\,C\rightarrow p\cdot C^{\prime}+(1-p)\cdot C^{\prime\prime}
and

\llbracket C\rrbracket\sqsubseteq p\cdot\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket+(1-p)\cdot\llbracket C^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket
, with

p=v(\set{\tau,e^{\prime}})
.

Note that in Lemma
3.28
, for the case where
l^{\prime}=\tau
, we only consider the existence
of an initial event of a command
C^{\prime}
. This is because to know the probability of
C^{\prime\prime}

to occur, we only need to know the probability
p
and subtract it from
1
. Furthermore, the
result states
\llbracket C\rrbracket\sqsubseteq p\cdot\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket+(1-p)\cdot\llbracket C^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket
instead
of
\llbracket C\rrbracket\equiv p\cdot\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket+(1-p)\cdot\llbracket C^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket
. The use of
sub-similarity instead of similarity arises with a similar argument as the one given by changing
from “equivalence” to “implication” in the definition of sub-similar probabilistic event
structures Definition
3.12
. For instance, consider the following small-step transition

(a\,+_{p}\,b)\,||\,c\longrightarrow p\cdot(\tau,a\,||\,c)+(1-p)\cdot(\tau,b\,||\,c)
. Here, the action
c
is duplicated on both branches of the probabilistic choice. Now
recall Figure
17
, corresponding to
\llbracket(a\,+_{p}\,b)\,||\,c\rrbracket
, and
Figure
17
, corresponding to

p\cdot\llbracket a\,||\,c\rrbracket+(1-p)\cdot\llbracket b\,||\,c\rrbracket
. In the former figure,
c
is concurrent
with every event, while in the latter figure, the copies of
c
,
c^{\prime}
and
c^{\prime\prime}
causally depend on

\tau
,
c^{\prime}
is in conflict with
b
, and
c^{\prime\prime}
is in conflict with
a
. These new relations are
not present in
\llbracket(a\,+_{p}\,b)\,||\,c\rrbracket
. Because the duplication of events in the
probabilistic branches introduces new causal and conflict relations, the probabilistic event
structure obtained after the transition cannot be similar to the original one, it can only be
sub-similar. Thus, the usage of
\sqsubseteq
in the second case of Lemma
3.28
.

We are ready to demonstrate the equivalence between the denotational and big-step semantics. The
reasoning is similar to the one presented in Theorem
3.27
.

Theorem 3.29

(Adequacy II)
.

For all
x_{0}\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\llbracket C\rrbracket)
, if

\emptyset\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\omega_{x_{0}}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}\ }}x_{0}
then we have

C\twoheadrightarrow v(x_{0})(\omega_{0},\checkmark)+\sum_{k}p_{k}(\omega_{k},C_{k})
,
for some
\omega_{k},p_{k},C_{k}
.

In Lemma
3.26
and Lemma
3.28
we see the usefulness of introducing the label

\tau
. It helps us identifying the situations where a transition occurred due to the probabilistic
command and when it did not.

Theorem
3.27
assures us that whenever any execution of the program leads to a
terminal command, we have a maximal configuration who matches the word and the respective
probability. Theorem
3.29
tells us that for every maximal configuration of a command

C
and for every covering chain of that configuration, there is an execution of the program
leading to a terminal command who matches the covering chain and the its respective probability.

3.4
Introducing cyclic behavior

We now introduce cyclic behavior to the language in Section
3.1
. In order to avoid
the introduction of the notion of state in the language, the cyclic behavior will be given by
recursion. In that way, we do not need to associate the notion of state to a command in the
operational semantics. We can just keep recording the actions that are being made by the program.

Another thing to have in mind is that with cyclic behavior we open the door to infinite
computations. However, covering chains are only defined in finite sequence of words and infinite
configurations are odd, because we would need to define precisely what it means to be an infinite
configuration. Hence, the words that we formed with the n-step will be always finite, despite the
possibility of them being infinite. We can justify this by saying that we are only concerned on the
“interesting words”,
i.e.
those who are finite.

To introduce recursion we need to add some restrictions when forming programs, since we do not want
to allow commands like:
\mu X.X\,;\,a
and
\mu X.a\,;\,X\,;\,b
.

Let
X\subseteq Var
, with
Var
a set of variables. The syntax is now given by:

C::=skip\mid a\in Act\mid C\,;\,C\mid C\,+_{p}\,C\mid C\,||\,C\mid\mu X.C\mid X

We define the set of free-variables and bound-variables as follows:

FV(skip)=\emptyset

BV(skip)=\emptyset

FV(a)=\emptyset

BV(a)=\emptyset

FV(C_{1}\,;\,C_{2})=FV(C_{1})\cup FV(C_{2})

BV(C_{1}\,;\,C_{2})=BV(C_{1})\cup BV(C_{2})

FV(C_{1}\,||\,C_{2})=FV(C_{1})\cup FV(C_{2})

BV(C_{1}\,||\,C_{2})=BV(C_{1})\cup BV(C_{2})

FV(C_{1}\,+_{p}\,C_{2})=FV(C_{1})\cup FV(C_{2})

BV(C_{1}\,+_{p}\,C_{2})=BV(C_{1})\cup BV(C_{2})

FV(X)=\set{X}

BV(X)=\emptyset

FV(\mu X.C)=FV(C)\backslash\set{X}

BV(\mu X.C)=\set{X}\cup BV(C)

We restrict the sequential composition to those whose free-variables and bound-variables on the left
are empty,
i.e.

C_{1}\,;\,C_{2}
if
FV(C_{1})=\emptyset=BV(C_{1})
.
With this restriction we forbid program like
\mu X.X\,;\,a
,

\mu X.a\,;\,X\,;\,b
(with the condition
FV(C_{1})=\emptyset
) and

(\mu X.a\,;\,X)\,;\,b
(with the condition
BV(C_{1})=\emptyset
).
We want to forbid these kind of programs in sequential composition, because if
C_{1}
never
terminates then the sequential composition never terminates. This is also a restriction that comes
from the fact that covering chains are only defined in finite sequences and that infinite
configurations are odd in event structures. Note however that we allow programs like

\mu X.X\,||\,a
and
\mu X.X\,\square\,a
, since they do not block the computation.

Inspired by
[
7
]
, we define substitution as follows:

Definition 3.30
.

Let
X\in Var
and
C,C^{\prime}
be commands. Define
C[X\leftarrow C^{\prime}]
,
where we substitute every free occurrence of
X
in
C
by
C^{\prime}
(while changing bound
variables to avoid clashes) by induction on
C
as follows:

\displaystyle skip[X\leftarrow C^{\prime}]=skip

\displaystyle a[X\leftarrow C^{\prime}]=a

\displaystyle(C_{1}\,;\,C_{2})[X\leftarrow C^{\prime}]=C_{1}\,;\,(C_{2}[X\leftarrow C^{\prime}])

\displaystyle(C_{1}\,||\,C_{2})[X\leftarrow C^{\prime}]=C_{1}[X\leftarrow C^{\prime}]\,||\,C_{2}[X\leftarrow C^{\prime}]

\displaystyle(C_{1}\,+_{p}\,C_{2})[X\leftarrow C^{\prime}]=C_{1}[X\leftarrow C^{\prime}]\,+_{p}\,C_{2}[X\leftarrow C^{\prime}]

\displaystyle X[X\leftarrow C]=C

\displaystyle(\mu X.C)[X\leftarrow C^{\prime}]=\mu X.C

\displaystyle(\mu Y.C)[X\leftarrow C^{\prime}]=\mu Y.C[X\leftarrow C^{\prime}]\text{ if }X\neq Y\text{ and }Y\not\in FV(C^{\prime})

We add to Figure
13
the following rules for the recursion command:

\displaystyle\mu X.C\rightarrow 1\cdot(l,C^{\prime}[X\leftarrow\mu X.C])C\rightarrow 1\cdot(l,C^{\prime})\hskip 28.45274pt\mu X.C\rightarrow\sum_{i}p_{i}\cdot(\tau,C_{i}[X\leftarrow\mu X.C])C\rightarrow\sum_{i}p_{i}\cdot(\tau,C_{i})

Similarly to what was done in Figure
13
, here we also distinguish if the action
that triggered the transition was an atomic or invisible action. Consequently, we define a rule for
each case. Both rules follow the same reasoning as the one explained in the non-deterministic
case. However, in the case where the transition is triggered by
\tau
, we need to substitute the
occurrence of
X
by
\mu X.C
in each
C_{i}
that is in

\sum_{i}p_{i}\cdot(\tau,C_{i})
.

Now we develop an illustrative example of recursion in the probabilistic setting.

Example 3.31
.

Figure
19
illustrates a probabilistic coin toss scenario where each time we toss
the coin, it executes with probability
p
the command
skip
or continues the tossing with
probability
1-p
. To understand this behavior, focus on the initial command. From there, we
transit to a distribution formed by the commands
skip
and

\mu X.(X\,+_{p}\,skip)
, which is the same as the initial command. From this
distribution we transit to
skip
with probability
p
or to

\mu X.(X\,+_{p}\,skip)
with probability
1-p
, enabling us to repeat the process.

\mu X.(skip\,+_{p}\,X)
skip
\bullet
\tau
p
1-p
\mu X.(skip\,+_{p}\,X)
skip
\bullet
\tau
p
1-p
\mu X.(skip\,+_{p}\,X)
skip
\bullet
\tau
p
1-p
\ddots

Figure 19
:
Fragment of the execution of
\mu X.(skip\,+_{p}\,X)

On the event structure side, we want to use the Knaster-Tarski Theorem to build the least-fix point.
To define it, we will use an order that does not ignore copies, differently from what happens with
Definition
3.12
.

Definition 3.32
.

Let
\mathrm{P}_{1}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1},\,v_{1})
and
\mathrm{P}_{2}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2},\,v_{2})
be
probabilistic event structures. Say
\mathrm{P}_{1}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{P}_{2}
if:

\displaystyle E_{1}\subseteq E_{2}

\displaystyle\forall e,e^{\prime}\ .\ e\leq_{1}e^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow e,e^{\prime}\in E_{1}\wedge e\leq_{2}e^{\prime}

\displaystyle\forall e,e^{\prime}\ .\ e\#_{1}e^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow e,e^{\prime}\in E_{1}\wedge e\#_{2}e^{\prime}

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathcal{C}(P)_{1}\,.\,v_{1}(x)=v_{2}(x)

The following lemmas confirm that Definition
3.32
is a partial order and that
it possesses a least element.

Lemma 3.33
.

\trianglelefteq
is a partial order.

Lemma 3.34
.

Define
\bot=(\emptyset,\,\emptyset,\,\emptyset,\,v_{\bot}(\emptyset)=1)
.
\bot
is the least
element of
\trianglelefteq
.

We extend Definition
2.42
to the probabilistic realm,
i.e.
we define what it means for a
probabilistic event structure to be a least upper bound in a chain of probabilistic event
structures.

Definition 3.35
.

Let
\mathrm{P}_{1}\trianglelefteq\dots\trianglelefteq\mathrm{P}_{n}\trianglelefteq\dots
be a

\omega
-chain. Let
\mathrm{P}^{\omega}=(E^{\omega},\,\leq^{\omega},\,\#^{\omega},\,v^{\omega})
be its
least upper bound where:

•

E^{\omega}=\cup_{n\in\omega}E_{n}

•

\leq^{\omega}=\cup_{n\in\omega}\leq_{n}

•

\#^{\omega}=\cup_{n\in\omega}\#_{n}

•

\forall x\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{P}^{\omega})\,,\,\exists n\in\omega\,.\,x\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{P}_{n})\,.\,v^{\omega}(x)=v_{n}(x)

We then show that the structure in Definition
3.35
is in fact a probabilistic event
structure and a least upper bound in a chain of probabilistic event structures.

Lemma 3.36
.

\mathrm{P}^{\omega}
is a probabilistic event structure.

Lemma 3.37
.

Let
\mathrm{P}_{1}\trianglelefteq\dots\trianglelefteq\mathrm{P}_{n}\trianglelefteq\dots
be a

\omega
-chain. Then
\mathrm{P}^{\omega}
is its least upper bound.

We have that sequential and concurrent composition, as well as probabilistic choice are monotone and
continuous with respect to Definition
3.32
. Recall that monotonicity and
continuity definitions are given by Definition
2.45
.

Lemma 3.38
.

Let
\mathrm{P},\mathrm{P}_{1},\mathrm{P}_{2}
be probabilistic event structures. If

\mathrm{P}_{1}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{P}_{2}
then

\mathrm{P}\,;\,\mathrm{P}_{1}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{P}\,;\,\mathrm{P}_{2}
.

Lemma 3.39
.

Let
\mathrm{P}_{1},\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{1},\mathrm{P}_{2},\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{2}
be probabilistic event structures. If

\mathrm{P}_{1}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{1}
and
\mathrm{P}_{2}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{2}
then

\mathrm{P}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{P}_{2}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{2}
.

Lemma 3.40
.

Let
\mathrm{P}_{1},\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{1},\mathrm{P}_{2},\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{2}
be probabilistic event structures. If

\mathrm{P}_{1}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{1}
and
\mathrm{P}_{2}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{2}
then

\mathrm{P}_{1}\,+_{p}\,\mathrm{P}_{2}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{1}\,+_{p}\,\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{2}
.

Lemma 3.41
.

\bigsqcup_{m}(\mathrm{P}\,;\,\mathrm{P}_{m})=\mathrm{P}\,;\,\bigsqcup_{m}\mathrm{P}_{m}
.

Lemma 3.42
.

\bigsqcup_{n,m}(\mathrm{P}_{n}\,||\,\mathrm{P}_{m})=\bigsqcup_{n}\mathrm{P}_{n}\,||\,\bigsqcup_{m}\mathrm{P}_{m}
.

Lemma 3.43
.

\bigsqcup_{n,m}(\mathrm{P}_{n}\,+_{p}\,\mathrm{P}_{m})=\bigsqcup_{n}\mathrm{P}_{n}\,+_{p}\,\bigsqcup_{m}\mathrm{P}_{m}
.

When adapting Lemma
2.49
(helps to show that operators defined in event structures are
continuous) and Lemma
2.53
(a version of the Kleene fixed-point theorem for the case
of event structures) to the probabilistic realm, we notice that the proofs we need to do are
analogous to the ones done in the non-deterministic case. Hence, we omit their formulation in this
section.

We now extend Definition
3.11
with the recursion and let
\mathbb{P}
denote the class
of probabilistic event structures.

Definition 3.44
.

Define an environment to be a function
\gamma:Var\rightarrow\mathbb{P}
from variables to
probabilistic event structures. For a command
C
and an environment
\gamma
define

\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\gamma}
as follows:

\displaystyle\llbracket skip\rrbracket_{\gamma}=(\set{sk},\set{sk\leq sk},\emptyset,v(\set{sk}=1))

\displaystyle\llbracket a\rrbracket_{\gamma}=(\set{a},\set{a\leq a},\emptyset,v(\set{a}=1))

\displaystyle\llbracket C_{1}\,;\,C_{2}\rrbracket_{\gamma}=\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{\gamma}\,;\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{\gamma}

\displaystyle\llbracket C_{1}\,+_{p}\,C_{2}\rrbracket_{\gamma}=\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{\gamma}\,+_{p}\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{\gamma}

\displaystyle\llbracket C_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\rrbracket_{\gamma}=\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{\gamma}\,||\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{\gamma}

\displaystyle\llbracket X\rrbracket_{\gamma}=\gamma(X)

\displaystyle\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma}=fix(\Gamma^{C,\gamma})

where
\Gamma^{C,\gamma}:\mathrm{P}\rightarrow\mathrm{P}
is given by

\Gamma^{C,\gamma}(\mathrm{P})=\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\gamma(X\leftarrow\mathrm{P})}
.

We now show that
\Gamma^{C,\gamma}
is continuous.

Lemma 3.45
.

\Gamma^{C,\gamma}
is continuous.

To prove a lemma that is helpful for showing soundness (Lemma
3.48
), we need the
probabilistic versions of the substitution lemma (Lemma
2.56
) and
Lemma
2.57
. Fortunately, the formulation of these probabilistic versions is the same as the
original ones. However, the proof of the substitution lemma needs to adjusted to accommodate the
probabilistic command. Hence, we show here its formulation (Lemma
3.46
).

Lemma 3.46
.

\llbracket C^{\prime}[X\leftarrow\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma}]\rrbracket_{\gamma}=\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\gamma{(X\leftarrow\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma}})}

To prove Theorem
3.49
, we found it helpful to have the following lemma, whose
reasoning is similar to that of Lemma
3.24
.

Lemma 3.47
.

If

\mu X.C\rightarrow\sum_{i}p_{i}\cdot(\tau,C_{i}[X\leftarrow\mu X.C])

then
x\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma})
and

x\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\sum_{i}p_{i}\cdot\llbracket C_{i}[X\leftarrow\mu X.C]\rrbracket_{\gamma})
such that

\exists\llbracket C_{i}\rrbracket_{\gamma}\,.\,v(x)=v_{i}(x)
.

With the introduction of recursion, the formulation of the equivalence between operational and
denotational semantics is as in the case without recursion. We proceed to show the formulation of
the respective lemmas and theorems, since the proof needs to be completed to contemplate the
recursion case.

Lemma 3.48

(Soundness I)
.

•

If
C\rightarrow 1\cdot(l,C^{\prime})
then

\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\gamma}\equiv\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\gamma}\backslash l

•

If
C\rightarrow\sum_{i}p_{i}(\tau,C_{i})
then

\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\gamma}\sqsubseteq\sum_{i}p_{i}\llbracket C_{i}\rrbracket_{\gamma}

Theorem 3.49

(Soundness II)
.

If
C\twoheadrightarrow p_{0}(\omega_{0},\checkmark)+\sum_{k}p_{k}(\omega_{k},C_{k})

then exists
x_{0}\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\gamma})
such that

\emptyset\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\omega_{0}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}\ }}x_{0}
and
p_{0}=v(x_{0})
.

Lemma 3.50

(Adequacy I)
.

Let
l^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}(\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\gamma})
.

1.

If
l^{\prime}\neq\tau
then

\exists C^{\prime}\in(C\cup\set{\checkmark})\,.\,C\rightarrow 1\cdot(l^{\prime},C^{\prime})
and

\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\gamma}\backslash l^{\prime}\equiv\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\gamma}
.

2.

If
l^{\prime}=\tau
then

\exists C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime}\,.\,\exists e^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}(\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\gamma})\,.\,C\rightarrow p\cdot C^{\prime}+(1-p)\cdot C^{\prime\prime}
and

\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\gamma}\sqsubseteq p\cdot\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\gamma}+(1-p)\cdot\llbracket C^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket_{\gamma}
, with

p=v(\set{\tau,e^{\prime}})
.

Theorem 3.51

(Adequacy II)
.

For all
x_{0}\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\gamma})
, if

\emptyset\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\omega_{x_{0}}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}\ }}x_{0}
then we have

C\twoheadrightarrow v(x_{0})(\omega_{0},\checkmark)+\sum_{k}p_{k}(\omega_{k},C_{k})
,
for some
\omega_{k},p_{k},C_{k}
.

The following example shows soundness and adequacy in practice, for commands without recursive
behavior.

Example 3.52
.

The probabilistic event structure in Example
3.2
corresponds to the command in
Example
3.3
.

To see how both semantics relate with each other, recall the maximal configurations in
Example
3.2
and the words that lead to the end of a computation in
Example
3.3
.

Similarly to what was shown in Example
2.61
, it is straightforward to see that each
word corresponds to a covering chain and vice-versa. What is left to verify is the probability.
From Example
3.3
we know that the word
\tau ab
has probability
p
, which is the
same probability of the corresponding covering chain. Similarly, the words
\tau cd
and

\tau dc
have probability
1-p
, which equals the probability of the respective covering chains.

Conversely, if we pick a covering chain of a maximal configuration, we quickly notice that its
probability and the probability of the respective word is the same.

4
Unitary Event Structures

A quantum event
structure
[
19
, Definition 2]
is an event structure together with a function that maps
events to unitary operators or projections on a finite-dimensional Hilbert space

\mathcal{H}
. Additionally, operators of concurrent events must commute in quantum event structures
and the latter support the notion of an initial state considered to be a density operator.

The definition of quantum event structures
[
19
, Definition 2]
does not mention
probabilities, even though probabilities arise as a result of measuring quantum states. So, how do
probabilities arise in quantum event structures? The key lies in the combination of the initial
state together with quantum operators associated with the events in a configuration (recall that a
configuration captures the notion of a computation in event structures). By applying the sequence of
operators associated with the events in a configuration to the initial state, we evolve the state
along that computational path. This process allows us to extract the probabilities related to
measurement outcomes. Winskel formalizes this idea
[
19
, Theorem 3]
. He shows that a
quantum event structure without conflicting events – also called an elementary quantum event
structure – can be transformed into a corresponding probabilistic event structure.

To drop the elementary condition imposed by Winskel in
[
19
, Theorem 3]
, we add two new
conditions to Winskel’s definition of quantum event structures
[
19
, Definition 2]
(in
Section
4.4
we show how these additional constraints allow us to remove the
elementary condition). First, we impose the minimal conflict to be transitive. Secondly, the sum of
the operators of events in minimal conflict, or an event itself, should be a unitary operator. The
intuition behind the restrictions is to consider events in minimal conflicts as measurements. We
call the resultant structure
unitary event structures
. Why do we add these restrictions?
The reason is to ensure that Equation
1
holds when considering unitary
event structures, jointly with an initial state, as probabilistic event structures. Recall from
Equation
1
that conflicting events have a conditional probability no
greater than one. Now, let us consider two events in conflict,
e
and
e^{\prime}
, such that

Q(e)=H=Q(e^{\prime})
. Taking
\ket{0}
as the initial state, the configuration-valuations of
\{e\}

and
\{e^{\prime}\}
are
v(\{e\})=1=v(\{e^{\prime}\})
. It is straightforward to check that
Equation
1
does not hold for
\emptyset\subseteq\{e\},\{e^{\prime}\}
:

\displaystyle v(\emptyset)-v(\{e\})-v(\{e^{\prime}\})\geq 0\Leftrightarrow 1-1-1\geq 0\Leftrightarrow-1\geq 0

Now, consider
Q(e)=P_{0}
and
Q(e^{\prime})=P_{1}
,
i.e.
projections onto
\ket{0}
and
\ket{1}
,
respectively. For any initial state
\rho
, their configuration-valuations are given by

v(\{e\})=p_{0}^{\rho}
and
v(\{e^{\prime}\})=p_{1}^{\rho}
,
i.e.
the probabilities of obtaining
\ket{0}

or
\ket{1}
when measuring
\rho
. Note that
p_{0}^{\rho}+p_{1}^{\rho}=1
. Now
Equation
1
holds for
\emptyset\subseteq\{e\},\{e^{\prime}\}
:

\displaystyle v(\emptyset)-v(\{e\})-v(\{e^{\prime}\})\geq 0\Leftrightarrow 1-p_{0}^{\rho}-p_{1}^{\rho}\geq 0\Leftrightarrow 1-1\geq 0\Leftrightarrow 0\geq 0

However, by being complacent with the satisfaction of Equation
1
we lose
the ability to interpret non-deterministic choice in unitary event structures. For instance, the
program
H\,\square\,X\,\square\,Z
would be represented as
e_{H}\mathrel{\!\!\!\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}{\hbox{\kern 17.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\!\!\!\!}e_{X}\mathrel{\!\!\!\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}{\hbox{\kern 17.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\!\!\!\!}e_{Z}
. Since
minimal conflict is not transitive, this fails to form a unitary event structure.

To define unitary event structures, we use the equivalence class of an event
e
, which is composed
of itself and by the events in which
e
is in minimal conflict,
i.e.

[e]=\set{e^{\prime}\mid e=e^{\prime},\ e\mathrel{\!\!\!\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}{\hbox{\kern 17.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\!\!\!\!}e^{\prime}}
.

Definition 4.1

(Unitary Event Structure)
.

A unitary event structure over a finite-dimensional Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, is a pair

(\mathrm{E},\ Q:E\rightarrow Op(\mathcal{H}))
comprised of an event structure

\mathrm{E}=(E,\,\leq,\,\#)
, where
Q
maps events
e\in E
to either projections or unitary
operators on
\mathcal{H}
such that:

•

\forall e_{1},e_{2}\in E,\,e_{1}\text{ co }e_{2}\Rightarrow Q(e_{1})Q(e_{2})=Q(e_{2})Q(e_{1})

•

\mathrel{\!\!\!\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}{\hbox{\kern 17.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\!\!\!\!}
is transitive

•

\forall e\in E,\ \sum_{e^{\prime}\in[e]}Q(e^{\prime})
is unitary

Given a finite configuration,
x\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{E})
, define the operator
A_{x}
as the
composition
Q_{e_{n}}Q_{e_{n-1}}\dots Q_{e_{2}}Q_{e_{1}}
for some covering chain

\emptyset\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e_{1}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ x_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e_{2}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ x_{2}\dots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e_{n}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ x_{n}
in
\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{E})
, with
x_{n}=x
. We additionally set

A_{\emptyset}=Id:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}
for the initial configuration. An

initial state
is given by a density operator
\rho
on
\mathcal{H}
.

We now show that the operator
A_{x}
in Definition
4.1
is well-defined. For that, we need
an auxiliary definition. Let
\alpha=\dots,e,e^{\prime},\dots
be a finite event sequence. Denote

Q(\alpha)
be the ordered product of operators along
\alpha
, accordingly to
Definition
4.1
,
i.e.

Q(\alpha)=\dots Q(e^{\prime})Q(e)\dots
Furthermore, we also need a
lemma stating that the operators for any two sequence of events that differ from swapping adjacent
concurrent events are the same.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
\alpha=\dots,e,e^{\prime},\dots
and
\beta=\dots,e^{\prime},e,\dots
be two finite event
sequences that differ only by swapping adjacent concurrent events
e\text{ co }e^{\prime}
. Then

\dots Q(e^{\prime})Q(e)\dots=\dots Q(e)Q(e^{\prime})\dots
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha=\dots,e,e^{\prime},\dots
and
\beta=\dots,e^{\prime},e,\dots
be two finite event sequences
such that
e\text{ co }e^{\prime}
. Let
Q(\alpha)=\dots Q(e^{\prime})Q(e)\dots
and

Q(\beta)=\dots Q(e)Q(e^{\prime})\dots
. Since
e\text{ co }e^{\prime}
then
Q(e^{\prime})Q(e)=Q(e)Q(e^{\prime})
. Thus

Q(\alpha)=\dots Q(e^{\prime})Q(e)\dots=\dots Q(e)Q(e^{\prime})\dots=Q(\beta)

∎

As stated by Winskel
[
19
]
, any two covering chains of a configuration
x
are
Mazurkiewicz trace equivalent,
i.e.
obtainable, one from the other, by successively interchanging
concurrent events. This fact together with Lemma
4.2
are crucial for showing
that
A_{x}
is well-defined.

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
x\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{E})
. Then
A_{x}
is well-defined,
i.e.

A_{x}
is independent from the chosen
covering chain of
x
.

Proof.

Let
x\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{E})
and
\alpha,\beta
be two covering chains of
x
. From
Winskel
[
19
]
, we know that any two covering chains of a configuration
x
are
obtainable, one from another, by successively swapping concurrent events (since covering chains
are by definition finite, then the interchanges performed are also finite). By applying
Lemma
4.2
at each swap, the associated operator is preserved at each
step. Hence
Q(\alpha)=Q(\beta)
, and therefore
A_{x}
is independent of the chosen covering
chain. The base case,
A_{\emptyset}=Id
holds by definition.
∎

Example
4.4
is designed for the reader to get used to unitary event structures.

Example 4.4
.

Figure
20
, depicts a unitary event structure composed of the events
a
,

\tau_{0}
,
\tau_{1}
,
d
, and
b
, where the subscript is the respective associated quantum
operator.
a
is the initial event, followed by
\tau_{0}
, which leads to
b
, and
\tau_{1}
, which
leads to
d
. Note that
\tau_{0}
and
\tau_{1}
are in conflict (specifically, minimal conflict)
and that their associated quantum operations form a measurement. Furthermore, since the conflict
relation is hereditary,
d
and
b
are in conflict.

The set of configurations is

\set{\emptyset,\set{a},\set{a,\tau_{0}},\set{a,\tau_{1}},\set{a,\tau_{0},b},\set{a,\tau_{1},d}}
. As said in Definition
4.1
, from a configuration
x
we have as operator

A_{x}
. For example, if we consider the maximal configurations
\set{a,\tau_{0},b}
and

\set{a,\tau_{1},d}
, the respective operators are
ZP_{0}H
and
XP_{1}H
. The former applies the
Hadamard gate to qubit
1
, projects it to
\ket{0}
, and then applies the
Z
gate. After
applying the Hadamard gate, the latter projects the qubit to
\ket{1}
and then applies the
X

gate.

a_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}H(1)}}
{\tau_{0_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P_{0}(1)}}}}
{\tau_{1_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}P_{0}(1)}}}}
b_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}Z(1)}}
d_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}X(1)}}

\begin{array}[]{l}Q(a)=H,\\
Q(\tau_{0})=P_{0},\ Q(\tau_{1})=P_{1},\\
Q(b)=Z(1),\\
Q(d)=X\end{array}

Figure 20
:
Example of an unitary event structure

4.1
Language

We adapt the language shown in Section
2.1
to the quantum setting. For that we need
some preliminaries. We consider at our disposal a finite number of qubits
N
, whose associated
space is
\mathbb{C}^{2^{N}}
. Each qubit is denoted by a natural number
n
and we let
\tilde{n}
be
a sequence of numbers, denoting multiple qubits. We will need the notion of a partial density
operator, which is a density operator whose trace is less or equal to one. We denote by

\mathcal{H}
its associated space and we denote by
\mathcal{D}_{\leq 1}(\mathcal{H})
the set of
partial density operators. We shall use
\rho
to represent a partial density operator. The set of
actions is now composed by a set of unitary gates
\mathcal{U}
together with a set of projections

\set{P_{0}^{n},P_{1}^{n}}
in which
P_{0}^{n}
and
P_{1}^{n}
represent the projection of qubit
n
into

\ket{0}
and
\ket{1}
, respectively. The set of labels is then

L^{\prime}=L\cup\set{P_{0}^{n},\,P_{1}^{n}}
, with
L=Act\cup\{sk\}
.

The set of commands allowed by the language are given by the following grammar:

C::=skip\mid U(\vec{n})\mid C\,;\,C\mid M(n,C_{1},C_{2})\mid C\,||\,C

where
U(\vec{n})
applies the unitary gate
U
to the qubits presented in
\vec{n}
and

M(n,C_{1},C_{2})
represents the measurement of a qubit
n
and if the measurement
was made by
P_{0}^{n}
then we execute
C_{1}
, else if the measurement was made by
P_{1}^{n}
then we
execute
C_{2}
. Note that the behavior of
M(n,C_{1},C_{2})
is similar to that of a
classical if clause.

The set of qubits being used in a command
C
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\text{qVar}(skip)=\emptyset

\displaystyle\text{qVar}(U(\vec{n}))=\vec{n}

\displaystyle\text{qVar}(M(n,C_{1},C_{2}))=\set{n}\cup\text{qVar}(C_{1})\cup\text{qVar}(C_{2})

\displaystyle\text{qVar}(C_{1}\,;\,C_{2})=\text{qVar}(C_{1})\cup\text{qVar}(C_{2})

\displaystyle\text{qVar}(C_{1}\,||\,C_{2})=\text{qVar}(C_{1})\cup\text{qVar}(C_{2})

We restrict the parallel operator to only compose commands with disjoint variables,
i.e.

C_{1}\,||\,C_{2}
iff
\text{qVar}(C_{1})\cap\text{qVar}(C_{2})=\emptyset
.

To define the operational semantics, we add a new symbol, denoted by
\checkmark
, that indicates
the end of a computation. We define the small-step transition step

\xrightarrow{a}\subseteq C\times L^{\prime}\times(C\cup\{\checkmark\})
, as the
smallest relation obeying the following rules:

\displaystyle skip\xrightarrow{sk}\checkmark\hskip 28.45274ptU(\vec{n})\xrightarrow{U(\vec{n})}\checkmark\hskip 28.45274ptM(n,C_{1},C_{2})\xrightarrow{P_{0}^{n}}C_{1}\hskip 28.45274ptM(n,C_{1},C_{2})\xrightarrow{P_{1}^{n}}C_{2}

\displaystyle C_{1}\,;\,C_{2}\xrightarrow{l}C_{2}C_{1}\xrightarrow{l}\checkmark\hskip 28.45274ptC_{1}\,;\,C_{2}\xrightarrow{l^{\prime}}C^{\prime}_{1}\,;\,C_{2}C_{1}\xrightarrow{l^{\prime}}C^{\prime}_{1}

\displaystyle C_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\xrightarrow{l}C_{2}C_{1}\xrightarrow{l}\checkmark\hskip 28.45274ptC_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\xrightarrow{l^{\prime}}C^{\prime}_{1}\,||\,C_{2}C_{1}\xrightarrow{l^{\prime}}C^{\prime}_{1}\hskip 28.45274ptC_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\xrightarrow{l}C_{1}C_{2}\xrightarrow{l}\checkmark\hskip 28.45274ptC_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\xrightarrow{l^{\prime}}C_{1}\,||\,C^{\prime}_{2}C_{2}\xrightarrow{l^{\prime}}C^{\prime}_{2}

Figure 21
:
Rules of the small-step operational semantics

Define a word to be a sequence of labels:

\omega::=l^{\prime}\mid l^{\prime}:\omega

where
l^{\prime}:\omega
appends
l^{\prime}
to the beginning of
\omega
. A word can also be seen as an
element of
(L^{\prime})^{+}
,
i.e.
a possibly infinite sequence of labels without the empty sequence. Despite

(L^{\prime})^{+}
allows the possibility of having infinite words, by now we focus only on the finite words.

Define the
n
-step transition,

\xrightarrow{\omega}\subseteq C\times(L^{\prime})^{+}\times(C\cup\{\checkmark\})
, where

n
is the length of the words, as follows:

\displaystyle C\xrightarrow{l}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow C^{\prime}C\xrightarrow{l}C^{\prime}\hskip 56.9055ptC\xrightarrow{l^{\prime}:\omega^{\prime}}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow C^{\prime}C\xrightarrow{l}C^{\prime\prime}\qquad C^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{\omega^{\prime}}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow C^{\prime}

Figure 22
:
Rules of the n-step operational semantics

4.2
Constructions on Unitary Event Structures

To define the constructions on unitary event structures, we extend the definitions of sequential and
parallel composition from Section
2.2
to include the corresponding mapping of
events to unitary or projection operators. Additionally, we define the measurement composition by
making slight adjustments to the definition of non-deterministic composition provided in
Section
2.2
.

The sequential composition of two unitary event structures is defined as follows:

Definition 4.5

(Sequential composition)
.

Let
\mathrm{U}_{1}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1},\,Q_{1})
and
\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2},\,Q_{2})
be unitary
event structures. Define
\mathrm{U}_{1}\,;\,\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E,\,\leq,\,\#,\,Q)
as:

\displaystyle E=E_{1}\uplus(E_{2}\times\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\mathrm{U}_{1}))

\displaystyle\leq\ =\set{e_{1}\leq e^{\prime}_{1}\mid e\leq_{1}e^{\prime}}\cup\set{(e_{2},x)\leq(e^{\prime}_{2},x)\mid\ e_{2}\leq_{2}e^{\prime}_{2}}\cup\set{e_{1}\leq(e_{2},x)\mid e_{1}\in x}

\displaystyle\#\ =\set{e\#e^{\prime}\mid\exists(e_{1}\leq e,e^{\prime}_{1}\leq e^{\prime})\ .\ e_{1}\#_{1}e^{\prime}_{1}}\cup\set{(e_{2},x)\#(e^{\prime}_{2},x)\mid e_{2}\#_{2}e^{\prime}_{2}}

\displaystyle Q(e)=\begin{cases}Q_{1}(e)&\text{ if }e\in E_{1}\\
Q_{2}(e_{2})&\text{ if }e=(e_{2},x)\in E_{2}\times\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\mathrm{U}_{1})\end{cases}

where
E_{2}\times\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\mathrm{U}_{1})=\set{(e,x)\mid e\in E_{2},\ x\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\mathrm{U}_{1})}
.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
\mathrm{U}_{1}
and
\mathrm{U}_{2}
be unitary event structures.
\mathrm{U}_{1}\,;\,\mathrm{U}_{2}
is a unitary
event structure.

When defining the parallel composition, we must consider the restriction in Definition
4.1

requiring the operators associated with concurrent events to commute. Since in
Definition
2.16
every event in
\mathrm{E}_{1}
is concurrent with every event of

\mathrm{E}_{2}
, then when extending this definition to the quantum case, the operators associated to

\mathrm{U}_{1}=(\mathrm{E}_{1},Q_{1})
and
\mathrm{U}_{2}=(\mathrm{E}_{2},Q_{2})
must commute for every event,
i.e.

\forall e_{1}\in E_{1},e_{2}\in E_{2}.\ [Q_{1}(e_{1}),Q_{2}(e_{2})]=0
.

Definition 4.7

(Parallel composition)
.

Let
\mathrm{U}_{1}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1},\,Q_{1})
and
\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2},\,Q_{2})
be
unitary event structures, such that

\forall e_{1}\in E_{1},e_{2}\in E_{2}.\ [Q_{1}(e_{1}),Q_{2}(e_{2})]=0
. Define

\mathrm{U}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E,\,\leq,\,\#,\,Q)
as:

\displaystyle E=E_{1}\uplus E_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\ =\leq_{1}\uplus\leq_{2}

\displaystyle\#=\#_{1}\uplus\#_{2}

\displaystyle Q(e)=\begin{cases}Q_{1}(e)&\text{ if }e\in E_{1}\\
Q_{2}(e)&\text{ if }e\in E_{2}\\
\end{cases}

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
\mathrm{U}_{1}
and
\mathrm{U}_{2}
be unitary event structures.
\mathrm{U}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{U}_{2}
is a
unitary event structure.

When defining the measurement command in terms of unitary event structures, we must consider the
restriction in Definition
4.1
that requires the sum of operators associated with events in
minimal conflict to be the identity.

Definition 4.9

(Measurement composition)
.

Let
\mathrm{U}_{1}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1},\,Q_{1})
and
\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2},\,Q_{2})
be unitary
event structures. Define
M(n,\mathrm{U}_{1},\mathrm{U}_{2})=(E,\,\leq,\,\#,\,Q)
as:

\displaystyle E=\set{\tau_{0}^{n},\,\tau_{1}^{n}}\uplus E_{1}\uplus E_{2}

\displaystyle\leq=\set{\tau_{0}^{n}\leq e\mid(e=\tau_{0}^{n}\vee e\in E_{1})}\cup\set{\tau_{1}^{n}\leq e\mid(e=\tau_{1}^{n}\vee e\in E_{2})}\cup\leq_{1}\uplus\leq_{2}

\displaystyle\#=\set{e\#e^{\prime}\mid(e=\tau_{0}^{n}\vee e\in E_{1}),(e^{\prime}=\tau_{1}^{n}\vee e^{\prime}\in E_{2})}

\displaystyle\hskip 22.76228pt\cup\set{e^{\prime}\#e\mid(e=\tau_{0}^{n}\vee e\in E_{1}),(e^{\prime}=\tau_{1}^{n}\vee e^{\prime}\in E_{2})}\cup\#_{1}\uplus\#_{2}

\displaystyle Q(e)=\begin{cases}P_{0}(n)&\text{ if }e=\tau_{0}^{n}\\
P_{1}(n)&\text{ if }e=\tau_{1}^{n}\\
Q_{1}(e)&\text{ if }e\in E_{1}\\
Q_{2}(e)&\text{ if }e\in E_{2}\end{cases}

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
\mathrm{U}_{1}
and
\mathrm{U}_{2}
be unitary event structures.
M(n,\mathrm{U}_{1},\mathrm{U}_{2})
is a
unitary event structure.

Note that, in Definition
4.9
, the fact that
Q(\tau_{0}^{n})=P_{0}(n)
and

Q(\tau_{1}^{n})=P_{1}(n)
, ensures us that
Q(\tau_{0}^{n})+Q(\tau_{1}^{n})=P_{0}(n)+P_{1}(n)=Id
.

Remark 4
.

As previously stated in the operational semantics, the measurement command can be seen as

P_{0}(n)\,;\,C_{1}\,\square\,P_{1}(n)\,;\,C_{2}
. To transport such relation to the setting
of unitary event structures, we find helpful to write
M(n,\mathrm{U}_{1},\mathrm{U}_{2})
as

\mathrm{P}_{0}^{n}\,;\,\mathrm{U}_{1}\,\square\,\mathrm{P}_{1}^{n}\,;\,\mathrm{U}_{2}
, where

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{0}^{n}=(\set{\tau_{0}^{n}},\,\set{\tau_{0}^{n}\leq\tau_{0}^{n}},\,\emptyset,\,Q(\tau_{0}^{n})=P_{0}(n))

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{1}^{n}=(\set{\tau_{1}^{n}},\,\set{\tau_{1}^{n}\leq\tau_{1}^{n}},\,\emptyset,\,Q(\tau_{1}^{n})=P_{1}(n))

Note that by doing this, we are abusing notation, since
\mathrm{P}_{0}^{n}
and
\mathrm{P}_{1}^{n}
are quantum
event structures and not unitary event structures, because the quantum operators associated to the
events
\tau_{0}^{n}
and
\tau_{1}^{n}
are not unitary. However, this notational abuse allows the
measurement command to connect to the operational counterpart directly.

In Definition
4.9
, the events associated with projections include a superscript
indicating the qubit on which the projection is performed. Whenever it is clear from the context, we
will drop the superscript.

We are now ready to interpret commands of the language in Section
4.1
as unitary
event structures.

Definition 4.11
.

We interpret commands as unitary event structures as follows:

\displaystyle\llbracket skip\rrbracket=(\set{sk},\set{sk\leq sk},\emptyset,Q(sk)=Id)

\displaystyle\llbracket U(\vec{n})\rrbracket=(\set{U_{\vec{n}}},\set{U_{\vec{n}}\leq U_{\vec{n}}},\emptyset,Q(U_{\vec{n}})=U(\vec{n}))

\displaystyle\llbracket M(n,C_{1},C_{2})\rrbracket=\mathrm{P}_{0}^{n}\,;\,\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket+\mathrm{P}_{1}^{n}\,;\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket C_{1}\,;\,C_{2}\rrbracket=\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\,;\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket C_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\rrbracket=\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\,||\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket

We extend Definition
2.21
to the quantum setting. In this case, we need to ensure that
the quantum operator is preserved across unitary event structures,
i.e.
if

\mathrm{U}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{U}_{2}
then the quantum operators of the events from
\mathrm{U}_{2}
that are
also events in
\mathrm{U}_{1}
should be the same. Recall what was said regarding plain and composite
events in Section
2.2
above Definition
2.21
.

Definition 4.12
.

Let
\mathrm{U}_{1}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1},\,Q_{1})
and
\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2},\,Q_{2})
be
unitary event structures. Say
\mathrm{U}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{U}_{2}
whenever exists an injective
function
f:E_{1}\rightarrow E_{2}
such that
\forall e,e^{\prime}\in E_{1}
:

\displaystyle\pi(f(e))=\pi(e)

\displaystyle e\leq_{1}e^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow f(e)\leq_{2}f(e^{\prime})

\displaystyle e\#_{1}e^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow f(e)\#_{2}f(e^{\prime})

\displaystyle Q_{1}(e)=Q_{2}(f(e))

We say that two event structures
\mathrm{U}_{1},\mathrm{U}_{2}
are similar, denoted

\mathrm{U}_{1}\equiv\mathrm{U}_{2}
, iff
\mathrm{U}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{U}_{2}
and

\mathrm{U}_{2}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{U}_{1}
.

We now extend the removal of an initial event to the quantum setting. Intuitively, we need to
preserve the quantum operators of the events that were not removed.

Definition 4.13

(Remove initial event)
.

Let
\mathrm{U}=(E,\,\leq,\,\#,\,Q)
be a unitary event structure and
a\in\mathcal{I}(\mathrm{U})
.
Define
\mathrm{U}\backslash a=(E^{\prime},\,\leq^{\prime},\,\#^{\prime},\,Q^{\prime})
as

\displaystyle E^{\prime}=\set{e\in E\mid\neg(e\#a),\,e\neq a}

\displaystyle\leq^{\prime}\,=\,\set{e\leq e^{\prime}\mid e,e^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\#^{\prime}\,=\,\set{e\#e^{\prime}\mid e,e^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}}

\displaystyle Q^{\prime}=Q|_{E^{\prime}}

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
\mathrm{U}
be a unitary event structure and
a\in\mathcal{I}(\mathrm{U})
.
\mathrm{U}\backslash a
is a
unitary event structure.

4.3
Results

Here we present the results obtained. Similarly to the previous subsection, we will just list what
was proved. We postpone the addition of the proofs as well as the examples for some results for
future versions of the document.

For this section, we interpret
\checkmark
as the empty unitary event structure,
i.e.

\llbracket\checkmark\rrbracket=(\emptyset,\,\emptyset,\,\emptyset,\,!:\emptyset\rightarrow Op(\mathcal{H}))
.

We start by showing that the sequential, parallel, and measurement operators are monotonic with
respect to Definition
4.12
.

Lemma 4.15
.

Let
\mathrm{U}_{1}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1},\,Q_{1})
and
\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2},\,Q_{2})
be
unitary event structures. If
\mathrm{U}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{1}
and

\mathrm{U}_{2}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{2}
then

\mathrm{U}_{1}\,;\,\mathrm{U}_{2}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{1}\,;\,\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{2}
.

Lemma 4.16
.

Let
\mathrm{U}_{1}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1},\,Q_{1})
and
\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2},\,Q_{2})
be
unitary event structures. If
\mathrm{U}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{1}
and

\mathrm{U}_{2}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{2}
then

\mathrm{U}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{U}_{2}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{2}
.

Lemma 4.17
.

Let
\mathrm{U}_{1}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1},\,Q_{1})
and
\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2},\,Q_{2})
be
unitary event structures. If
\mathrm{U}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{1}
and

\mathrm{U}_{2}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{2}
then

M(n,\mathrm{U}_{1},\mathrm{U}_{2})\sqsubseteq M(n,\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{1},\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{2})
.

Next, we show how unitary event structures composed by the sequential, concurrent, and measurement
operators interact with the removal of initial events.

Lemma 4.18
.

Let
\mathrm{U}_{1}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1},\,Q_{1})
and
\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2},\,Q_{2})
be
unitary event structures. Consider
\mathrm{U}_{1}\,;\,\mathrm{U}_{2}
such that

l\in\mathcal{I}(\mathrm{U}_{1}\,;\,\mathrm{U}_{2})
. Then

(\mathrm{U}_{1}\,;\,\mathrm{U}_{2})\backslash l\equiv(\mathrm{U}_{1}\backslash l)\,;\,\mathrm{U}_{2}
.

Lemma 4.19
.

Let
\mathrm{U}_{1}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1},\,Q_{1})
and
\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2},\,Q_{2})
be
unitary event structures. Consider
M(n,\mathrm{U}_{1},\mathrm{U}_{2})
such that

l\in\mathcal{I}(M(n,\mathrm{U}_{1},\mathrm{U}_{2}))
. Then

(M(n,\mathrm{U}_{1},\mathrm{U}_{2}))\backslash l\equiv\begin{cases}\mathrm{U}_{1}&\text{ if }l=\tau_{0}^{n}\\
\mathrm{U}_{2}&\text{ if }l=\tau_{1}^{n}\end{cases}

Lemma 4.20
.

Let
\mathrm{U}_{1}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1},\,Q_{1})
and
\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2},\,Q_{2})
be
unitary event structures. Consider
\mathrm{U}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{U}_{2}
such that

l\in\mathcal{I}(\mathrm{U}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{U}_{2})
. Then

(\mathrm{U}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{U}_{2})\backslash l\equiv(\mathrm{U}_{1}\backslash l)\,||\,(\mathrm{U}_{2}\backslash l)
.

It is straightforward to see that:

Lemma 4.21
.

Let
\mathrm{U}_{1},\mathrm{U}_{2}
be unitary event structures. Then

\mathrm{U}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{U}_{2}=\mathrm{U}_{2}\,||\,\mathrm{U}_{1}
.

Proof.

It follows directly from Definition
4.7
.
∎

We now establish a relation between the operational and denotational semantics by a soundness and
adequacy theorem. The formulation of the following lemmas and theorems is equal to the ones in
Section
2.3
. We thus suggest that the reader recalls what was said about these
lemmas and theorems. It is useful to recall that
\bot=\llbracket\checkmark\rrbracket
.

Lemma 4.22

(Soundness I)
.

If
C\xrightarrow{l}C^{\prime}
then
\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket\equiv\llbracket C\rrbracket\backslash l
.

Theorem 4.23

(Soundness II)
.

If
C\xrightarrow{\omega}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow C^{\prime}
then
\exists x\in\mathcal{C}(\llbracket C\rrbracket)

such that
\emptyset\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\omega}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}\ }}x
.

Lemma 4.24

(Adequacy I)
.

Let
l\in\mathcal{I}(\llbracket C\rrbracket)
. Then
\exists C^{\prime}\in(C\cup\{\checkmark\})
s.t

C\xrightarrow{l}C^{\prime}
and
\llbracket C\rrbracket\backslash l\equiv\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket
.

Theorem 4.25

(Adequacy II)
.

If
\emptyset\neq x\in\mathcal{C}(\llbracket C\rrbracket)
s.t.

\emptyset\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\omega}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}\ }}x
then
\exists C^{\prime}
s.t.

C\xrightarrow{\omega}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow C^{\prime}
.

4.4
Unitary Event Structures with initial state

This section shows how the restrictions added to Definition
4.1
(minimal conflict being
transitive and the sum of the operators in minimal conflict, or an event itself, should be a unitary
operator), when compared to Winskel’s definition of quantum event
structures
[
19
, Definition 2]
, allow to extend a result obtained by
Winskel
[
19
, Theorem 3]
. Furthermore, in this section, we show most of the proofs after
stating a result, since these are not so evident.

According to
[
19
, Theorem 3]
, an elementary quantum event structure,
i.e.
an event
structure without conflicting events, paired with an initial state
\rho
and a valuation function
defined as
v(x)=\text{Tr}(A_{x}^{\dagger}A_{x}\rho)
, corresponds to a probabilistic event
structure. Recall that by Proposition
[
19
, Proposition 5]
it suffices to verify
Equation
1
for
y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e_{1}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ x_{1},\,\dots,\,y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e_{n}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ x_{n}
to check
if a structure is a probabilistic event structure or not. Hence, we focus only on the relation
between these events. These events are either in conflict or are concurrent. The reason why Winskel
considers elementary quantum event structures lies in the fact that in a probabilistic event
structure, the sum of the probability of events in conflict is less than or equal to one. For
example, consider two conflicting events
a
and
b
, such that
v(\{a\})=1
and
v(\{b\})=1
.
In this case, the sum condition in Definition
3.1
fails when taking
y
as the empty
set and
x_{1}
,
x_{2}
as
\set{a}
and
\set{b}
, respectively. Through some calculations, the sum
simplifies to
v(\emptyset)-(v(\set{a})+v(\set{b}))=1-(1+1)=-1
, which does not meet the
criteria of being greater than or equal to
0
.

We aim to remove the restriction of considering only elementary quantum event structures
from
[
19
, Theorem 3]
. Hence, based on the previous example, we must restrict the
probabilities associated with events in conflict. To do this, Definition
4.1
includes the
condition that “
\mathrel{\!\!\!\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}{\hbox{\kern 17.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\!\!\!\!}
is transitive”. Recall that the intuition behind this condition is
that events in minimal conflict correspond to projections that form a measurement. Since these
projections are orthogonal, they are inherently captured by conflicting events. For instance, if we
project onto
\ket{0}
, we cannot project onto
\ket{1}
. Furthermore, the probabilities associated
with projections are complementary,
i.e.
if the probability of projecting onto
\ket{0}
is
p
,
then the probability of projecting onto
\ket{1}
must be
1-p
. By applying this new information to
the previous example of the valuation of conflicting events, we have that

v(\emptyset)-(v(\set{a})+v(\set{b}))=1-(p+(1-p))=1-1=0
, which meets the criteria
of being greater than or equal to
0
. Here, the event
a
represents the projection to
\ket{0}

and the event
b
represents the projection to
\ket{1}
. Furthermore, Definition
4.1
also
demands the sum of events in minimal conflict or the event itself to be a unitary. This is useful to
ensure that we are only considering unitary operations.

To achieve our goal, the difference between our definition and Winskel’s quantum event structures
lies in the additional restrictions we impose. Therefore, we use as basis the proof outlined
in
[
19
, Theorem 3]
. This entails that we need only to consider the case in which all the
events are mapped to projections such that either all events are in conflict or there are events in
conflict. For the former, we have everything. For the latter, we need extra machinery, which we show
here.

Recall that according to
[
19
, Proposition 5]
, to show that a structure is a
probabilistic event structure, we only need to show that the condition in
Definition
3.1
holds for
y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e_{1}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ x_{1},\,\dots,\,y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e_{n}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ x_{n}
. We then
build a unitary event structure formed by the events of

y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e_{1}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ x_{1},\,\dots,\,y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e_{n}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ x_{n}
.

Definition 4.26
.

Let
\mathrm{U}=(E,\,\leq,\,\#,\,Q)
be a unitary event structure and
y\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{U})
.
Define
\tilde{\mathrm{U}}_{y}=(\tilde{E},\,\tilde{\leq},\,\tilde{\#},\,\tilde{Q})
as follows:

\displaystyle\tilde{E}=\set{e\mid y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ y\cup\set{e}}

\displaystyle\tilde{\leq}=\set{(e,e)\mid e\in\tilde{E}}

\displaystyle\tilde{\#}=\#\cap(\tilde{E}\times\tilde{E})

\displaystyle\tilde{Q}=Q|_{\tilde{E}}

Lemma 4.27
.

\tilde{\mathrm{U}}_{y}=(\tilde{E},\,\tilde{\leq},\,\tilde{\#},\,\tilde{Q})
is a unitary event
structure.

Next, we merge the conflicting events from Definition
4.26
. This gives us an empty
conflict relation, hence an elementary unitary event structure (recall that

[e]=\set{e^{\prime}\mid e=e^{\prime},\ e\mathrel{\!\!\!\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}{\hbox{\kern 17.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\!\!\!\!}e^{\prime}}
).

Definition 4.28
.

Let
\tilde{\mathrm{U}}_{y}
be a unitary event structure. Define

\hat{\mathrm{U}}=(\hat{E},\,\hat{\leq},\,\hat{\#},\,\hat{Q})
as follows:

\displaystyle\hat{E}=\set{[e]\mid e\in\tilde{\mathrm{U}}}

\displaystyle\hat{\leq}=\set{([e],[e])\mid[e]\in\hat{E}}

\displaystyle\hat{\#}=\emptyset

\displaystyle\hat{Q}([e])=\begin{cases}Q(e)&\text{ if }|[e]|=1\\
\sum_{e^{\prime}\in[e]}Q(e^{\prime})&\text{ if }|[e]|>1\end{cases}

Lemma 4.29
.

\hat{\mathrm{U}}=(\hat{E},\,\hat{\leq},\,\hat{\#},\,\hat{Q})
is a unitary event structure.

We then define a map of event structures,
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}
, between the underlying event
structures of
\tilde{\mathrm{U}}_{y}
and
\hat{\mathrm{U}}
. Note that
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}
is a total
map of event structures. Hence, by recalling Example
2.11
, for any configuration

x\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y})
we have
|x|=|proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}[x]|
. Formally, the
definition of the map of event structures is as follows:

proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}:(\tilde{E},\,\tilde{\leq},\,\tilde{\#})\rightarrow(\hat{E},\,\hat{\leq},\,\hat{\#})
, where

\displaystyle proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}:\tilde{E}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\hat{E}

\displaystyle e

\displaystyle\mapsto[e]

Lemma 4.30
.

proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}:(\tilde{E},\,\tilde{\leq},\,\tilde{\#})\rightarrow(\hat{E},\,\hat{\leq},\,\hat{\#})

is a map of events structures.

We now prove that our intuition about a feature of
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}
is true,
i.e.
that for a
given configuration
x\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y})
we have
|x|=|proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}[x]|
.

Lemma 4.31
.

Consider
\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y}
,
\hat{\mathrm{E}}
, and
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}
.
If
x\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y})
then
|proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}[x]|=|x|
.

Proof.

Let
x\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y})
. We know that
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}
is total, hence

proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}[x]=\set{proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}(e)\mid e\in x}=\set{[e]\mid e\in x}

Furthermore,
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}
is locally injective.
Hence if
e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\in x
then

proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}(e_{1}),\dots,proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}(e_{n})\in proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}[x]

Thus
|x|=|proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}[x]|
.
∎

The next lemma states that for any
\hat{x}\in\mathcal{C}(\hat{\mathrm{E}})
and

\tilde{x}\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{E}})
, the set of configurations
\tilde{x}
satisfying

proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}[\tilde{x}]=\hat{x}
consists precisely of those configurations formed by
selecting one event from each equivalence class that makes up
\hat{x}
. Furthermore, the
equivalence classes in
\hat{x}
are composed of single events or events in minimal
conflict. Consequently, multiple configurations in
\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{E}})
can be mapped to the
same configuration in
\mathcal{C}(\hat{\mathrm{E}})
.

Lemma 4.32
.

Consider
\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y}
,
\hat{\mathrm{E}}
. Let

\hat{x}=\set{[e_{1}],\dots,[e_{n}]}\in\mathcal{C}(\hat{\mathrm{E}})
and

\tilde{x}\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y})
.
Then
\set{\tilde{x}\mid proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}[\tilde{x}]=\hat{x}}=\set{\set{\tilde{e}_{1},\dots,\tilde{e}_{n}}\mid\forall i,\tilde{e}_{i}\in[e_{i}]}
.

Proof.

We have two cases:

•

\set{\tilde{x}\mid proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}[\tilde{x}]=\hat{x}}\subseteq\set{\set{\tilde{e}_{1},\dots,\tilde{e}_{n}}\mid\forall i,\tilde{e}_{i}\in[e_{i}]}

Let
\tilde{x}=\set{\tilde{e}_{1},\dots,\tilde{e}_{n}}\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y})
. By
Definition
2.9
,
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}[\tilde{x}]=\hat{x}\in\mathcal{C}(\hat{\mathrm{E}})
.
Furthermore,

proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}[\tilde{x}]=proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}(e_{1}),\dots,proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}(e_{n})=\set{[e_{1}],\dots,[e_{n}]}
.
By definition of
[e]
, we know that
\forall i\,.\,\tilde{e}_{i}\in\tilde{x}
we have

\tilde{e}_{i}\in[e_{i}]
.
Hence, we are done.

•

\set{\set{\tilde{e}_{1},\dots,\tilde{e}_{n}}\mid\forall i,\tilde{e}_{i}\in[e_{i}]}\subseteq\set{\tilde{x}\mid proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}[\tilde{x}]=\hat{x}}

Let
\set{\tilde{e}_{1},\dots,\tilde{e}_{n}}\in\set{\set{\tilde{e}_{1},\dots,\tilde{e}_{n}}\mid\forall i,\tilde{e}_{i}\in[e_{i}]}
.
We need to show that
\set{\tilde{e}_{1},\dots,\tilde{e}_{n}}\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y})
.

1.

\forall\tilde{e},\tilde{e}^{\prime}\in\set{\tilde{e}_{1},\dots,\tilde{e}_{n}}\,.\,\neg(\tilde{e}\#\tilde{e}^{\prime})

Let
\tilde{e},\tilde{e}^{\prime}\in\set{\tilde{e}_{1},\dots,\tilde{e}_{n}}
. Then we know that

\tilde{e}\in[e]
and
\tilde{e}^{\prime}\in[e^{\prime}]
. By definition of
[e]
, we have that

\neg(\tilde{e}\mathrel{\!\!\!\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}{\hbox{\kern 17.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\!\!\!\!}\tilde{e}^{\prime})
, which by Definition
4.26
means

\neg(\tilde{e}\#\tilde{e}^{\prime})
.

2.

\forall\tilde{e},\tilde{e}^{\prime}\,.\,\tilde{e}^{\prime}\tilde{\leq}\tilde{e}\wedge\tilde{e}\in\set{\tilde{e}_{1},\dots,\tilde{e}_{n}}\Rightarrow\tilde{e}^{\prime}\in\set{\tilde{e}_{1},\dots,\tilde{e}_{n}}

By Definition
4.26
, the causal relation is the equality. Hence this condition
trivially holds.

Since
\set{\tilde{e}_{1},\dots,\tilde{e}_{n}}\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y})
, it lacks to show
that

proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y}}[\set{\tilde{e}_{1},\dots,\tilde{e}_{n}}]\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y})=\hat{x}
. That comes directly from applying
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y}}
to

\set{\tilde{e}_{1},\dots,\tilde{e}_{n}}\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y})
, as follows:

proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y}}[\set{\tilde{e}_{1},\dots,\tilde{e}_{n}}]=\set{proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y}}(\tilde{e}_{1}),\dots,proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y}}(\tilde{e}_{n})}=\set{[e_{1}],\dots,[e_{n}]}=\hat{x}

∎

To better understand the mapping of event structures,
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}
,
Lemma
4.31
, and Lemma
4.32
consider the following example.

Example 4.33
.

Let us consider the following unitary event structure (where we omit the associated quantum
operators):

e_{1}
e_{2}
e_{3}
e_{4}
e_{5}
e_{6}

Figure 23
:
Unitary event structure
\mathrm{U}

From Figure
23
we can deduce all possible covering chains and consequently all
possible configurations.

\emptyset
\{e_{1}\}
\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\}
\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{4}\}
\{e_{1},e_{4},e_{6}\}
\{e_{1},e_{3},e_{5}\}
\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{5}\}
\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{4},e_{6}\}
\{e_{1},e_{3}\}
\{e_{1},e_{4}\}
e_{1}
e_{3}
e_{2}
e_{4}
e_{2}
e_{2}
e_{2}
e_{2}
e_{3}
e_{4}
e_{5}
e_{5}
e_{6}
e_{6}

Figure 24
:
Covering chains of
\mathrm{U}

Let us now focus on Figure
25
, which is a specific part of
Figure
24
.

\{e_{1}\}
\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
\{e_{1},e_{3}\}
\{e_{1},e_{4}\}
e_{3}
e_{2}
e_{4}

Figure 25
:
Fragment of the covering chains of
\mathrm{U}

From Figure
25
we can build the unitary event structure

\tilde{U}
, shown in Figure
26
.

e_{2}
e_{3}
e_{4}

Figure 26
:
Unitary event structure
\tilde{\mathrm{U}}_{y}

By applying the mapping of event structures
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}
we obtain:

\displaystyle e_{3},e_{4}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}}}{{\mapsto}}[e_{3,4}]

\displaystyle e_{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}}}{{\mapsto}}[e_{2}]

which gives the unitary event structure in Figure
27
.

[e_{2}]
[e_{3,4}]

Figure 27
:
Unitary event structure
\hat{\mathrm{U}}_{y}

Note that all the events of
\tilde{\mathrm{U}}
were mapped to
\hat{\mathrm{U}}
. Hence the map

proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}
is total. Furthermore, the events
e_{3}
and
e_{4}
are mapped to the same
event
[e_{3,4}]
.

Now we verify that the size of a configuration
\tilde{x}\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{E}})
remains
unchanged after applying the map
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}
. The set of configurations of

\tilde{\mathrm{E}}
is

\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{E}})=\{\emptyset,\{e_{2}\},\{e_{3}\},\{e_{4}\},\{e_{3},e_{2}\},\{e_{4},e_{2}\}\}

and the set of configurations of
\hat{\mathrm{E}}
is

\mathcal{C}(\hat{\mathrm{E}})=\{\emptyset,\{[e_{2}]\},\{[e_{3,4}]\},\{[e_{3,4}],[e_{2}]\}\}
. By
applying
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}
to each configuration of
\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{E}})
we have:

\displaystyle\emptyset

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}}}{{\mapsto}}\emptyset

\displaystyle\{e_{2}\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}}}{{\mapsto}}[e_{2}]

\displaystyle\{e_{3}\},\{e_{4}\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}}}{{\mapsto}}[e_{3,4}]

\displaystyle\{e_{3},e_{2}\},\{e_{4},e_{2}\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}}}{{\mapsto}}\{[e_{3,4}],[e_{2}]\}

We can then conclude that the map
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}
preserves the size of the configurations
in
\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{E}})
.

Now it lacks to see Lemma
4.32
in action. For this we focus on the case where

\{e_{3},e_{2}\},\{e_{4},e_{2}\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}}}{{\mapsto}}\{[e_{3,4}],[e_{2}]\}

First we expand the equivalence classes:
\{\{e_{3},e_{4}\},\{e_{2}\}\}
. Next, we calculate the set
of configurations formed by picking one event from each equivalence class of

\{[e_{3,4}],[e_{2}]\}
:

\set{\set{\tilde{e}_{1},\dots,\tilde{e}_{n}}\mid\forall i,\tilde{e}_{i}\in[e_{i}]}=\{\{e_{3},e_{2}\},\{e_{4},e_{2}\}\}

From this, we can see that the configurations we obtained are precisely the ones that map to

\{[e_{3,4}],[e_{2}]\}
after applying
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}
.

To prove our claim, we need one more auxiliary lemma. The idea behind the following lemma is: if we
can show that
d^{(n)}_{\tilde{v}}[\emptyset\,;\,\set{e_{1}},\dots,\set{e_{n}}]\geq 0
, where

\tilde{v}(\tilde{x})=\dfrac{v(y\cup\tilde{x})}{v(y)}
, then it follows directly that

d^{(n)}_{v}[y\,;\,y\cup\set{e_{1}},\dots,y\cup\set{e_{n}}]\geq 0
. In other words, the condition
in Definition
3.1
is satisfied.

Lemma 4.34
.

Consider
\tilde{\mathrm{U}}_{y}
together with an initial state
\rho_{y}
, such that all the events of

\tilde{\mathrm{U}}_{y}
are projections. For any
\tilde{x}\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{U}}_{y})
let

\tilde{v}(\tilde{x})=\dfrac{v(y\cup\tilde{x})}{v(y)}
. Then

d^{(n)}_{\tilde{v}}[\emptyset\,;\,\set{e_{1}},\dots,\set{e_{n}}]\geq 0
.

Proof.

To show
d^{(n)}_{\tilde{v}}[\emptyset\,;\,\set{e_{1}},\dots,\set{e_{n}}]\geq 0
, it is helpful to
consider
\hat{\mathrm{E}}
and
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}
.

Recall that
\hat{\mathrm{E}}
does not have events in conflict. Hence, by
[
19
, Corollary 3]
,
we have that
\hat{\mathrm{U}}
with
\rho_{y}
and

\hat{v}(\hat{x})=Tr(A_{\hat{x}}^{\dagger}A_{\hat{x}}\rho_{y})
is a probabilistic event
structure.

We need to show that

\hat{v}(\hat{x})=Tr(A_{\hat{x}}^{\dagger}A_{\hat{x}}\rho_{y})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{y}\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{U}})\\
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y}}[\tilde{y}]=\hat{x}\end{subarray}}\tilde{v}(\tilde{y})
.

We note that
\hat{Q}([e])=U=\sum_{\tilde{e}\in[e]}\tilde{Q}(\tilde{e})
when
|[e]|>1

and that for a configuration
\hat{x}\in\mathcal{C}(\hat{\mathrm{E}})
, the operator

A_{\hat{x}}=\hat{Q}([e_{n}])\cdots\hat{Q}([e_{1}])=\prod_{[e]\in\hat{x}}\hat{Q}([e])
.

Let us expand
\prod_{[e]\in\hat{x}}\hat{Q}([e])
:

\displaystyle\prod_{[e]\in\hat{x}}\hat{Q}([e])=

\displaystyle\prod_{[e]\in\hat{x}}\left(\sum_{\tilde{e}\in[e]}\tilde{Q}(\tilde{e})\right)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{\tilde{e}_{i}\in[e_{i}]}\tilde{Q}(\tilde{e}_{i})\right)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{e}_{1},\cdots,\tilde{e}_{n}\in\tilde{E}_{y}\\
\forall i,\tilde{e}_{i}\in[e_{i}]\end{subarray}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{Q}(\tilde{e}_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{e}_{1},\cdots,\tilde{e}_{n}\in\tilde{E}_{y}\\
\forall i,\tilde{e}_{i}\in[e_{i}]\end{subarray}}\left(\prod_{\tilde{e}\in\set{\tilde{e}_{1},\cdots,\tilde{e}_{n}}}\tilde{Q}(\tilde{e})\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(Lemma~\ref{lem:help-sum})}}}{{=}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{x}\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{U}}_{y})\\
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y}}[\tilde{x}]=\hat{x}\end{subarray}}\left(\prod_{\tilde{e}\in\tilde{x}}\tilde{Q}(\tilde{e})\right)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{x}\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{U}}_{y})\\
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y}}[\tilde{x}]=\hat{x}\end{subarray}}A_{\tilde{x}}

Then it follows directly that:

\displaystyle\hat{v}(\hat{x})

\displaystyle=Tr(A_{\hat{x}}^{\dagger}A_{\hat{x}}\rho_{y})=Tr\left(\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{y}\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{U}}_{y})\\
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y}}[\tilde{y}]=\hat{x}\end{subarray}}A_{\tilde{y}}^{\dagger}\right)\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{y}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{U}}_{y})\\
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y}}[\tilde{y}^{\prime}]=\hat{x}\end{subarray}}A_{\tilde{y}^{\prime}}\right)\rho_{y}\right)

\displaystyle=Tr\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{y}\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{U}})\\
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y}}[\tilde{y}]=\hat{x}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{y}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{U}})\\
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y}}[\tilde{y}^{\prime}]=\hat{x}\end{subarray}}A_{\tilde{y}}^{\dagger}A_{\tilde{y}^{\prime}}\rho_{y}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\star)}}{{=}}Tr\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{y}\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{U}})\\
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y}}[\tilde{y}]=\hat{x}\end{subarray}}A_{\tilde{y}}^{\dagger}A_{\tilde{y}}\rho_{y}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{y}\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{U}})\\
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y}}[\tilde{y}]=\hat{x}\end{subarray}}\left(Tr(A_{\tilde{y}}^{\dagger}A_{\tilde{y}}\rho_{y})\right)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{y}\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{U}})\\
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y}}[\tilde{y}]=\hat{x}\end{subarray}}\tilde{v}(\tilde{y})

Where in step
(\star)
we note that if
\tilde{y}\neq\tilde{y}^{\prime}
then

A_{\tilde{y}}^{\dagger}A_{\tilde{y}^{\prime}}=0
. That is straightforward to see because

proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y}}[\tilde{y}]=\hat{x}=proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y}}[\tilde{y}^{\prime}]
. Hence it
exists
\tilde{e}\in\tilde{y}
and
\tilde{e}^{\prime}\in\tilde{y}^{\prime}
such that

\tilde{Q}(\tilde{e})\cdot\tilde{Q}(\tilde{e}^{\prime})=0
. In other words,
\tilde{e}
and

\tilde{e}^{\prime}
are in conflict.

Now we are ready to show that
