d^{(n)}_{\tilde{v}}[\emptyset\,;\,\set{e_{1}},\dots,\set{e_{n}}]\geq 0
.

By
[
19
, Proposition 1]
,

\displaystyle d^{(n)}_{\tilde{v}}[\emptyset\,;\,\set{e_{1}},\dots,\set{e_{n}}]

\displaystyle=\tilde{v}(\emptyset)-\sum_{\emptyset\neq I\subseteq\set{1,\dots,n}}(-1)^{|I|+1}\tilde{v}\left(\bigcup_{i\in I}\set{e_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{I\subseteq\set{1,\dots,n}}(-1)^{|I|}\tilde{v}\left(\bigcup_{i\in I}\set{e_{i}}\right)

Now we note that it exists events that are in conflict, and since the union of events that are in
conflict do not form a configuration, we have that its valuation is zero.
We can then remove those terms from the sum.

\displaystyle\sum_{I\subseteq\set{1,\dots,n}}(-1)^{|I|}\tilde{v}\left(\bigcup_{i\in I}\set{e_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq\set{1,\dots,n}\\
\forall i,j\in I\,.\,e_{i}\text{ co }e_{j}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|I|}\tilde{v}\left(\bigcup_{i\in I}\set{e_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq\set{1,\dots,n}\\
\bigcup_{i\in I}\set{e_{i}}\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{U}})\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|I|}\tilde{v}\left(\bigcup_{i\in I}\set{e_{i}}\right)

On the other side, on
\hat{\mathrm{U}}
, we have:

\displaystyle d^{(k)}_{\hat{v}}[\emptyset\,;\,\hat{x}_{1},\dots,\hat{x}_{k}]

\displaystyle=\hat{v}(\emptyset)-\sum_{\emptyset\neq J\subseteq\set{1,\dots,k}}(-1)^{|J|+1}\hat{v}(\bigcup_{j\in J}\hat{x}_{j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{J\subseteq\set{1,\dots,k}}(-1)^{|J|}\hat{v}(\bigcup_{j\in J}\hat{x}_{j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{J\subseteq\set{1,\dots,k}}(-1)^{|J|}\hat{v}(\bigcup_{j\in J}\set{\hat{e}_{j}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\subseteq\set{1,\dots,k}\\
\tilde{y}\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{U}}_{y})\\
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y}}[\tilde{y}]=\bigcup_{j\in J}\set{\hat{e}_{j}}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|J|}\tilde{v}(\tilde{y})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\subseteq\set{1,\dots,k}\\
I\subseteq\set{1,\dots,n}\\
\bigcup_{i\in I}\set{e_{i}}\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{U}}_{y})\\
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y}}[\bigcup_{i\in I}\set{e_{i}}]=\bigcup_{j\in J}\set{\hat{e}_{j}}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|J|}\tilde{v}(\bigcup_{i\in I}\set{e_{i}})

By Lemma
4.31
we know that

|proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y}}[\bigcup_{i\in I}\set{e_{i}}]|=|\bigcup_{i\in I}\set{e_{i}}|=|I|
.
Furthermore we also know that
|\bigcup_{j\in J}\set{e_{j}}|=|J|
. Since

proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y}}[\bigcup_{i\in I}\set{e_{i}}]=\bigcup_{j\in J}\set{\hat{e}_{j}}
, again
by Lemma
4.31
we have

|proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y}}[\bigcup_{i\in I}\set{e_{i}}]|=|\bigcup_{j\in J}\set{\hat{e}_{j}}|
.
Thus
|I|=|J|
. Hence

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\subseteq\set{1,\dots,k}\\
I\subseteq\set{1,\dots,n}\\
\bigcup_{i\in I}\set{e_{i}}\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{U}}_{y})\\
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y}}[\bigcup_{i\in I}\set{e_{i}}]=\bigcup_{j\in J}\set{\hat{e}_{j}}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|J|}\tilde{v}(\bigcup_{i\in I}\set{e_{i}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\subseteq\set{1,\dots,k}\\
I\subseteq\set{1,\dots,n}\\
\bigcup_{i\in I}\set{e_{i}}\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{U}}_{y})\\
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y}}[\bigcup_{i\in I}\set{e_{i}}]=\bigcup_{j\in J}\set{\hat{e}_{j}}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|I|}\tilde{v}(\bigcup_{i\in I}\set{e_{i}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq\set{1,\dots,n}\\
\bigcup_{i\in I}\set{e_{i}}\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{U}}_{y})\end{subarray}}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\subseteq\set{1,\dots,k}\\
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}_{y}}[\bigcup_{i\in I}\set{e_{i}}]=\bigcup_{j\in J}\set{\hat{e}_{j}}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|I|}\tilde{v}(\bigcup_{i\in I}\set{e_{i}})\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\star)}}{{=}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq\set{1,\dots,n}\\
\bigcup_{i\in I}\set{e_{i}}\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{U}}_{y})\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|I|}\tilde{v}(\bigcup_{i\in I}\set{e_{i}})

\displaystyle=d^{(n)}_{\tilde{v}}[\emptyset\,;\,\tilde{x}_{1},\dots,\tilde{x}_{n}]

In step
(\star)
the sum no longer depends on
J
, hence we drop it.

We shown that

d^{(n)}_{\tilde{v}}[\emptyset\,;\,\tilde{x}_{1},\dots,\tilde{x}_{n}]=d^{(k)}_{\hat{v}}[\emptyset\,;\,\hat{x}_{1},\dots,\hat{x}_{k}]
.

Hence
d^{(n)}_{\tilde{v}}[\emptyset\,;\,\tilde{x}_{1},\dots,\tilde{x}_{n}]\geq 0
.
∎

Now we are ready to extend
[
19
, Theorem 3]
.

Proposition 4.35
.

Let
\mathrm{U}=(E,\,\leq,\,\#,\,Q)
be a unitary event structure with initial state
\rho
. For
each
x\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{U})
let
v(x)=Tr(\rho_{x})=Tr(A_{x}^{\dagger}A_{x}\rho)
. Then

\mathrm{U}=(E,\,\leq,\,\#,\,v)
is a probabilistic event structure.

Proof.

From
[
19
, Proposition 5]
we need to show
d^{(n)}_{v}[y\,;\,x_{1},\dots x_{n}]
when

y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e_{1},\dots,e_{n}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
. We then identify the following cases:

1.

v(\emptyset)=1

2.

\exists e_{i}\in e_{1},\dots,e_{n}
such that
Q(e_{i})
is a unitary

3.

\forall e_{i}\in e_{1},\dots,e_{n}
we have
Q(e_{i})
is a projection

(a)

all the events are concurrent

(b)

all events are in conflict

(c)

there are events in conflict

The proof of
1
,
2
, and
3.
(a) can be found in
[
19
, Theorem 3]
. Thus, we only
show the proof of
3.
(b) and
3.
(c).

3.
(b)

Case every event is in conflict we know that the sum of the associated quantum
operators is a unitary. Hence we are in case
2.
and consequently

d^{(n)}_{v}[y\,;\,x_{1},\dots x_{n}]=0
.

3
.(c)

Case there are events in conflict :

\displaystyle d^{(n)}_{v}[y\,;\,x_{1},\dots x_{n}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle v(y)-\sum_{\emptyset\neq I\subseteq\set{1,\dots,n}}(-1)^{|I|+1}v(\bigcup_{i\in I}x_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle v(y)-\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\emptyset\neq I\subseteq\set{1,\dots,n}\\
|I|=1\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|I|+1}v(\bigcup_{i\in I}x_{i})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\emptyset\neq I\subseteq\set{1,\dots,n}\\
|I|>1\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|I|+1}v(\bigcup_{i\in I}x_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle v(y)-\sum_{i=1}^{n}v(x_{i})-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\emptyset\neq I\subseteq\set{1,\dots,n}\\
|I|>1\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|I|+1}v(\bigcup_{i\in I}x_{i})

Focus on

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\emptyset\neq I\subseteq\set{1,\dots,n}\\
|I|>1\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|I|+1}v(\bigcup_{i\in I}x_{i})

Since
x_{i}=\set{e_{i}}\cup y
then

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\emptyset\neq I\subseteq\set{1,\dots,n}\\
|I|>1\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|I|+1}v(\bigcup_{i\in I}x_{i})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\emptyset\neq I\subseteq\set{1,\dots,n}\\
|I|>1\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|I|+1}v(\bigcup_{i\in I}\set{e_{i}}\cup y)

We know that with
|I|>1
we are not considering singletons. Hence we are either making the
union of events that are concurrent or are in conflict. W.l.o.g consider

v(\set{e_{j},e_{k}}\cup y)
with
1\leq j\neq k\leq n
. Case
e_{j}\#e_{k}
then we know that

Q(e_{j})\cdot Q(e_{k})=0=Q(e_{k})\cdot Q(e_{j})
and consequently we have

v(\set{e_{j},e_{k}}\cup y)=Tr(A_{y}^{\dagger}\cdot(Q(e_{j})\cdot Q(e_{k}))^{\dagger}\cdot Q(e_{j})\cdot Q(e_{k})\cdot A_{y}\rho)=0
. On the other side, case
e_{j}\text{ co }e_{k}
then we know that

Q(e_{j})\cdot Q(e_{k})=Q(e_{k})\cdot Q(e_{j})
and consequently
v(\set{e_{j},e_{k}}\cup y)\geq 0
.

When events are in conflict their contribution to the sum is null, hence we can discard them. As
a consequence, the sum is composed of elements that are concurrent.
Hence we have

\displaystyle v(y)-\sum_{i=1}^{n}v(x_{i})-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\emptyset\neq I\subseteq\set{1,\dots,n}\\
|I|>1\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|I|+1}v(\bigcup_{i\in I}x_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle v(y)-\sum_{i=1}^{n}v(x_{i})-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\emptyset\neq I\subseteq\set{1,\dots,n}\\
|I|>1\\
\bigcup_{i\in I}x_{i}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{U})\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|I|+1}v(\bigcup_{i\in I}x_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle v(y)-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\emptyset\neq I\subseteq\set{1,\dots,n}\\
|I|=1\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|I|+1}v(\bigcup_{i\in I}x_{i})-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\emptyset\neq I\subseteq\set{1,\dots,n}\\
|I|>1\\
\forall(i\neq j)\in I\,.\,e_{i}\text{ co }e_{j}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|I|+1}v(\bigcup_{i\in I}x_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle v(y)-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\emptyset\neq I\subseteq\set{1,\dots,n}\\
\forall(i\neq j)\in I\,.\,e_{i}\text{ co }e_{j}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|I|+1}v(\bigcup_{i\in I}x_{i})

Despite removing the valuations from ill-configurations, we are not in case
3.
(a) since there
are still events in conflict.
We thus resort to Lemma
4.34
.
Concretely:

\displaystyle d^{(n)}_{\tilde{v}}[\emptyset\,;\,\tilde{x}_{1},\dots,\tilde{x}_{n}]\geq 0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\tilde{v}(\emptyset)-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\emptyset\neq I\subseteq\set{1,\dots,n}\\
\bigcup_{i\in I}\set{e_{i}}\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{E}_{y})\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|I|+1}\tilde{v}(\bigcup_{i\in I}\set{e_{i}})\geq 0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq\set{1,\dots,n}\\
\bigcup_{i\in I}\set{e_{i}}\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{E}_{y})\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|I|}\tilde{v}(\bigcup_{i\in I}\set{e_{i}})\geq 0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq\set{1,\dots,n}\\
\bigcup_{i\in I}\set{e_{i}}\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{E}_{y})\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|I|}\dfrac{v(y\cup\bigcup_{i\in I}\set{e_{i}})}{v(y)}\geq 0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq\set{1,\dots,n}\\
\bigcup_{i\in I}\set{e_{i}}\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{E}_{y})\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|I|}v(y\cup\bigcup_{i\in I}\set{e_{i}})\geq 0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle d^{(n)}_{v}[y\,;\,x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]\geq 0

With all cases proved, we have that
\mathrm{U}=(E,\,\leq,\,\#,\,v)
is a probabilistic event
structure.
∎

Hence we proved that any unitary event structure with an initial state is a probabilistic event
structure.

We can now show soundness and adequacy having in mind initial states. The intuition behind the
n-step in Section
4
is: given a command
C
and a list of instructions, which is a
word,
a:\omega^{\prime}
we reach a command
C^{\prime}
in n-steps. If we give an initial state to

C
, the evolution of the state will correspond to the application of the word to the initial
state. We define a state to be a partial density operator,
i.e.
a density operator whose trace is
less or equal to one and denote the set of partial density operator as

\mathcal{D}_{\leq 1}(\mathcal{H})
. We now define how a word is applied to a state:

Definition 4.36
.

Let
\omega
be a word and
\rho
a partial density operator.
Define
\omega(\rho)
inductively as follows:

\omega(\rho)=\begin{cases}a\rho a^{\dagger}&\text{ if }\omega=a\\
\omega^{\prime}(a\rho a^{\dagger})&\text{ if }\omega=a:\omega^{\prime}\\
\end{cases}

Note that when applying a word
\omega
to a state
\rho
, the first action to be applied on
\rho

is the head of
\omega
.

Following Lemma
4.35
we can define a new denotational semantics for quantum
event structures who takes into consideration initial states.

Definition 4.37
.

We define
(
\llbracket-\rrbracket_{\_}:C\times\mathcal{D}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{C}^{2})\rightarrow(\mathrm{U},\,\rho,\,v)
, where
\mathrm{U}
is a unitary event structure) as follows:

\displaystyle\llbracket skip\rrbracket_{\rho}=(\llbracket skip\rrbracket,\,\rho,\,v(\set{sk})=1)

\displaystyle\llbracket U(\vec{n})\rrbracket_{\rho}=(\llbracket U(\vec{n})\rrbracket,\,\rho,\,v(\set{U_{\vec{n}}})=1)

\displaystyle\llbracket M(n,C_{1},C_{2})\rrbracket_{\rho}=(\llbracket M(n,C_{1},C_{2})\rrbracket,\,\rho,\,v)

\displaystyle\llbracket C_{1}\,;\,C_{2}\rrbracket_{\rho}=(\llbracket C_{1}\,;\,C_{2}\rrbracket,\,\rho,\,v)

\displaystyle\llbracket C_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\rrbracket_{\rho}=(\llbracket C_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\rrbracket,\,\rho,\,v)

At this point we can establish an equivalence between the semantics with or without initial state.
However we note that for the former we first need to show the equivalence without initial state.

Consider the case without initial state. We observe that both the operational and denotational
semantics presented in this section closely resemble those developed in
Section
2
. Consequently, the results obtained in Section
2.3

can be straightforwardly adapted to the quantum setting. It is worth to emphasize that removing an
initial element form
M(n,\mathrm{U}_{1},\mathrm{U}_{2})
is equal to
\mathrm{U}_{1}
or to
\mathrm{U}_{2}
if the
event removed is
\tau_{0}^{n}
or
\tau_{1}^{n}
, respectively.

To show the equivalence of the semantics with an initial state we state the following.

Theorem 4.38

(Soundness)
.

Let
\rho
be an initial state. If
C\xrightarrow{\omega}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow C^{\prime}
then

\exists x\in\mathcal{C}(\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\rho})
such that
\emptyset\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\omega}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}\ }}x
and

v(x)=\omega(\rho)
.

Theorem 4.39

(Adequacy)
.

Let
\rho
be an initial state. If
(x\neq\emptyset)\in\mathcal{C}(\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\rho})
s.t.

\emptyset\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\omega}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}\ }}x
then
\exists C^{\prime}
s.t.

C\xrightarrow{\omega}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow C^{\prime}
and
v(x)=Tr(\omega(\rho))
.

To prove the above statements we make use of Theorem
4.23
and
Theorem
4.25
, respectively. What is left to show is that
v(x)=Tr(\omega(\rho))
. However that comes freely because the operations applied on

\xrightarrow{\omega}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow
and on
\emptyset\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\omega}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ x
are the same.

Example 4.40
.

In Figure
28
, we have the labeled transition system of

H(n)\,;\,M(n,X(n),Z(n))
. This program applies first the Hadamard gate
to qubit
n
and then measures it. If the measurement was made by
P_{0}^{n}
then we apply the
X

gate to qubit
n
and we are done. On the other side, if the measurement was performed by
P_{1}^{n}

we apply the
Z
gate to qubit
n
finishing the computation. With the help of
Figure
28
, it is straightforward to see that the words that lead to a terminal
command are:
H(n)P_{0}^{n}X(n)
and
H(n)P_{1}^{n}Z(n)
. By applying each word to the state

\rho=\ket{0}\bra{0}
, we obtain the following possible final states:

(H(n)P_{0}^{n}X(n))(\ket{0}\bra{0})=\dfrac{1}{2}\ket{1}\bra{1}
and

(H(n)P_{1}^{n}Z(n))(\ket{0}\bra{0})=\dfrac{1}{2}\ket{1}\bra{1}
.

H(n)\,;\,M(n,X(n),Z(n))

Labeled transition system of

4.5
Introducing cyclic behavior

Differently from what was done in Section
2.4
and
Section
3.4
, here the cyclic behavior will not be given by recursion. Instead
it will be given by a while loop. By doing this we still manage to keep the intended philosophy in
the operational semantics, because the while loop is defined in terms of a measurement. In other
words, Section
4.1
already has all that we need to implement the while loop.

The set of commands allowed by the language are given by the following grammar:

C::=skip\mid U(\vec{n})\mid C\,;\,C\mid M(n,C_{1},C_{2})\mid C\,||\,C\mid while\ M(n,C)

where
U(\vec{n})
applies the unitary gate
U
to the qubits presented in
\vec{n}
, the parallel
composition is disjoint
4
4

4

C_{1}\,||\,C_{2}
being disjoint means that

C_{1}
and
C_{2}
do not share any qubit

M(n,C_{1},C_{2})
represents
the measurement of a qubit
n
such that if the measurement is made by
P_{0}^{n}
then we execute

C_{1}
, else if the measurement is made by
P_{1}^{n}
then we execute
C_{2}
, and

while\ M(n,C)
is a while loop that stops the computation if the measurement is made by

P_{0}^{n}
. Note that the behavior of
M(n,C_{1},C_{2})
is similar to that of a
classical if clause.

Remark 5
.

In this section, we used a while loop instead of a recursive command for cyclic behavior, unlike
Sections
2.1
and
3.1
. The reason to opt by a while loop in this
section comes from the behavior of a measurement resembling an if-then-else command. Furthermore
projections decide if the computation stops or continues, allowing us to implement the while loop
without needing a notion of state. On the other hand, implementing the while loop in
Sections
2.1
and
3.1
would require a notion of state associated with
the command, which would obliged us to change the operational semantics we have designed without
loops.

The set of qubits being used in a command
C
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\text{qVar}(skip)=\emptyset

\displaystyle\text{qVar}(U(\vec{n}))=\vec{n}

\displaystyle\text{qVar}(M(n,C_{1},C_{2}))=\set{n}\cup\text{qVar}(C_{1})\cup\text{qVar}(C_{2})

\displaystyle\text{qVar}(C_{1}\,;\,C_{2})=\text{qVar}(C_{1})\cup\text{qVar}(C_{2})

\displaystyle\text{qVar}(C_{1}\,||\,C_{2})=\text{qVar}(C_{1})\cup\text{qVar}(C_{2})

\displaystyle\text{qVar}(while\ M(n,C))=\set{n}\cup\text{qVar}(C)

We add the following rules to Figure
21
.

\displaystyle while\ M(n,C)\xrightarrow{P_{0}^{n}}\checkmark\qquad while\ M(n,C)\xrightarrow{P_{1}^{n}}C\,;\,while\ M(n,C)

Example 4.41
.

Figure
29
illustrates the behavior of a quantum toss coin, which, similarly
to Example
2.38
, produces a possibly empty sequence
H(n)P_{1}^{n}
that finishes with

P_{0}^{n}
. To understand this we observe that the initial program has two possible transitions:
(1) transits through
P_{0}^{n}
and the computation finishes; (2) transits through
P_{1}^{n}
to

H(n)\,;\,while\ M(n,H(n))
, which executes
H(n)
to transit to

while\ M(n,H(n))
, which is the same command as the initial one.

while\ M(n,H(n))

:
Fragment of the execution of
while\ M(n,H(n))

Definition 4.42
.

Let
\mathrm{U}_{1}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1},\,Q_{1})
and
\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2},\,Q_{2})
be
unitary event structures. Say
\mathrm{U}_{1}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}_{2}
if:

\displaystyle E_{1}\subseteq E_{2}

\displaystyle\forall e,e^{\prime}\ .\ e\leq_{1}e^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow e,e^{\prime}\in E_{1}\wedge e\leq_{2}e^{\prime}

\displaystyle\forall e,e^{\prime}\ .\ e\#_{1}e^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow e,e^{\prime}\in E_{1}\wedge e\#_{2}e^{\prime}

\displaystyle\forall e\in E_{1}\,.\,Q_{1}(e)=Q_{2}(e)

The following lemmas confirm that Definition
4.42
is a partial order and that
it has a least element.

Lemma 4.43
.

\trianglelefteq
is a partial order.

Lemma 4.44
.

Define
\bot=(\emptyset,\,\emptyset,\,\emptyset,\,!:\emptyset\rightarrow Op(\mathcal{H}))
.

\bot
is the least element of
\trianglelefteq
.

We now extend Definition
2.42
to the quantum setting.

Definition 4.45
.

Let
\mathrm{U}_{1}\trianglelefteq\dots\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}_{n}\trianglelefteq\dots
be a

\omega
-chain. Let
\mathrm{U}^{\omega}=(E^{\omega},\,\leq^{\omega},\,\#^{\omega},\,Q^{\omega})
be its
least upper bound where:

•

E^{\omega}=\cup_{n\in\omega}E_{n}

•

\leq^{\omega}=\cup_{n\in\omega}\leq_{n}

•

\#^{\omega}=\cup_{n\in\omega}\#_{n}

•

Q^{\omega}(e)\Leftrightarrow\exists n\in\omega\,.\,e\in E_{n}
and
Q_{n}(e)=Q^{\omega}(e)

We then show that the structure in Definition
4.45
is a unitary event structure and a
least upper bound in a chain of unitary event structures.

Lemma 4.46
.

\mathrm{U}^{\omega}
is a unitary event structure.

Lemma 4.47
.

Let
\mathrm{U}_{1}\trianglelefteq\dots\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}_{n}\trianglelefteq\dots
be a

\omega
-chain. Then
\mathrm{U}^{\omega}
is its least upper bound.

We have that sequential, concurrent, and measurement composition are monotone and continuous with
respect to Definition
4.42
. Recall that the monotone and continuous definitions
are given by Definition
2.45
.

Lemma 4.48
.

Let
\mathrm{U},\mathrm{U}_{1},\mathrm{U}_{2}
be unitary event structures. If

\mathrm{U}_{1}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}_{2}
then

\mathrm{U}\,;\,\mathrm{U}_{1}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}\,;\,\mathrm{U}_{2}
.

Lemma 4.49
.

Let
\mathrm{U}_{1},\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{1},\mathrm{U}_{2},\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{2}
be unitary event structures. If

\mathrm{U}_{1}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{1}
and
\mathrm{U}_{2}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{2}
then

\mathrm{U}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{U}_{2}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{2}
.

Lemma 4.50
.

Let
\mathrm{U}_{1},\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{1},\mathrm{U}_{2},\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{2}
be unitary event structures. If

\mathrm{U}_{1}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{1}
and
\mathrm{U}_{2}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{2}
then

M(n,\mathrm{U}_{1},\mathrm{U}_{2})\trianglelefteq M(n,\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{1},\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{2})
.

Lemma 4.51
.

\bigsqcup_{m}(\mathrm{U}\,;\,\mathrm{U}_{m})=\mathrm{U}\,;\,\bigsqcup_{m}\mathrm{U}_{m}
.

Lemma 4.52
.

\bigsqcup_{n,m}(\mathrm{U}_{n}\,||\,\mathrm{U}_{m})=\bigsqcup_{n}\mathrm{U}_{n}\,||\,\bigsqcup_{m}\mathrm{U}_{m}
.

Lemma 4.53
.

\bigsqcup_{n,m}(M(q,\mathrm{U}_{n},\mathrm{U}_{m}))=M(q,\bigsqcup_{n}\mathrm{U}_{n},\bigsqcup_{m}\mathrm{U}_{m})
.

When adapting Lemma
2.49
(helps to show that operators defined in event structures are
continuous) and Lemma
2.53
(a version of the Kleene fixed-point theorem for the case
of event structures) to the quantum realm, we notice that the proofs we need to do are analogous to
the ones done in the non-deterministic case. Hence, we omit their formulation here.

We now extend Definition
4.11
with the while command and let
\mathbb{U}
denote the
class of unitary event structures.

Definition 4.54
.

We interpret commands as unitary event structures as follows:

\displaystyle\llbracket skip\rrbracket=(\set{sk},\set{sk\leq sk},\emptyset,Q(sk)=Id)

\displaystyle\llbracket U_{\vec{n}}\rrbracket=(\set{U_{\vec{n}}},\set{U_{\vec{n}}\leq U_{\vec{n}}},\emptyset,Q(U_{\vec{n}})=U(\vec{n}))

\displaystyle\llbracket M(n,C_{1},C_{2})\rrbracket=\mathrm{P}_{0}^{n}\,;\,\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket+\mathrm{P}_{1}^{n}\,;\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket C_{1}\,;\,C_{2}\rrbracket=\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\,;\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket C_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\rrbracket=\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\,||\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket while\ M(n,C)\rrbracket=fix(\Gamma^{n})

where
\Gamma^{n}:\mathrm{U}\rightarrow\mathrm{U}
is given by

\Gamma^{n}(\mathrm{U})=\mathrm{P}_{0}^{n}+\mathrm{P}_{1}^{n}\,;\,\mathrm{U}
.

Furthermore, note that

\llbracket while\ M(n,C)\rrbracket=M(n,\checkmark,\llbracket C\,;\,while\ M(n,C)\rrbracket)=\mathrm{P}_{0}^{n}+\mathrm{P}_{1}^{n}\,;\,\llbracket C\,;\,while\ M(n,C)\rrbracket
and
\bot=\llbracket\checkmark\rrbracket
. These facts
will be useful when showing the equivalence between the semantics.

We note that
\Gamma^{n}
is continuous because it is composed of continuous functions.

To show the equivalence between the operational and the denotational semantics, we reuse what was
done in Section
4.3
. The only lemmas in which we need to add the proof for the
recursion case are the following:

Lemma 4.55

(Soundness I)
.

If
C\xrightarrow{l}C^{\prime}
then
\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket\equiv\llbracket C\rrbracket\backslash l
.

Lemma 4.56

(Adequacy I)
.

Let
l\in\mathcal{I}(\llbracket C\rrbracket)
. Then
\exists C^{\prime}\in(C\cup\{\checkmark\})
s.t

C\xrightarrow{l}C^{\prime}
and
\llbracket C\rrbracket\backslash l\equiv\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket
.

Similarly to what was done in Section
4.3
, we can consider the equivalence
between semantics with an initial state. However, doing it is very similar to what we already have,
hence we postpone it.

Example 4.57
.

The unitary event structure in Example
4.4
corresponds to the interpretation of the
command in Example
4.40
.

To see the equivalence between both semantics, recall the maximal configurations in
Example
4.4
and the words used in Example
4.40
. It is trivial to see that
for each word we have a corresponding covering chain, and vice-versa.

It lacks to verify the probability when an initial state is given. Consider that the initial
state is
\rho=\ket{0}\bra{0}
. Applying the word
H(n)P_{0}^{n}X(n)
to
\rho
yields a
probability of
0.5
, which matches the probability of the respective covering chain. Similarly,
when we apply the word
H(n)P_{1}^{n}Z(n)
to
\rho
, we obtain a probability of
0.5
, once again
matching the probability of the respective covering chain.

Conversely, if we obtained the probability from the trace of
A_{x}\rho
, where
x
is a
configuration from a covering chain, we observe that applying the respective word to
\rho
gives
the same probability. Concretely, the covering chain of
\set{H_{1},\tau_{0}^{1},X_{1}}
is

\emptyset\stackrel{{\scriptstyle H_{1}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ \set{H_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau_{0}^{1}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ \set{H_{1},\tau_{0}^{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle X_{1}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ \set{H_{1},\tau_{0}^{1},X_{1}}
. The associated operator
A_{x}
is
X(1)P_{0}^{1}H(1)
. By applying
A_{x}
to
\rho

we obtain the state
\ket{1}\bra{1}
with probability
0.5
, which corresponds to the probability
of applying the respective word to
\rho
.

Example 4.58
.

Figure
30
shows the event structure corresponding to the interpretation of

\llbracket H(n)\,;\,M(n,skip,X(n))\rrbracket
. The set of configurations is

\set{\emptyset,\set{H_{n}},\set{H_{n},\tau_{0}^{n}},\set{H_{n},\tau_{1}^{n}},\set{H_{n},\tau_{0}^{n},sk},\set{H_{n},\tau_{1}^{n},X_{n}}}
.

To see the equivalence between both semantics through an example, we first derive the words that
can be formed by the n-step in Example
4.40
:
H(n)
,
H(n)P_{0}^{n}
,
H(n)P_{1}^{n}
,

H(n)P_{0}^{n}sk
and
H(n)P_{1}^{n}X(n)
.

Each word corresponds to a covering chain, which represents a configuration. For example the words

H(n)P_{0}^{n}sk
and
H(n)P_{1}^{n}X(n)
correspond to the covering chains

\emptyset\stackrel{{\scriptstyle H_{n}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ \set{H_{n}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau_{0}^{n}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ \set{H_{n},\tau_{0}^{n}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle sk}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ \set{H_{n},\tau_{0}^{n},sk}=x_{1}
and

\emptyset\stackrel{{\scriptstyle H_{n}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ \set{H_{n}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau_{1}^{n}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ \set{H_{n},\tau_{1}^{n}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle X_{n}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ \set{H_{n},\tau_{1}^{n},X_{n}}=x_{2}
, respectively.

Furthermore, given as initial state
\rho=\ket{0}\bra{0}
, we have the following probabilities:

v(x_{1})=0.5
and
v(x_{2})=0.5
, which correspond to the probabilities obtained by respectively
applying the words
H(n)P_{0}^{n}sk
and
H(n)P_{1}^{n}X(n)
to the same state, as shown in
Example
4.40
.

H_{n}
\tau_{0}^{n}
\tau_{1}^{n}
sk
X_{n}

\displaystyle Q(H_{n})=H(n),

\displaystyle Q(\tau_{0}^{1})=P_{0}^{n},\ Q(\tau_{1}^{1})=P_{1}^{n},

\displaystyle Q(sk)=Id(n),\ Q(X_{n})=X(n)

Figure 30
:
Event structure of
\llbracket H(n)\,;\,M(n,skip,X(n))\rrbracket

5
Related Work

Most work on event structures extend them to different computational effects and when they give
denotational semantics for a language, most of the languages include notions of communication, which
are absent in the languages we consider.

In the classical setting, Winskel used event structures to give denotational semantics to
CCS
[
16
,
18
]
. In the probabilistic setting, Varacca and Yoshida used a
probabilistic version of event structures
[
14
]
to interpret a probabilistic

\pi
-calculus
[
13
]
. Marc de Visme later adapted Winskel’s probabilistic event
structures
[
19
]
, equivalent to Varacca’s definition, to furnish a probabilistic
CCS
[
2
]
with a denotational semantics. In the quantum setting event structures have only
been used as the backbone for game semantics
[
5
]
.

A closer approach to ours is found in Castellan’s work
[
3
]
, where event structures
interpret a simple imperative and concurrent language in the context of weak memory models. His goal
was to capture execution paths generated by compilers during code optimization, missed by
interleaving semantics. Interestingly, his definition of sequential and parallel composition are
similar to ours.

6
Conclusion

In this paper, we discussed how Winskel’s event structures can be tamed as a model of computation
for representing sequences of actions, with causal and conflicting relationships, and even refined
Winskel’s notion of quantum event structure to better match the probabilistic ones. We show how
Winskel’s event structures support non-deterministic, probabilistic and quantum effects.

Proofs of Section
2

Proof of Lemma
2.15

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}_{1}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1})

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}_{2}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2})

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}_{1}\,;\,\mathrm{E}_{2}=(E,\,\leq,\,\#)

We need to show that
\leq
is a partial order and that
\#
is symmetric and irreflexive.

•

\leq
is a partial order:

–

Reflexivity (
e\leq e
): we have two cases

1.

Case
e\in E_{1}
. This entails
e\leq_{1}e
. Since
\leq_{1}
is a partial order we are
done.

2.

Case
(e,x)\in E_{2}\times\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\mathrm{E}_{1})
. This entails
e\leq_{2}e
. Since

\leq_{2}
is a partial order we are done.

–

Transitivity (
e\leq e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime}\leq e^{\prime\prime}
then
e\leq e^{\prime\prime}
): if
e,e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime}\in E_{1}
or

e,e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime}\in E_{2}
, we are done since
\leq_{1}
and
\leq_{2}
are partial orders. We then have
two more cases:

1.

Case
e\leq(e^{\prime},x)
and
(e^{\prime},x)\leq(e^{\prime\prime},x)
. From
e\leq(e^{\prime},x)
we have that

e\in x
. From
(e^{\prime},x)\leq(e^{\prime\prime},x)
we know that
e^{\prime}\leq_{2}e^{\prime\prime}
and since
x
is the
same for both events, ten
e\in x
. Thus
e\leq(e^{\prime\prime},x)
.

2.

Case
e\leq e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime}\leq(e^{\prime\prime},x)
. From
e\leq e^{\prime}
we know that
e^{\prime}\in x
. From

e\leq e^{\prime}
we know that
e\leq_{1}e^{\prime}
. Since
x
is a maximal configuration in
\mathrm{E}_{1}

and
e\leq_{1}e^{\prime}
then
e\in x
. Thus
e\leq(e^{\prime\prime},x)

–

Antisymmetry (
e\leq e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime}\leq e
then
e^{\prime}=e
): if
e,e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime}\in E_{1}
or

e,e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime}\in E_{2}
, we are done since
\leq_{1}
and
\leq_{2}
are partial orders. Since it is
not possible to have
(e^{\prime},x)\leq e
we are done.

Hence
\leq
is a partial order.

•

\#
is symmetric and irreflexive:

–

Symmetric (if
e\#e^{\prime}
then
e^{\prime}\#e
): we have two cases:

1.

(e_{2},x)\#(e^{\prime}_{2},x)
then
(e^{\prime}_{2},x)\#(e_{2},x)
. From
(e_{2},x)\#(e^{\prime}_{2},x)
we
have that
e_{2}\#_{2}e^{\prime}_{2}
, which is symmetric since
\#_{2}
is a partial order. Hence

e^{\prime}_{2}\#_{e}e_{2}
and
(e^{\prime}_{2},x)\#(e_{2},x)
.

2.

e\#e^{\prime}
then
e^{\prime}\#e
.
e\#e^{\prime}
entails the existence of
e_{1}\leq e
and

e^{\prime}_{1}\leq e^{\prime}
such that
e_{1}\#_{1}e^{\prime}_{1}
. Since
\#_{1}
is a partial order, it is
symmetric. then
e^{\prime}_{1}\#_{1}e_{1}
. Furthermore, since
e_{1}\leq e
and
e^{\prime}_{1}\leq e^{\prime}
then

e^{\prime}\#e
.

–

Irreflexive (
\neg(e\#e)
): we show this by showing that the elements of
\#
are of the
form
e\#e^{\prime}
with
e\neq e^{\prime}
. We then have four cases:

1.

Case
e,e^{\prime}\in E_{1}
. Then we have
e_{1}\leq e
,
e^{\prime}_{1}\leq e^{\prime}
, and
e_{1}\#_{1}e^{\prime}_{1}
.
Furthermore, we have
e_{1}\leq_{1}e
and
e^{\prime}_{1}\leq_{1}e^{\prime}
. Since
e_{1}\#_{1}e^{\prime}_{1}
and

e_{1}\leq_{1}e
we have
e^{\prime}_{1}\#_{1}e
. Since
e^{\prime}_{1}\#_{1}e
and
e^{\prime}_{1}\leq_{1}e^{\prime}
we have

e\#_{1}e^{\prime}
. By irreflexivity of
\#_{1}
we have that
e\neq e^{\prime}
.

2.

Case
(e,x),(e^{\prime},x^{\prime})\in E_{2}\times\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\mathrm{E}_{1})
. If
x\neq x^{\prime}
then it is clear
that
(e,x)\neq(e^{\prime},x^{\prime})
. If
x=x^{\prime}
then we have
e_{1}\leq(e,x)
and
e^{\prime}_{1}\leq(e^{\prime},x)
,
which entails that
e_{1},e^{\prime}_{1}\in x
. However,
e_{1}\leq_{1}e^{\prime}_{1}
, hence
e_{1},e^{\prime}_{1}\not\in x
.
Thus we have a contradiction, and consequently
(e,x)\neq(e^{\prime},x)
.

3.

Case
e\in E_{1}
and
e^{\prime}\in E_{2}\times\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\mathrm{E}_{1})
. They are clearly different.

4.

Case
e\in E_{2}\times\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\mathrm{E}_{1})
and
e^{\prime}\in E_{1}
. They are clearly different.

Hence
\#
is symmetric and irreflexive.

We need to show that
\forall e,e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime}\in E
:

1.

\set{e^{\prime}\mid e^{\prime}\leq e}
is finite

(a)

Case
e\in E_{1}
we are done.

(b)

Case
e\in E_{2}\times\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\mathrm{E}_{1})
. Then
e=(e_{2},x_{1})
with
e_{2}\in E_{2}
and

x_{1}\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\mathrm{E}_{1})
. We know that

\set{e^{\prime}\mid e^{\prime}\leq(e_{2},x_{1})}=\set{(e^{\prime}_{2},x_{1})\mid(e^{\prime}_{2},x_{1})\leq(e_{2},x_{1})}\cup\set{e_{1}\mid e_{1}\leq(e_{2},x_{1}),\,e_{1}\in x_{1}}
. Both sets are finite because
\mathrm{E}_{2}
is
an event structure and
x_{1}
is finite, respectively. Since both sets are finite and the union
of finite sets is finite, then
\set{e^{\prime}\mid e^{\prime}\leq(e_{2},x_{1})}
is finite.

2.

e\#e^{\prime}\leq e^{\prime\prime}\Rightarrow e\#e^{\prime\prime}

(a)

Case
e,e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime}\in E_{1}
or
a,a^{\prime},e,e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime}\in E_{2}\times\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\mathrm{E}_{1})
we are
done.

(b)

Case
e,e^{\prime}\in E_{1}
and
e^{\prime\prime}\in E_{2}\times\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\mathrm{E}_{1})
. We want to show that

e\#e^{\prime\prime}
. Hence we have to show that

\exists(e_{1}\leq e,\ e^{\prime}_{1}\leq e^{\prime})\ .\ e_{1}\#_{1}e^{\prime}_{1}
. Since
e,e^{\prime}\in E_{1}
then

e_{1},e^{\prime}_{1}\in E_{1}
. Let
e_{1}=e
and
e^{\prime}_{1}=e^{\prime}
. Hence we have

e_{1}\leq e\Leftrightarrow e\leq e\Leftrightarrow e\leq_{1}e
. By the initial assumption,

e^{\prime}\leq e^{\prime\prime}\Leftrightarrow e^{\prime}_{1}\leq e^{\prime\prime}
. It lacks to show that
e_{1}\#_{1}e^{\prime}_{1}
. That
follows directly from the initial assumption

e\#e^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow e\#_{1}e^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow e_{1}\#_{1}e^{\prime}_{1}
.

∎

Proof of Lemma
2.17

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}_{1}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1})

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}_{2}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2})

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{E}_{2}=(E,\,\leq,\,\#)

We need to show that
\leq
is a partial order and that
\#
is symmetric and irreflexive.

•

\leq
is a partial order:

–

Reflexivity (
e\leq e
): we have two cases:
e_{1}\leq_{1}e^{\prime}_{1}
or
e_{2}\leq_{2}e^{\prime}_{2}
. Since

\leq_{1}
and
\leq_{2}
are partial orders, we are done.

–

Transitivity (
e\leq e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime}\leq e^{\prime\prime}
then
e\leq e^{\prime\prime}
): we have two cases:
(
e\leq_{1}e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime}\leq_{1}e^{\prime\prime}
) or (
e\leq_{2}e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime}\leq_{2}e^{\prime\prime}
). Since
\leq_{1}
and

\leq_{2}
are partial orders, we have
e\leq_{1}e^{\prime\prime}
or
e\leq_{2}e^{\prime\prime}
. Hence
e\leq e^{\prime\prime}
.

–

Antisymmetry (
e\leq e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime}\leq e
then
e^{\prime}=e
): we have two cases: (
e\leq_{1}e^{\prime}

and
e^{\prime}\leq_{1}e
) or (
e\leq_{2}e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime}\leq_{2}e
). Since
\leq_{1}
and
\leq_{2}
are
partial orders, we have
e=e^{\prime}
.

Hence
\leq
is a partial order.

•

\#
is symmetric and irreflexive:

–

Symmetric (if
e\#e^{\prime}
then
e^{\prime}\#e
): we have either
e\#_{1}e^{\prime}
or
e\#_{2}e^{\prime}
. Since

\#_{1}
and
\#_{2}
are symmetric then we have
e^{\prime}\#_{1}e
or
e^{\prime}\#_{2}e
. Thus
e^{\prime}\#e
.

–

Irreflexive (
\neg(e\#e)
): We have either
\neg(e\#_{1}e)
or
\neg(e\#_{2}e)
. Since

\#_{1}
and
\#_{2}
are irreflexive then
\neg(e\#e)
.

Hence
\#
is symmetric and irreflexive.

We need to show that
\forall e,e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime}\in E
:

1.

\set{e^{\prime}\mid e^{\prime}\leq e}
is finite

(a)

Case
e\in E_{1}
then
\set{e^{\prime}\mid e^{\prime}\leq e}={e^{\prime}\mid e^{\prime}\leq_{1}e}
, which is finite
since
\mathrm{E}_{1}
is a event structure.

(b)

Case
e\in E_{2}
then
\set{e^{\prime}\mid e^{\prime}\leq e}={e^{\prime}\mid e^{\prime}\leq_{2}e}
, which is finite
since
\mathrm{E}_{2}
is a event structure.

2.

e\#e^{\prime}\leq e^{\prime\prime}\Rightarrow e\#e^{\prime\prime}

We have two cases:
e,e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime}\in E_{1}
or
e,e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime}\in E_{2}
. In both cases this holds
because
\mathrm{E}_{1}
and
\mathrm{E}_{2}
are event structures.

∎

Proof of Lemma
2.19

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}_{1}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1})

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}_{2}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2})

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}_{1}\,\square\,\mathrm{E}_{2}=(E,\,\leq,\,\#)

We need to show that
\leq
is a partial order and that
\#
is symmetric and irreflexive.

•

\leq
is a partial order:

–

Reflexivity (
e\leq e
): we have two cases:
e_{1}\leq_{1}e^{\prime}_{1}
or
e_{2}\leq_{2}e^{\prime}_{2}
. Since

\leq_{1}
and
\leq_{2}
are partial orders, we are done.

–

Transitivity (
e\leq e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime}\leq e^{\prime\prime}
then
e\leq e^{\prime\prime}
): we have two cases:
(
e\leq_{1}e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime}\leq_{1}e^{\prime\prime}
) or (
e\leq_{2}e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime}\leq_{2}e^{\prime\prime}
). Since
\leq_{1}
and

\leq_{2}
are partial orders, we have
e\leq_{1}e^{\prime\prime}
or
e\leq_{2}e^{\prime\prime}
. Hence
e\leq e^{\prime\prime}
.

–

Antisymmetry (
e\leq e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime}\leq e
then
e^{\prime}=e
): we have two cases: (
e\leq_{1}e^{\prime}

and
e^{\prime}\leq_{1}e
) or (
e\leq_{2}e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime}\leq_{2}e
). Since
\leq_{1}
and
\leq_{2}
are
partial orders, we have
e=e^{\prime}
.

Hence
\leq
is a partial order.

•

\#
is symmetric and irreflexive:

–

Symmetric (if
e\#e^{\prime}
then
e^{\prime}\#e
): we have the following cases

1.

if
e\#_{1}e^{\prime}
or
e\#_{2}e^{\prime}
. Since
\#_{1}
and
\#_{2}
are symmetric then we have

e^{\prime}\#_{1}e
or
e^{\prime}\#_{2}e
. Thus
e^{\prime}\#e
.

2.

if
e\in E_{1}
and
e^{\prime}\in E_{2}
, or vice-versa, then by Definition
2.18
we
also have
e^{\prime}\leq e
.

–

Irreflexive (
\neg(e\#e)
): We have either
\neg(e\#_{1}e)
or
\neg(e\#_{2}e)
. Since

\#_{1}
and
\#_{2}
are irreflexive then
\neg(e\#e)
. There are no more cases since either

e\in E_{1}
or
e\in E_{2}
.

Hence
\#
is symmetric and irreflexive.

We need to show that
\forall e,e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime}\in E
:

1.

\set{e^{\prime}\mid e^{\prime}\leq e}
is finite

(a)

Case
e\in E_{1}
then
\set{e^{\prime}\mid e^{\prime}\leq e}={e^{\prime}\mid e^{\prime}\leq_{1}e}
, which is finite
since
\mathrm{E}_{1}
is an event structure.

(b)

Case
e\in E_{2}
then
\set{e^{\prime}\mid e^{\prime}\leq e}={e^{\prime}\mid e^{\prime}\leq_{2}e}
, which is finite
since
\mathrm{E}_{2}
is an event structure.

2.

e\#e^{\prime}\leq e^{\prime\prime}\Rightarrow e\#e^{\prime\prime}

We have two cases:
e,e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime}\in E_{1}
or
e,e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime}\in E_{2}
. In both cases this holds
because
\mathrm{E}_{1}
and
\mathrm{E}_{2}
are event structures.
