\exists y\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\mathrm{P}),\,y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{P}_{1})\,.\,x=y\cup(y^{\prime}\times\{y\})

Then we have
v^{1}(x)=v(y)\cdot v_{1}(y^{\prime})
. Since
\mathrm{P}_{1}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{P}_{2}
,

\forall y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{P}_{1})\,.\,v_{1}(y^{\prime})=v_{2}(y^{\prime})
. Then

v(y)\cdot v_{1}(y^{\prime})=v(y)\cdot v_{2}(y^{\prime})=v^{2}(x)
. Hence
v^{1}(x)=v^{2}(x)
.

∎

Proof of Lemma
3.39

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{1}=(\mathrm{E}_{1},v_{1})

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{2}=(\mathrm{E}_{2},v_{2})

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{1}=(\mathrm{E}^{\prime}_{1},v^{\prime}_{1})

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{2}=(\mathrm{E}^{\prime}_{2},v^{\prime}_{2})

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{P}_{2}=(\mathrm{E},v)

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{2}=(\mathrm{E}^{\prime},v^{\prime})

We want to show
\forall x\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{P}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{P}_{2})\,.\,v(x)=v^{\prime}(x)
.
Due to Lemma
2.47
we only focus on the valuations.

Let
x\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{P}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{P}_{2})\,.\,v(x)=v_{1}(x\cap E_{1})\cdot v_{2}(x\cap E_{2})
,
such that
x_{1}=x\cap E_{1}
and
x_{2}=x\cap E_{2}
. Since
\mathrm{P}_{1}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{1}

and
\mathrm{P}_{2}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{2}
then

\forall x_{1}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{P}_{1})\,.\,v_{1}(x_{1})=v^{\prime}_{1}(x_{1})
and

\forall x_{2}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{P}_{2})\,.\,v_{2}(x_{2})=v^{\prime}_{1}(x_{2})
, respectively. Hence

v(x)=v_{1}(x_{1})\cdot v_{2}(x_{2})=v^{\prime}_{1}(x_{1})\cdot v^{\prime}_{2}(x_{2})=v^{\prime}(x)
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.40

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{1}=(\mathrm{E}_{1},\,v_{1})

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{1}=(\mathrm{E}^{\prime}_{1},\,v^{\prime}_{1})

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{2}=(\mathrm{E}_{2},\,v_{2})

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{2}=(\mathrm{E}^{\prime}_{2},\,v^{\prime}_{2})

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{1}\,+_{p}\,\mathrm{P}_{2}=(\mathrm{E},\,v)

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{1}\,+_{p}\,\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{2}=(\mathrm{E}^{\prime},\,v^{\prime})

The conditions to check are:

1.

E\subseteq E^{\prime}

2.

\forall e,e^{\prime}\,.\,e\leq e^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow e,e^{\prime}\in E\wedge e\leq^{\prime}e^{\prime}

3.

\forall e,e^{\prime}\,.\,e\#e^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow e,e^{\prime}\in E\wedge e\#^{\prime}e^{\prime}

4.

\forall x\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{P}_{1}\,+_{p}\,\mathrm{P}_{2})\,.\,v(x)\geq v^{\prime}(y)

The first three conditions follow directly from Definition
3.7
. Hence we focus on
the last one.

Let
x\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{P}_{1}\,+_{p}\,\mathrm{P}_{2})
. We have two cases:

1.

x\backslash\tau\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{P}_{1})

It follows directly that
v(x)=v^{\prime}(x)
, since
\mathrm{P}_{1}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{1}
and

v(x)=p\cdot v_{1}(x\backslash\tau)=p\cdot v^{\prime}_{1}(x\backslash\tau)=v^{\prime}(x)
.

2.

x\backslash\tau\in\mathcal{C}(\mathrm{P}_{2})

It follows directly that
v(x)=v^{\prime}(x)
, since
\mathrm{P}_{2}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{2}
and

v(x)=(1-p)\cdot v_{2}(x\backslash\tau)=(1-p)\cdot v^{\prime}_{2}(y\backslash\tau)=v^{\prime}(x)
.

∎

Proof of Lemma
3.41

Proof.

By Lemma
3.38
, the sequential composition is monotone at right. Furthermore,
showing that each event of
\mathrm{P}\,;\,\bigsqcup_{m}\mathrm{P}_{m}
is an event of

\bigsqcup_{m}(\mathrm{P}\,;\,\mathrm{P}_{m})
is already done in Lemma
2.50
. By
Lemma
2.49
we are done.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.42

Proof.

By Lemma
3.39
, the parallel composition is monotone at right.
Furthermore, showing that each event of
\bigsqcup_{n}\mathrm{P}_{n}\,||\,\bigsqcup_{m}\mathrm{P}_{m}
is an
event of
\bigsqcup_{n,m}(\mathrm{P}_{n}\,||\,\mathrm{P}_{m})
is already done in
Lemma
2.51
. By Lemma
2.49
we are done.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.43

Proof.

By Lemma
3.40
we know that the probabilistic choice is monotone. It lacks
to show that each event of
\bigsqcup_{n}\mathrm{P}_{n}\,+_{p}\,\bigsqcup_{m}\mathrm{P}_{m}
is an event of

\bigsqcup_{n,m}(\mathrm{P}_{n}\,+_{p}\,\mathrm{P}_{m})
.

Let
\mathrm{P}_{1}\trianglelefteq\dots\trianglelefteq\mathrm{P}_{n}\trianglelefteq\dots
and

\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{1}\trianglelefteq\dots\trianglelefteq\mathrm{P}^{\prime}_{m}\trianglelefteq\dots
be

\omega
-chains with least upper bound
\bigsqcup_{n}\mathrm{P}_{n}
and
\bigsqcup_{m}\mathrm{P}_{m}
,
respectively. Let
e
be an event of
\bigsqcup_{n}\mathrm{P}_{n}\,+_{p}\,\bigsqcup_{m}\mathrm{P}_{m}
.
By Definition
3.7
we have three cases:

1.

e=\tau

It follows directly from Definition
3.7
that
\tau
is an event of

\mathrm{P}_{n}\,+_{p}\,\mathrm{P}_{m}
. Consequently it is an event of

\bigsqcup_{n,m}(\mathrm{P}_{n}\,+_{p}\,\mathrm{P}_{m})
.

2.

e
is an event of
\bigsqcup_{n}\mathrm{P}_{n}

By Definition
3.35
,
\exists n\in\omega\,.\,e
is an event of
\mathrm{P}_{n}
. By
Definition
3.7
,
e
is an event of
\mathrm{P}_{n}\,+_{p}\,\mathrm{P}_{m}
and
consequently it is an event of
\bigsqcup_{n,m}(\mathrm{P}_{n}\,+_{p}\,\mathrm{P}_{m})
.

3.

e
is an event of
\bigsqcup_{m}\mathrm{P}_{m}

Similar to the previous point.

By Lemma
2.49
we are done.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.45

Proof.

We only do for the probabilistic choice, since for the remaining cases the prove is the same as in
Lemma
2.55
.

\displaystyle\Gamma^{C_{1}\,+_{p}\,C_{2},\gamma}(\bigsqcup_{n}\mathrm{P}_{n})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\set{\text{Definition~\ref{def:fix-den-sem-2}}}

\displaystyle\llbracket C_{1}\,+_{p}\,C_{2}\rrbracket_{\gamma(X\leftarrow\bigsqcup_{n}\mathrm{P}_{n})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\set{\text{Definition~\ref{def:fix-den-sem-2}}}

\displaystyle\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{\gamma(X\leftarrow\bigsqcup_{n}\mathrm{P}_{n})}\,+_{p}\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{\gamma(X\leftarrow\bigsqcup_{n}\mathrm{P}_{n})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\set{\text{Definition~\ref{def:fix-den-sem-2}}}

\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{n}\Gamma^{C_{1},\gamma}(\mathrm{P}_{n})\,+_{p}\,\bigsqcup_{n}\Gamma^{C_{2},\gamma}(\mathrm{P}_{n})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\set{\text{Lemma~\ref{lem:probc-cont-2}}}

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup_{n}(\Gamma^{C_{1},\gamma}(\mathrm{P}_{n})\,+_{p}\,\Gamma^{C_{2},\gamma}(\mathrm{P}_{n}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\set{\text{Definition~\ref{def:fix-den-sem-2}}}

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup_{n}(\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{\gamma(X\leftarrow\mathrm{P}_{n})}\,+_{p}\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{\gamma(X\leftarrow\mathrm{P}_{n})})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\set{\text{Definition~\ref{def:fix-den-sem-2}}}

\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{n}\llbracket C_{1}\,+_{p}\,C_{2}\rrbracket_{\gamma(X\leftarrow\mathrm{P}_{n})}

∎

Proof of Lemma
3.46

Proof.

We only show the probabilistic choice, since the proof for the other cases is in
Lemma
2.56
.

\displaystyle\llbracket(C_{1}\,+_{p}\,C_{2})[X\leftarrow\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma}]\rrbracket_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\set{\text{Adaptation of Definition~\ref{fig:substitution-1}}}

\displaystyle\llbracket C_{1}[X\leftarrow\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma}]\,+_{p}\,C_{2}[X\leftarrow\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma}]\rrbracket_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\set{\text{Definition~\ref{def:fix-den-sem-2}}}

\displaystyle\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket[X\leftarrow\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma}]\,+_{p}\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{\gamma}[X\leftarrow\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\set{\text{i.h.}}

\displaystyle\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{\gamma(X\leftarrow\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma})}\,+_{p}\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{\gamma(X\leftarrow\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\set{\text{Definition~\ref{def:fix-den-sem-2}}}

\displaystyle\llbracket C_{1}\,+_{p}\,C_{2}\rrbracket_{\gamma(X\leftarrow\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma})}

∎

Proof of Lemma
3.47

Proof.

\displaystyle\mu X.C\rightarrow\sum_{i}p_{i}\cdot(\tau,C_{i}[X\leftarrow\mu X.C])

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\text{rules in Figure~\ref{fig:op-small2}}}

\displaystyle C\rightarrow\sum_{i}p_{i}(\tau,C_{i})

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\text{i.h.}}

\displaystyle x\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\gamma})\text{ and }x\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\sum_{i}p_{i}\cdot\llbracket C_{i}\rrbracket_{\gamma})\text{ s.t. }\exists\llbracket C_{i}\rrbracket_{\gamma}\,.\,v_{i}(x)=v(x)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\gamma=\gamma(X\leftarrow\llbracket\mu X.C)\rrbracket_{\gamma}}

\displaystyle x\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\gamma(X\leftarrow\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma})})\text{ and }x\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\sum_{i}p_{i}\cdot\llbracket C_{i}\rrbracket_{\gamma(X\leftarrow\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma})})

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\text{Lemma~\ref{lem:2-1} and Lemma~\ref{lem:1-2}}}

\displaystyle x\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\mu X.C)_{\gamma}\text{ and }x\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\sum_{i}p_{i}\llbracket C_{i}[X\leftarrow\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma}]\rrbracket_{\gamma})

∎

Proof of Lemma
3.48

Proof.

•

\displaystyle\mu X.C\rightarrow 1\cdot(l,C^{\prime}[x\leftarrow\mu X.C])

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\text{Figure~\ref{fig:op-small2}}}

\displaystyle C\rightarrow 1\cdot(l,C^{\prime})

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\text{i.h.}}

\displaystyle\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\gamma}\backslash l\equiv\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\gamma}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\gamma=\gamma(X\leftarrow\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma})}

\displaystyle\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\gamma(X\leftarrow\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma})}\backslash l\equiv\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\gamma(X\leftarrow\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma})}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\text{Lemma~\ref{lem:2-1}, Lemma~\ref{lem:1-2}}}

\displaystyle\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma}\backslash l\equiv\llbracket C^{\prime}[X\leftarrow\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma}]\rrbracket_{\gamma}

•

\displaystyle\mu X.C\rightarrow\sum_{i}p_{i}\cdot(\tau,C_{i}[x\leftarrow\mu X.C])

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\text{Figure~\ref{fig:op-small2}}}

\displaystyle C\rightarrow\sum_{i}p_{i}\cdot(\tau,C_{i})

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\text{i.h.}}

\displaystyle\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\gamma}\sqsubseteq\sum_{i}p_{i}\llbracket C_{i}\rrbracket_{\gamma}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\gamma=\gamma(X\leftarrow\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma})}

\displaystyle\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\gamma(X\leftarrow\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma})}\equiv\sum_{I}p_{i}\cdot\llbracket C_{i}\rrbracket_{\gamma(X\leftarrow\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma})}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\text{Lemma~\ref{lem:2-1}, Lemma~\ref{lem:1-2}}}

\displaystyle\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma}\equiv\sum_{i}p_{i}\llbracket C_{i}[X\leftarrow\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma}]\rrbracket_{\gamma}

∎

Proof of Theorem
3.49

Proof.

We only need to add the following sub-case when the size of the word is bigger than one and the
transition is made by
\tau
.

•

Case
C=\mu X.D

By Remark
3
and Lemma
3.47

\displaystyle\forall i,\exists\set{\tau}\cup x_{i0}\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\llbracket\mu X.D\rrbracket)\,.\,\emptyset\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ \set{\tau}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\omega_{i0}^{\prime}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ \set{\tau}\cup x_{i0}^{\prime},\ v_{i}(\set{\tau}\cup x_{i0}^{\prime})=p_{i}\cdot p_{0}^{\prime}=p_{0}

∎

Proof of Lemma
3.50

Proof.

•

l^{\prime}\neq\tau\in\mathcal{I}(\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma})

By Definition
3.44
and Definition
3.35
,

l^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}(\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\gamma})
. By i.h.,
\exists C^{\prime}
such that

C\rightarrow 1\cdot(l^{\prime},C^{\prime})
and

\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\gamma}\backslash l^{\prime}\equiv\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\gamma}
. By Figure
13

and by letting
\gamma=\gamma(X\leftarrow\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma})
and Lemma
2.57
and
Lemma
3.46
,

\mu X.C\rightarrow 1\cdot(l^{\prime},C^{\prime}[X\leftarrow\mu X.C])
and

\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma}\backslash l^{\prime}\equiv\llbracket C^{\prime}[X\leftarrow\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma}]\rrbracket_{\gamma}
.

•

l^{\prime}=\tau\in\mathcal{I}(\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma})

By Definition
3.44
and Definition
3.35
,

l^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}(\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\gamma})
. By i.h.
\exists C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime}
,

\exists e\in\mathcal{I}(\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\gamma})
such that

C\rightarrow p\cdot(\tau,C^{\prime})+(1-p)\cdot(\tau,C^{\prime\prime})
with

p=v(\set{\tau,e})
and

\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\gamma}\sqsubseteq p\cdot\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\gamma}+(1-p)\cdot\llbracket C^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket_{\gamma}
. By Figure
13
and by letting

\gamma=\gamma(X\leftarrow\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma})
and Lemma
2.57
and
Lemma
3.46
,

\mu X.C\rightarrow p\cdot(\tau,C^{\prime}[X\leftarrow\mu X.C])+(1-p)\cdot(\tau,C^{\prime\prime}[X\leftarrow\mu X.C])
and

\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma}\sqsubseteq p\cdot\llbracket C^{\prime}[X\leftarrow\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma}]\rrbracket_{\gamma}+(1-p)\cdot\llbracket C^{\prime\prime}[X\leftarrow\llbracket\mu X.C\rrbracket_{\gamma}\rrbracket_{\gamma}
.

∎

Proof of Theorem
3.51

Proof.

We only need to add the following sub-case when the size of the word is bigger than one and the
transition is made by
\tau
.

•

Case
C=\mu X.D

By Lemma
3.47
,

x_{0}\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(p\llbracket C^{\prime}[X\leftarrow\mu X.D]\rrbracket+(1-p)\llbracket C^{\prime\prime}[X\leftarrow\mu X.D]\rrbracket)
. By Definition
3.7
,

x_{0}\backslash\tau\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\llbracket C^{\prime}[X\leftarrow\mu X.D]\rrbracket)
or

x_{0}\backslash\tau\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\llbracket C^{\prime\prime}[X\leftarrow\mu X.D]\rrbracket)
. We only
consider the former, since the latter has a similar reasoning. By i.h.,

C^{\prime}[X\leftarrow\mu X.D]\twoheadrightarrow v(x_{1})(w_{x_{1}},\checkmark)+\sum_{n}p_{n}(\omega_{n},C_{n})
, for some
p_{n},\omega_{n},C_{n}
. By
Lemma
3.25
,

C^{\prime\prime}[X\leftarrow\mu X.D]\twoheadrightarrow\sum_{m}p_{m}(\omega_{m},C_{m})
.
By Figure
14
,

\displaystyle C\twoheadrightarrow p\cdot\left(v(x_{1})(\tau:w_{x_{1}},\checkmark)+\sum_{n}p_{n}(\tau:\omega_{n},C_{n})\right)+(1-p)\cdot\sum_{m}p_{m}(\tau:\omega_{m},C_{m})

∎

Proofs of Section
4

Proof of Lemma
4.6

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{1}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1},\,Q_{1})

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2},\,Q_{2})

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{1}\,;\,\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E,\,\leq,\,\#,\,Q)

Due to Lemma
2.15
we only need show the conditions added in the definition of unitary
event structures.

1.

\forall e,e^{\prime}\in E,\,e\text{ co }e^{\prime}\Rightarrow[Q(e),Q(e^{\prime})]=0

Since
e\text{ co }e^{\prime}
only if
e,e^{\prime}\in E_{1}
or
e,e^{\prime}\in E_{2}\times\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\mathrm{U}_{1})
we
are done.

2.

\mathrel{\!\!\!\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}{\hbox{\kern 17.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\!\!\!\!}
is transitive

It follows directly since
\mathrel{\!\!\!\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}{\hbox{\kern 17.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\!\!\!\!}
only occurs between events of the same set of events.

3.

\forall e\in E\sum_{e^{\prime}\in[e]}Q(e^{\prime})
is unitary

We have two cases, since there is no minimal conflict between events in
E_{1}
and

E_{2}\times\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\mathrm{U}_{1})
:

(a)

\forall e\in E_{1}

Since
E_{1}
is a unitary event structure, we are done.

(b)

\forall e\in E_{2}\times\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\mathrm{U}_{1})

Since
E_{2}
is a unitary event structure, we are done.

∎

Proof of Lemma
4.8

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{1}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1},\,Q_{1})

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2},\,Q_{2})

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E,\,\leq,\,\#,\,Q)

Due to Lemma
2.17
we only need show the conditions added in the definition of unitary
event structures.

1.

\forall e,e^{\prime}\in E,\,e\text{ co }e^{\prime}\Rightarrow[Q(e),Q(e^{\prime})]=0

We have two cases:

(a)

e,e^{\prime}\in E_{1}
or
e,e^{\prime}\in E_{2}

The condition trivially holds, since
\mathrm{U}_{1}
and
\mathrm{U}_{2}
are unitary event structures.

(b)

e\in E_{1}
and
e^{\prime}\in E_{2}

It follows directly from Definition
4.7
.

2.

\mathrel{\!\!\!\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}{\hbox{\kern 17.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\!\!\!\!}
is transitive

It follows directly since the parallel composition does not create new conflicts and that the
conflict relation is inherited from
\mathrm{U}_{1}
and
\mathrm{U}_{2}
which are unitary event
structures.

3.

\forall e\in E,\sum_{e^{\prime}\in[e]}Q(e^{\prime})
is unitary

Since there is no minimal conflict between events in
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
, it follows directly that
if
\forall e\in E_{1}
or
\forall e\in E_{2}
the condition holds since
\mathrm{U}_{1}
and

\mathrm{U}_{2}
are unitary event structures.

∎

Proof of Lemma
4.10

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{1}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1},\,Q_{1})

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2},\,Q_{2})

\displaystyle M(n,\mathrm{U}_{1},\mathrm{U}_{2})=(E,\,\leq,\,\#,\,Q)

We need to show that
\leq
is a partial order and that
\#
is symmetric and irreflexive.

•

\leq
is a partial order:

–

Reflexivity (
e\leq e
): we have the following cases:

1.

Case
e\in\{\tau_{0}^{n},\tau_{1}^{n}\}
. It follows directly from
Definition
4.9
that either
\tau_{0}^{n}\leq\tau_{0}^{n}
or

\tau_{1}^{n}\leq\tau_{1}^{n}
.

2.

Case
e_{1}\leq_{1}e^{\prime}_{1}
or
e_{2}\leq_{2}e^{\prime}_{2}
. Since
\leq_{1}
and
\leq_{2}
are partial
orders, we are done.

–

Transitivity (
e\leq e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime}\leq e^{\prime\prime}
then
e\leq e^{\prime\prime}
): we have the following cases:

1.

Case (
e\leq_{1}e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime}\leq_{1}e^{\prime\prime}
) or (
e\leq_{2}e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime}\leq_{2}e^{\prime\prime}
). Since

\leq_{1}
and
\leq_{2}
are partial orders, we have
e\leq_{1}e^{\prime\prime}
or
e\leq_{2}e^{\prime\prime}
. Hence

e\leq e^{\prime\prime}
.

2.

Case
e=\tau_{0}^{n}
and
e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime}\in E_{1}
. We have
\tau_{0}^{n}\leq e^{\prime}
and

e^{\prime}\leq e^{\prime\prime}
. Since
e^{\prime\prime}\in E_{1}
then
\tau_{n}^{0}\leq e^{\prime\prime}
.

3.

Case
e=\tau_{1}^{n}
and
e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime}\in E_{2}
. We have
\tau_{1}^{n}\leq e^{\prime}
and

e^{\prime}\leq e^{\prime\prime}
. Since
e^{\prime\prime}\in E_{2}
then
\tau_{n}^{1}\leq e^{\prime\prime}
.

–

Antisymmetry (
e\leq e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime}\leq e
then
e^{\prime}=e
): we have two cases: (
e\leq_{1}e^{\prime}

and
e^{\prime}\leq_{1}e
) or (
e\leq_{2}e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime}\leq_{2}e
). Since
\leq_{1}
and
\leq_{2}
are
partial orders, we have
e=e^{\prime}
. Since there are no more cases we are done.

Hence
\leq
is a partial order.

•

\#
is symmetric and irreflexive:

–

Symmetric (if
e\#e^{\prime}
then
e^{\prime}\#e
): we have the following cases

1.

Case
e\#_{1}e^{\prime}
or
e\#_{2}e^{\prime}
. Since
\#_{1}
and
\#_{2}
are symmetric then we have

e^{\prime}\#_{1}e
or
e^{\prime}\#_{2}e
. Thus
e^{\prime}\#e
.

2.

Case (
e=\tau_{0}^{n}
or
e\in E_{1}
) and (
e^{\prime}=\tau_{1}^{n}
or
e^{\prime}\in E_{2}
). It follows
directly from Definition
4.9
.

3.

Case (
e=\tau_{1}^{n}
or
e\in E_{2}
) and (
e^{\prime}=\tau_{0}^{n}
or
e^{\prime}\in E_{1}
). It follows
directly from Definition
4.9
.

–

Irreflexive (
\neg(e\#e)
): We have either
\neg(e\#_{1}e)
or
\neg(e\#_{2}e)
. Since

\#_{1}
and
\#_{2}
are irreflexive then
\neg(e\#e)
. There are no more cases since either

e\in E_{1}
or
e\in E_{2}
.

Hence
\#
is symmetric and irreflexive.

We need to prove:

1.

\set{e^{\prime}\mid e^{\prime}\leq e}
is finite

We have four cases:

(a)

e=\tau_{0}^{n}

It follows directly that
\set{e^{\prime}\mid e^{\prime}\leq\tau_{0}^{n}}=\set{\tau_{0}^{n}}
since

\tau_{0}^{n}\in\mathcal{I}(M(n,\mathrm{U}_{1},\mathrm{U}_{2}))
.

(b)

e=\tau_{1}^{n}

It follows directly that
\set{e^{\prime}\mid e^{\prime}\leq\tau_{1}^{n}}=\set{\tau_{1}^{n}}
since

\tau_{1}^{n}\in\mathcal{I}(M(n,\mathrm{U}_{1},\mathrm{U}_{2}))
.

(c)

e\in E_{1}

We have that
\set{e^{\prime}\mid e^{\prime}\leq e}=\set{\tau_{0}^{n}}\cup\set{e^{\prime}\mid e^{\prime}\leq_{1}e}
. Since

\mathrm{U}_{1}
is a unitary event structure, then we know that
\set{e^{\prime}\mid e^{\prime}\leq_{1}e}
is
finite. Hence
\set{\tau_{0}^{n}}\cup\set{e^{\prime}\mid e^{\prime}\leq_{1}e}
is finite.

(d)

e\in E_{2}

We have that
\set{e^{\prime}\mid e^{\prime}\leq e}=\set{\tau_{1}^{n}}\cup\set{e^{\prime}\mid e^{\prime}\leq_{2}e}
. Since

\mathrm{U}_{2}
is a unitary event structure, then we know that
\set{e^{\prime}\mid e^{\prime}\leq_{2}e}
is
finite. Hence
\set{\tau_{1}^{n}}\cup\set{e^{\prime}\mid e^{\prime}\leq_{2}e}
is finite.

2.

e\#e^{\prime}\leq e^{\prime\prime}\Rightarrow e\#e^{\prime\prime}

It follows directly by Definition
4.9
that
e\#e^{\prime\prime}
.

3.

e\text{ co }e^{\prime}\Rightarrow[Q(e),Q(e^{\prime})]=0

The concurrent events are either in
\mathrm{U}_{1}
or in
\mathrm{U}_{2}
, which are unitary event
structures, hence the condition trivially holds.

4.

\mathrel{\!\!\!\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}{\hbox{\kern 17.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\!\!\!\!}
is transitive

It follows directly since the conflict relation is inherited from
\mathrm{U}_{1},\mathrm{U}_{2}
, which
are unitary event structures, and from the fact that the new events,
\tau_{0}^{n}
and
\tau_{1}^{n}
,
are in minimal conflict between them,
i.e.

\tau_{0}^{n}\mathrel{\!\!\!\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}{\hbox{\kern 17.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\!\!\!\!}\tau_{1}^{n}
.

5.

\forall e\in E,\sum_{e^{\prime}\in[e]}Q(e^{\prime})
is unitary

We have two cases (since if
e_{1}\in E_{1},e_{2}\in E_{2}
then
\neg(e_{1}\mathrel{\!\!\!\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}{\hbox{\kern 17.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\!\!\!\!}e_{2})
):

(a)

e=\tau_{0}^{n},e^{\prime}=\tau_{1}^{n}
or vice-versa

It follows directly from Definition
4.9
that
Q(\tau_{0}^{n})+Q(\tau_{1}^{n})=Id
,
which is unitary.

(b)

\forall e\in E_{1}
or
\forall e\in E_{2}

It follows directly from
\mathrm{U}_{1}
and
\mathrm{U}_{2}
being unitary events structures.

∎

Proof of Lemma
4.14

Proof.

Let
\mathrm{U}=(E,\,\leq,\,\#,\,Q)
and
\mathrm{U}\backslash a=(E^{\prime},\,\leq^{\prime},\,\#^{\prime},\,Q^{\prime})
.

Due to Lemma
2.24
we only need to check the conditions added in the definition
of unitary event structures.

1.

\forall e,e^{\prime}\in E^{\prime},\,e\text{ co }e^{\prime}\Rightarrow[Q^{\prime}(e),Q^{\prime}(e^{\prime})]=0

It follows directly from Definition
4.13
that

[Q^{\prime}(e),Q^{\prime}(e^{\prime})]=[Q|_{E^{\prime}}(e),Q|_{E^{\prime}}(e^{\prime})]=0

2.

\mathrel{\!\!\!\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}{\hbox{\kern 17.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\!\!\!\!}
is transitive

It follows directly since the conflict relation
\#^{\prime}
is the restriction of
\#
to the events
of
E^{\prime}
.

3.

\forall e\in E,\sum_{e^{\prime}\in[e]}Q(e^{\prime})
is unitary

It follows directly from Definition
4.13
that

\sum_{e^{\prime}\in[e]}Q^{\prime}(e^{\prime})=\sum_{e^{\prime}\in[e]}Q|_{E^{\prime}}(e^{\prime})
which is unitary.

∎

Proof of Lemma
4.15

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{1}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1},\,Q_{1})

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{1}=(E^{\prime}_{1},\,\leq^{\prime}_{1},\,\#^{\prime}_{1},\,Q^{\prime}_{1})

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2},\,Q_{2})

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{2}=(E^{\prime}_{2},\,\leq^{\prime}_{2},\,\#^{\prime}_{2},\,Q^{\prime}_{2})

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{1}\,;\,\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E,\,\leq,\,\#,\,Q)

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{1}\,;\,\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{2}=(E^{\prime},\,\leq^{\prime},\,\#^{\prime},\,Q^{\prime})

such that
\mathrm{U}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{1}
and
\mathrm{U}_{2}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{2}
.

Due to Lemma
2.25
we only need to show

\forall e\in E\ .\ Q(e)=Q^{\prime}(f(e))
.

Let
e\in E
.
We have two cases:

1.

e\in E_{1}

Since
\mathrm{U}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{1}
, it follows directly that

Q(e)=Q_{1}(e)=Q_{1}^{\prime}(e)=Q^{\prime}(e)
.

2.

e=(e_{2},x)\in E_{2}\times\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\mathrm{U}_{1})

By Definition
4.5
, we know that
Q(e_{2},x)=Q_{2}(e_{2})
. Since

\mathrm{U}_{2}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{2}
, then
Q_{2}(e_{2})=Q^{\prime}_{2}(e_{2})
. By
Definition
4.5
, we have that
Q^{\prime}_{2}(e_{2})=Q^{\prime}(e_{2},m_{1}(x))
.

∎

Proof of Lemma
4.16

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{1}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1},\,Q_{1})

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{1}=(E^{\prime}_{1},\,\leq^{\prime}_{1},\,\#^{\prime}_{1},\,Q^{\prime}_{1})

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2},\,Q_{2})

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{2}=(E^{\prime}_{2},\,\leq^{\prime}_{2},\,\#^{\prime}_{2},\,Q^{\prime}_{2})

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E,\,\leq,\,\#,\,Q)

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{2}=(E^{\prime},\,\leq^{\prime},\,\#^{\prime},\,Q^{\prime})

such that
\mathrm{U}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{1}
and
\mathrm{U}_{2}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{2}
.

Due to Lemma
2.27
we only need to show

\forall e\in E\ .\ Q(e)=Q^{\prime}(f(e))
.

Let
e\in E
. If
e\in E_{1}
then by Definition
4.7
we have
Q(e)=Q_{1}(e)
,
which by
\mathrm{U}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{U}_{1}^{\prime}
gives
Q_{1}(e)=Q_{1}^{\prime}(e)
that by
Definition
4.7
gives
Q_{1}^{\prime}(e)=Q^{\prime}(e)
. Similarly when
e\in E_{2}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.17

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{1}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1},\,Q_{1})

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{1}=(E^{\prime}_{1},\,\leq^{\prime}_{1},\,\#^{\prime}_{1},\,Q^{\prime}_{1})

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2},\,Q_{2})

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{2}=(E^{\prime}_{2},\,\leq^{\prime}_{2},\,\#^{\prime}_{2},\,Q^{\prime}_{2})

\displaystyle M(n,\mathrm{U}_{1},\mathrm{U}_{2})=(E,\,\leq,\,\#,\,Q)

\displaystyle M(n,\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{1},\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{2})=(E^{\prime},\,\leq^{\prime},\,\#^{\prime},\,Q^{\prime})

such that
\mathrm{U}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{1}
and
\mathrm{U}_{2}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{2}
.

We have to show that:

1.

We start by defining
f:E\rightarrow E^{\prime}
such that

f(e)=\begin{cases}e&\text{ if }e\in\mathcal{I}(M(n,\mathrm{U}_{1},\mathrm{U}_{2}))\\
f_{1}(e)&\text{ if }e\in E_{1}\\
f_{2}(e)&\text{ if }e\in E_{2}\end{cases}

It is straightforward to see that
f
is injective

2.

\pi(f(e))=\pi(e)

If
e\in E_{1}
or
e\in E_{2}
, we are done since
\mathrm{U}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{U^{\prime}}_{1}
and
\mathrm{U}_{2}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{U^{\prime}}_{2}
.
If
e\in\mathcal{I}(M(n,\mathrm{U}_{1},\mathrm{U}_{2}))
, then
\pi(f(e))=\pi(e)
, since
f(e)=e
.

3.

e\leq e^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow f(e)\leq^{\prime}f(e^{\prime})

If
e\in E_{1}
or
e\in E_{2}
, we are done since
\mathrm{U}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{U^{\prime}}_{1}
and
\mathrm{U}_{2}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{U^{\prime}}_{2}
.
If
e\in\mathcal{I}(M(n,\mathrm{U}_{1},\mathrm{U}_{2}))
, since
f(e)=e
. Consequently
e\leq e^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow e\leq^{\prime}f(e^{\prime})
, which is trivially satisfied by Definition
4.9
.

4.

e\#e^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow f(e)\#^{\prime}f(e^{\prime})

This case is similar to that of Lemma
2.26
.

5.

\forall e\in E\ .\ Q(e)=Q^{\prime}(f(e))

If
e=\tau_{0}^{n}
or
e=\tau_{1}^{n}
then we are done, since
f(e)=e
and, consequently,

Q(e)=Q^{\prime}(e)
. If
e\in E_{1}
or
e\in E_{2}
we are done since
\mathrm{U}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{1}
and

\mathrm{U}_{2}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{2}
.

The first three conditions follow directly from Definition
4.9
. For the last
condition we argue as follows:
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.18

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{1}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1},\,Q_{1})

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2},\,Q_{2})

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{1}\,;\,\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E_{1\,;\,2},\,\leq_{1\,;\,2},\,\#_{1\,;\,2},\,Q_{1\,;\,2})

\displaystyle(\mathrm{U}_{1}\,;\,\mathrm{U}_{2})\backslash l=(E,\,\leq,\,\#,\,Q)

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{1}\backslash l=(E_{1}^{l},\,\leq_{1}^{l},\,\#_{1}^{l},\,Q_{1}^{l})

\displaystyle(\mathrm{U}_{1}\backslash l)\,;\,\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E^{\prime},\,\leq^{\prime},\,\#^{\prime},\,Q^{\prime})

\displaystyle l\in\mathcal{I}(\mathrm{U}_{1}\,;\,\mathrm{U}_{2})

Due to Lemma
2.29
we focus only on the quantum part.

•

(\mathrm{U}_{1}\,;\,\mathrm{U}_{2})\backslash l\sqsubseteq(\mathrm{U}_{1}\backslash l)\,;\,\mathrm{U}_{2}

We need to show
\forall e\in E\ .\ Q(e)=Q^{\prime}(f(e))
. Let
e\in E
. By
Definition
4.13
,
Q(e)=Q_{1\,;\,2}|_{E}(e)
.
By Definition
4.5
we have two cases:

–

e\in E_{1}

By Definition
4.5
and since
e\neq l
and
\neg(e\#l)
, which gives

e\in E_{1}^{l}
, then
Q_{1\,;\,2}|_{E}(e)=Q_{1}|_{E_{1}^{l}}(e)
. By
Definition
4.13
,
Q_{1}|_{E_{1}^{l}}(e)=Q_{1}^{l}(e)
. By
Definition
4.5
,
Q_{1}^{l}(e)=Q^{\prime}(e)
.

–

e=(e_{2},x)\in E_{2}\times\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\mathrm{U}_{1})

By Definition
4.5
and since
l\not\in E_{2}
,

Q_{1\,;\,2}|_{E}(e_{2},x)=Q_{2}(e_{2})
. Hence, again by
Definition
4.5
,
Q_{2}(e_{2})=Q^{\prime}(e_{2},m_{1}(x))
.

•

(\mathrm{U}_{1}\backslash l)\,;\,\mathrm{U}_{2}\sqsubseteq(\mathrm{U}_{1}\,;\,\mathrm{U}_{2})\backslash l

Similar reasoning to the previous bullet.

∎

Proof of Lemma
4.19

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{1}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1},\,Q_{1})

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2},\,Q_{2})

\displaystyle M(n,\mathrm{U}_{1},\mathrm{U}_{2})=(E,\,\leq,\,\#,\,Q)

\displaystyle(M(n,\mathrm{U}_{1},\mathrm{U}_{2}))\backslash l=(E^{\prime},\,\leq^{\prime},\,\#^{\prime},\,Q^{\prime})

Let us focus on the case where
l=\tau_{0}^{n}
, since the reasoning when
l=\tau_{1}^{n}
is equal to
the one below.

•

(M(n,\mathrm{U}_{1},\mathrm{U}_{2}))\backslash\tau_{0}^{n}\sqsubseteq\mathrm{U}_{1}

1.

We start by defining
f:E^{\prime}\rightarrow E_{1}
as being the identity, which is injective.

2.

\pi(f(e))=\pi(e)

It follows directly since
f
is the identity.

3.

e\leq^{\prime}e^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow f(e)\leq_{1}f(e^{\prime})

Since
f
is the identity,
e\leq^{\prime}e^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow e\leq_{1}e^{\prime}
. It then follows directly
from Definition
4.13
and Definition
4.9
.

4.

e\#^{\prime}e^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow f(e)\#_{1}f(e^{\prime})

Since
f
is the identity,
e\#^{\prime}e^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow e\#_{1}e^{\prime}
. It then follows directly
from Definition
4.13
and Definition
4.9
.

5.

\forall e\in E^{\prime}\ .\ Q^{\prime}(e)=Q_{1}(f(e))

Since
f
is the identity,
Q^{\prime}(e)=Q_{1}(e)
. Let
e\in E^{\prime}
. By
Definition
4.13
,
Q^{\prime}(e)=Q|_{E^{\prime}}(e)
. Since
\tau_{0}^{n}\leq e
we know that

e\in E_{1}
, hence by Definition
4.9
,
Q|_{E^{\prime}}(e)=Q_{1}(e)
.

•

\mathrm{U}_{1}\sqsubseteq(M(n,\mathrm{U}_{1},\mathrm{U}_{2}))\backslash\tau_{0}^{n}

1.

We start by defining
f:E_{1}\rightarrow E^{\prime}
as being the identity, which is injective.

2.

\pi(f(e))=\pi(e)

It follows directly since
f
is the identity.

3.

e\leq_{1}e^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow f(e)\leq^{\prime}f(e^{\prime})

Since
f
is the identity,
e\leq_{1}e^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow e\leq^{\prime}e^{\prime}
. It then follows directly
from Definition
4.13
and Definition
4.9
.

4.

e\#_{1}e^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow f(e)\#^{\prime}f(e^{\prime})

Since
f
is the identity,
e\#_{1}e^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow e\#^{\prime}e^{\prime}
. It then follows directly
from Definition
4.13
and Definition
4.9
.

5.

\forall e\in E_{1}\ .\ Q_{1}(e)=Q^{\prime}(f(e))

Since
f
is the identity,
Q_{1}(e)=Q^{\prime}(e)
. Let
e\in E_{1}
. By
Definition
4.9
,
Q(e)=Q_{1}(e)
. Since
e\neq l
and
\neg(e\#l)
, then

Q^{\prime}(e)=Q(e)
. Thus
Q_{1}(e)=Q^{\prime}(e)
.

∎

Proof of Lemma
4.20

Proof.

Let
l\in\mathcal{I}(\mathrm{E}_{1})
and

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{1}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1},\,Q_{1})

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2},\,Q_{2})

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E_{1\,||\,2},\,\leq_{1\,||\,2},\,\#_{1\,||\,2},\,Q_{1\,||\,2})

\displaystyle(\mathrm{U}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{U}_{2})\backslash l=(E,\,\leq,\,\#,\,Q)

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{1}\backslash l=(E_{1}^{l},\,\leq_{1}^{l},\,\#_{1}^{l},\,Q_{1}^{l})

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{2}\backslash l=(E_{2}^{l},\,\leq_{2}^{l},\,\#_{2}^{l},\,Q_{2}^{l})

\displaystyle(\mathrm{U}_{1}\backslash l)\,||\,\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E^{\prime},\,\leq^{\prime},\,\#^{\prime},\,Q^{\prime})

\displaystyle l\in\mathcal{I}(\mathrm{U}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{U}_{2})

Due to Lemma
2.31
we focus only on the quantum part.

•

(\mathrm{U}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{U}_{2})\backslash l\sqsubseteq(\mathrm{U}_{1}\backslash l)\,||\,\mathrm{U}_{2}

We need to show
\forall e\in E\ .\ Q(e)=Q^{\prime}(f(e))
.
Let
e\in E
.
By Definition
4.13
,
Q(e)=Q_{1\,||\,2}|_{E}(e)
.
By Definition
4.7
we have two cases:

1.

e\in E_{1}

Since
l\in\mathcal{I}(\mathrm{U}_{1})
then
e\neq l
and
\neg(e\#l)
. Thus
e\in E_{1}^{l}
.
Consequently,
Q_{1\,||\,2}|_{E}(e)=Q|_{E_{1}^{l}}(e)
. By Definition
4.13
,

Q1|_{E_{1}^{l}}(e)=Q_{1}^{l}(e)
. By Definition
4.7
,
Q_{1}^{l}(e)=Q^{\prime}(e)
.

2.

e\in E_{2}

Then
Q_{1\,||\,2}|_{E}(e)=Q_{2}(e)
. By Definition
4.7
,
Q_{2}(e)=Q^{\prime}(e)
.

•

(\mathrm{U}_{1}\backslash l)\,||\,\mathrm{U}_{2}\sqsubseteq(\mathrm{U}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{U}_{2})\backslash l

We need to show
\forall e\in E^{\prime}\ .\ Q^{\prime}(e)=Q(e)
.
By Definition
4.7
we have two cases:

1.

e\in E_{1}^{l}

Then
Q^{\prime}(e)=Q_{1}^{l}(e)
. By Definition
4.13
,
Q_{1}^{l}(e)=Q_{1}|_{E_{1}^{l}}(e)
. By
Definition
4.7
,
Q_{1}|_{E_{1}^{l}}(e)=Q_{1\,||\,2}|_{E_{1}^{l}\uplus E_{2}}
. By
Definition
4.13
,
Q_{1\,||\,2}|_{E_{1}^{l}\uplus E_{2}}=Q(e)
.

2.

e\in E_{2}^{l}

Then
Q^{\prime}(e)=Q_{2}(e)
. By Definition
4.7
,
Q_{2}(e)=Q_{1\,||\,2}(e)
. By
Definition
4.13
,
Q_{1\,||\,2}(e)=Q(e)
.

∎

Proof of Lemma
4.21

Proof.

It follows directly from Definition
4.7
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.22

Proof.

Induction over rules in Figure
21
.

•

skip\xrightarrow{sk}\checkmark

It follows directly that
\llbracket\checkmark\rrbracket\equiv\llbracket skip\rrbracket\backslash sk\equiv\emptyset
.

•

a\xrightarrow{a}\checkmark

It follows directly that
\llbracket\checkmark\rrbracket\equiv\llbracket a\rrbracket\backslash a\equiv\emptyset
.

•

M(n,C_{1},C_{2})\xrightarrow{\tau_{0}^{n}}C_{1}

It follows directly since

\mathrm{P}_{0}^{n}\,;\,\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\mathrm{P}_{0}^{n}\,;\,\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket+\mathrm{P}_{1}^{n}\,;\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket=\llbracket M(n,C_{1},C_{2})\rrbracket
.

•

M(n,C_{1},C_{2})\xrightarrow{\tau_{1}^{n}}C_{2}

It follows directly since

\mathrm{P}_{1}^{n}\,;\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\mathrm{P}_{0}^{n}\,;\,\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket+\mathrm{P}_{1}^{n}\,;\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket=\llbracket M(n,C_{1},C_{2})\rrbracket
.

•

C_{1}\,;\,C_{2}\xrightarrow{l}C_{2}

\displaystyle C_{1}\,;\,C_{2}\xrightarrow{l}C_{2}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\xrightarrow{l}\text{ entails}}

\displaystyle C_{1}\xrightarrow{l}\checkmark

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\text{i.h.}}

\displaystyle\llbracket\checkmark\rrbracket\equiv\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\backslash l

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\text{Lemma~\ref{lem:seq-mono3}}}

\displaystyle\llbracket\checkmark\rrbracket\,;\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket\equiv(\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\backslash l)\,;\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\llbracket\checkmark\rrbracket\,;\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket\equiv\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket,\text{ Lemma~\ref{lem:seq-rem-init3}}}

\displaystyle\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket\equiv(\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\,;\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket)\backslash l

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\text{Definition~\ref{def:den-sem3}}}

\displaystyle\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket\equiv\llbracket C_{1}\,;\,C_{2}\rrbracket\backslash l

•

C_{1}\,;\,C_{2}\xrightarrow{l}C^{\prime}_{1}\,;\,C_{2}

\displaystyle C_{1}\,;\,C_{2}\xrightarrow{l}C^{\prime}_{1}\,;\,C_{2}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\xrightarrow{l}\text{ entails}}

\displaystyle C_{1}\xrightarrow{l}C^{\prime}_{1}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\text{i.h.}}

\displaystyle\llbracket C^{\prime}_{1}\rrbracket\equiv\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\backslash l

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\text{Lemma~\ref{lem:seq-mono3}}}

\displaystyle\llbracket C^{\prime}_{1}\rrbracket\,;\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket\equiv(\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\backslash l)\,;\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\text{Lemma~\ref{lem:seq-rem-init3}}}

\displaystyle\llbracket C^{\prime}_{1}\rrbracket\,;\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket\equiv(\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\,;\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket)\backslash l

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\text{Definition~\ref{def:den-sem3}}}

\displaystyle\llbracket C^{\prime}_{1}\,;\,C_{2}\rrbracket\equiv\llbracket C_{1}\,;\,C_{2}\rrbracket\backslash l

•

C_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\xrightarrow{l}C_{2}

\displaystyle C_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\xrightarrow{l}C_{2}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\xrightarrow{l}\text{ entails}}

\displaystyle C_{1}\xrightarrow{l}\checkmark

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\text{i.h.}}

\displaystyle\llbracket\checkmark\rrbracket\equiv\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\backslash l

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\text{Lemma~\ref{lem:conc-mono3}}}

\displaystyle\llbracket\checkmark\rrbracket\,||\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket\equiv(\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\backslash l)\,||\,C_{2}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\llbracket\checkmark\rrbracket\,||\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket\equiv\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket\equiv(\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\backslash l)\,||\,C_{2}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\text{Lemma~\ref{lem:conc-rem-init3}},\text{Definition~\ref{def:den-sem3}}}

\displaystyle\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket\equiv\llbracket C_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\rrbracket\backslash l

•

C_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\xrightarrow{l}C^{\prime}_{1}\,||\,C_{2}

\displaystyle C_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\xrightarrow{l}C^{\prime}_{1}\,||\,C_{2}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\xrightarrow{l}\text{ entails}}

\displaystyle C_{1}\xrightarrow{l}C^{\prime}_{1}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\text{i.h.}}

\displaystyle\llbracket C^{\prime}_{1}\rrbracket\equiv\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\backslash l

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\text{Lemma~\ref{lem:conc-mono3}}}

\displaystyle\llbracket C^{\prime}_{1}\rrbracket\,||\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket\equiv(\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\backslash l)\,||\,C_{2}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\text{Lemma~\ref{lem:conc-rem-init3}},\text{Definition~\ref{def:den-sem3}}}

\displaystyle\llbracket C^{\prime}_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\rrbracket\equiv\llbracket C_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\rrbracket\backslash l

•

C_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\xrightarrow{l}C_{1}

\displaystyle C_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\xrightarrow{l}C_{1}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\xrightarrow{l}\text{ entails}}

\displaystyle C_{2}\xrightarrow{l}\checkmark

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\text{i.h.}}

\displaystyle\llbracket\checkmark\rrbracket\equiv\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket\backslash l

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\text{Lemma~\ref{lem:conc-mono3}}}

\displaystyle\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\,||\,\llbracket\checkmark\rrbracket\equiv\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\,||\,(\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket\backslash l)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\,||\,\llbracket\checkmark\rrbracket\equiv\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\equiv\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\,||\,(\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket\backslash l)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\text{Lemma~\ref{lem:conc-rem-init3}},\text{Definition~\ref{def:den-sem3}}}

\displaystyle\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\equiv\llbracket C_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\rrbracket\backslash l

•

C_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\xrightarrow{l}C_{1}\,||\,C^{\prime}_{2}

\displaystyle C_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\xrightarrow{l}C_{1}\,||\,C^{\prime}_{2}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\xrightarrow{l}\text{ entails}}

\displaystyle C_{2}\xrightarrow{l}C^{\prime}_{2}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\text{i.h.}}

\displaystyle\llbracket C^{\prime}_{2}\rrbracket\equiv\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket\backslash l

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\text{Lemma~\ref{lem:conc-mono3}}}

\displaystyle\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\,||\,\llbracket C^{\prime}_{2}\rrbracket\equiv C_{1}\,||\,(\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket\backslash l)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\text{Lemma~\ref{lem:conc-rem-init3}},\text{Definition~\ref{def:den-sem3}}}

\displaystyle\llbracket C_{1}\,||\,C^{\prime}_{2}\rrbracket\equiv\llbracket C_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\rrbracket\backslash l

∎

Proof of Theorem
4.23

Proof.

•

|\omega|=1

It follows directly that

\exists\set{l}\in\mathcal{C}(\llbracket C\rrbracket)\,.\,\emptyset\stackrel{{\scriptstyle l}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ \set{l}

•

|\omega|>1

\displaystyle C\xrightarrow{\omega}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow C^{\prime}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\text{Definition~\ref{fig:op-nstep3}}}

\displaystyle C\xrightarrow{l}C^{\prime\prime}\qquad C^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{\omega^{\prime}}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow C^{\prime}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\text{Lemma~\ref{res:soundI-3}, i.h.}}

\displaystyle\llbracket C^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket\equiv\llbracket C\rrbracket\backslash l\qquad\exists y\in\mathcal{C}(\llbracket C^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket)\,.\,\emptyset\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\omega^{\prime}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ y

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\set{\text{Definition~\ref{def:rem-init3}}}

\displaystyle\set{l}\cup y\in\mathcal{C}(\llbracket C\rrbracket)\,.\,\emptyset\stackrel{{\scriptstyle l}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ \set{l}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\omega^{\prime}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ \set{l}\cup y=x

∎

Proof of Lemma
4.24

Proof.

Induction over the interpretation of commands.

•

sk\in\mathcal{I}(skip)

Let
C^{\prime}=\checkmark
. It follows directly that
skip\xrightarrow{sk}\checkmark

and that
\llbracket skip\rrbracket\backslash sk\equiv\llbracket\checkmark\rrbracket
.

•

a\in\mathcal{I}(a)

Let
C^{\prime}=\checkmark
. It follows directly that
a\xrightarrow{a}\checkmark

and that
\llbracket a\rrbracket\backslash a\equiv\llbracket\checkmark\rrbracket
.

•

l\in\mathcal{I}(M(n,C_{1},C_{2}))

By Definition
4.11
,
\llbracket{M(n,C_{1},C_{2})}\rrbracket=\mathrm{P}_{0}^{n}\,;\,\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket+\mathrm{P}_{1}^{n}\,;\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket
.
We have two cases:

1.

l^{\prime}=\tau_{0}^{n}

By Lemma
4.19
,

(\mathrm{P}_{0}^{n}\,;\,\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket+\mathrm{P}_{1}^{n}\,;\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket)\backslash\tau_{0}^{n}\equiv\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket
. Furthermore

M(n,C_{1},C_{2})\xrightarrow{\tau_{0}^{n}}C_{1}
.

2.

l^{\prime}=\tau_{1}^{n}

By Lemma
4.19
,

(\mathrm{P}_{0}^{n}\,;\,\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket+\mathrm{P}_{1}^{n}\,;\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket)\backslash\tau_{1}^{n}\equiv\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket
. Furthermore

M(n,C_{1},C_{2})\xrightarrow{\tau_{1}^{n}}C_{2}
.

•

l^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}(C_{1}\,;\,C_{2})

By Definition
4.5
we have that
l^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}(\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket)
. By i.h.,

\exists C^{\prime}
such that
C_{1}\xrightarrow{l}C^{\prime}
and

\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\backslash l^{\prime}\equiv\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket
. We have two cases:

1.

C^{\prime}=\checkmark

We have
C_{1}\xrightarrow{l^{\prime}}\checkmark
and

\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\backslash l^{\prime}\equiv\llbracket\checkmark\rrbracket
. By the rules in Figure
21
,

C_{1}\,;\,C_{2}\xrightarrow{l^{\prime}}C_{2}
.
By Definition
4.5
,
(\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\backslash l^{\prime})\,;\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket\equiv\llbracket\checkmark\rrbracket\,;\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket\equiv\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket
.

2.

C^{\prime}=C^{\prime}_{1}

We have
C_{1}\xrightarrow{l^{\prime}}C^{\prime}_{1}
and

\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\backslash l^{\prime}\equiv\llbracket C^{\prime}_{1}\rrbracket
. By the rules in Figure
21
,

C_{1}\,;\,C_{2}\xrightarrow{l^{\prime}}C^{\prime}_{1}\,;\,C_{2}
. By
Definition
4.5
,

(\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\backslash l^{\prime})\,;\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket\equiv\llbracket C^{\prime}_{1}\rrbracket\,;\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket
.
By Definition
4.11
,
\llbracket C^{\prime}_{1}\,;\,C_{2}\rrbracket
.

•

l^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}(C_{1}\,||\,C_{2})

By Definition
4.7
we have two cases:

1.

l^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}(\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket)

By i.h.
\exists C^{\prime}\,.\,C_{1}\xrightarrow{l^{\prime}}C^{\prime}
and

\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\backslash l^{\prime}\equiv\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket
.
By the rules in Figure
21
we have two cases:

(a)

C^{\prime}=\checkmark

We have
C_{1}\xrightarrow{l^{\prime}}\checkmark
and

\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\backslash l^{\prime}\equiv\llbracket\checkmark\rrbracket
. By the rules in
Figure
21
we have
C_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\xrightarrow{l^{\prime}}C_{2}
.
By Definition
4.7
,
(\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\backslash l^{\prime})\,||\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket
.
By Lemma
4.20
we have

(\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\,||\,\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket)\backslash l^{\prime}
. By Definition

,

\llbracket C_{1}\,||\,C_{2}\rrbracket\backslash l^{\prime}
.

we have

. By Definition

By i.h.

and

.
By the rules in Figure

we have two cases:

C^{\prime}=\checkmark

and

. By the rules in Figure

Proof of Theorem
4.25

Proof.

Induction over the length of
\omega
.

•

|\omega|=1

We have
\set{l^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{C}(C)
such that
\emptyset\stackrel{{\scriptstyle l^{\prime}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ \set{l^{\prime}}
. Furthermore

l^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}(\llbracket C\rrbracket)
. By Lemma
4.24
,
C\xrightarrow{l^{\prime}}C^{\prime}
and

\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket\equiv\llbracket C\rrbracket\backslash l^{\prime}
. By the rules in Figure
22
,

C\xrightarrow{l^{\prime}}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow C^{\prime}
.

•

|\omega|>1

We have
x\in\mathcal{C}(\llbracket C\rrbracket)
such that
\emptyset\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\omega}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ x
. Since

\omega=l_{0}l_{1}\dots l_{n}
, then
\emptyset\stackrel{{\scriptstyle l_{0}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ \set{l_{0}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\omega^{\prime}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ x
. Hence

l_{0}\in\mathcal{I}(\llbracket C\rrbracket)
. By Lemma
4.24
,
C\xrightarrow{l_{0}}C^{\prime}

and
\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket\equiv\llbracket C\rrbracket\backslash l_{0}
. By Definition
4.13
,

\exists y\in\mathcal{C}(\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket)
such that
\emptyset\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\omega^{\prime}}}{{\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 2.25006pt\hbox{$\rule{11.38092pt}{0.5pt}$}\cr\hbox{$\subset\mkern-12.0mu$}\cr}}}}}\ y
. By
i.h.
\exists C^{\prime\prime}
such that
C^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\omega^{\prime}}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow C^{\prime\prime}
. By the
rules in Figure
22
,
C\xrightarrow{\omega}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow C^{\prime\prime}
, where

\omega=l_{0}:\omega^{\prime}
.

∎

Proof of Lemma
4.27

Proof.

We show that
\tilde{\leq}
is a partial order and that
\tilde{\#}
is symmetric and irreflexive.

•

\tilde{\leq}
is a partial order:

–

Reflexivity follows directly from Definition
4.26
.

–

Transitivity we note that the pairs are compose of the same event. Hence transitivity
holds.

–

Antisymmetry holds with the same argument as in transitivity.

•

\tilde{\#}
is symmetric and irreflexive since
\tilde{\#}
equals
\#
for the events in

\tilde{E}
.

We show
\tilde{\mathrm{U}}_{y}
obeys the conditions of a unitary event structure.

•

\set{e^{\prime}\mid e^{\prime}\tilde{\leq}e}
is finite

Trivially holds because every
e\in\tilde{E}
is only causally related to itself.

•

e\tilde{\#}e^{\prime}\tilde{\leq}e^{\prime\prime}\Rightarrow e\tilde{\#}e^{\prime\prime}

Trivially holds because every
e\in\tilde{E}
is only causally related to itself. Hence

e^{\prime}\tilde{\leq}e^{\prime\prime}
is by definition
e^{\prime}\tilde{\leq}e^{\prime}
. It then follows directly

e\tilde{\#}e^{\prime}\tilde{\leq}e^{\prime}\Rightarrow e\tilde{\#}e^{\prime}
.

•

e\text{ co }e^{\prime}\Rightarrow[\tilde{Q}(e),\tilde{Q}(e^{\prime})]=0

It follows directly since if
e\text{ co }e^{\prime}
in
\tilde{{\mathrm{U}}}
, then
e\text{ co }e^{\prime}
in

\mathrm{U}
, in which
[Q(e),Q(e^{\prime})]=0
. Hence

[\tilde{Q}(e),\tilde{Q}(e^{\prime})]=[Q|_{\tilde{E}}(e),Q|_{\tilde{E}}(e^{\prime})]=0

•

\mathrel{\!\!\!\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}{\hbox{\kern 17.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\!\!\!\!}
is transitive

It follows directly from the fact that
\tilde{\#}
is inherited from
\mathrm{U}
.

•

\forall e\in\tilde{E},\sum_{e^{\prime}\in[e]}Q(e^{\prime})
is unitary

Let
e\in\tilde{E}
. By definition we have

\tilde{Q}=Q|_{\tilde{E}}
. It then follows that

\sum_{e^{\prime}\in[e]}\tilde{Q}([e])=\sum_{e^{\prime}\in[e]}Q|_{\tilde{E}}(e^{\prime})
is unitary.

∎

Proof of Lemma
4.29

Proof.

We show that
\hat{\leq}
is a partial order and that
\hat{\#}
is symmetric and irreflexive.

•

\hat{\leq}
is a partial order:

–

Reflexivity follows directly from Definition
4.28
.

–

Transitivity we note that the pairs are compose of the same event. Hence transitivity
holds.

–

Antisymmetry holds with the same argument as in transitivity.

•

\hat{\#}
is symmetric and irreflexive since
\hat{\#}
is the empty relation.

We show
\hat{\mathrm{U}}
obeys the conditions of a unitary event structure. For convenience we
sometimes write
U
instead of
\sum_{e^{\prime}\in[e]}Q(e^{\prime})
.

•

\set{[e^{\prime}]\mid[e^{\prime}]\hat{\leq}[e]}
is finite

Trivially holds because every
[e]\in\hat{E}
is only causally related to itself.

•

[e]\hat{\#}[e^{\prime}]\hat{\leq}[e^{\prime\prime}]\Rightarrow[e]\hat{\#}[e^{\prime\prime}]

Trivially holds because the conflict relation is empty.

•

[e]\text{ co }[e^{\prime}]\Rightarrow[\hat{Q}([e]),\hat{Q}([e^{\prime}])]=0

We have three cases:

1.

|[e]|=|[e^{\prime}]|=1

It follows directly that
[\hat{Q}([e]),\hat{Q}([e^{\prime}])]=[Q(e),Q(e^{\prime})]=0

2.

|[e]|=1
and
|[e^{\prime}]|>1

We have

[\hat{Q}([e]),\hat{Q}([e^{\prime}])]=[Q(e),U]=0
, since the event
e
and all the events in
[e^{\prime}]
are concurrent.

3.

|[e]|>1
and
|[e^{\prime}]|>1

We have

[\hat{Q}([e]),\hat{Q}([e^{\prime}])]=[U_{1},U_{2}]=0
, since all the events in
[e]
are concurrent with the events in
[e^{\prime}]
.

•

\mathrel{\!\!\!\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}{\hbox{\kern 17.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\!\!\!\!}
is transitive

It follows directly since
\hat{\#}=\emptyset
.

•

\forall[e]\in\hat{E},\sum_{[e^{\prime}]\in[[e]]}\hat{Q}([e^{\prime}])
is unitary

Since
\hat{\#}=\emptyset
then
\sum_{[e^{\prime}]\in[[e]]}\hat{Q}([e^{\prime}])=\hat{Q}([e^{\prime}])
since

|[[e]]|=1
. By Definition
4.28
we have two cases:

1.

|[e^{\prime}]|=1

Then
\hat{Q}([e^{\prime}])=Q(e^{\prime})
which is a unitary.

2.

|[e]|>1

Then
\hat{Q}([e^{\prime}])=U
which is a unitary.

∎

Proof of Lemma
4.30

Proof.

We show that
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}
satisfies the conditions to be a map of event structures.

•

\forall x\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{E}})\Rightarrow proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}(x)\in\mathcal{C}(\hat{\mathrm{E}})

It follows straightforwardly since
\hat{\#}
is empty and
\hat{\leq}
is the trivial one.

•

\forall(e\neq e^{\prime})\in x\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{E}})
, if
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}
is
defined in both then
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}(e)\neq proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}(e^{\prime})

Let
(e\neq e^{\prime})\in x\in\mathcal{C}(\tilde{\mathrm{E}})
. Since
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}
is total,

proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}
is defined in both and since
e,e^{\prime}\in x
then
\neg(e\tilde{\#}e^{\prime})
.
Hence it follows straightforwardly that
proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}(e)\neq proj_{\tilde{\mathrm{E}}}(e^{\prime})
.

∎

Proof of Lemma
4.43

Proof.

Due to Lemma
2.40
we only focus on the condition of the quantum operators. Let

\mathrm{U}_{1}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1},\,Q_{1})
,
\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2},\,Q_{2})
, and

\mathrm{U}_{3}=(E_{3},\,\leq_{3},\,\#_{3},\,Q_{3})
be quantum event structures.

•

Reflexivity:
\mathrm{U}_{1}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}_{1}

It follows directly that
\forall e\in E_{1}\,.\,Q_{1}(e)=Q_{1}(e)

•

Transitivity :
\mathrm{U}_{1}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}_{2},\,\mathrm{U}_{2}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}_{3}\Rightarrow\mathrm{U}_{1}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}_{3}

From
\mathrm{U}_{1}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}_{2}
,
\forall e\in E_{1}\,.\,Q_{1}(e)=Q_{2}(e)
. From

\mathrm{U}_{2}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}_{3}
,
\forall e\in E_{2}\,.\,Q_{2}(e)=Q_{3}(e)
. Hence

\forall e\in E_{1}\,.\,Q_{1}(e)=Q_{2}(e)=Q_{3}(e)
.

•

Antisymmetry:
\mathrm{U}_{1}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}_{2},\,\mathrm{U}_{2}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}_{1}\Rightarrow\mathrm{U}_{1}=\mathrm{U}_{2}

From Lemma
2.40
we know that
E_{1}=E_{2}
. Hence it follows directly that

\mathrm{U}_{1}=\mathrm{U}_{2}
.

∎

Proof of Lemma
4.44

Proof.

We begin by showing that
\bot
is a unitary event structure. We already know that

(\emptyset,\,\emptyset,\,\emptyset)
is an event structure, hence it lacks to verify the
conditions regarding the quantum operator. However, since there are no events, the conditions
trivially holds.

To show that
\bot
is the least element, consider any unitary event structure
\mathrm{U}
. We need
to show
\bot\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}
. Due to Lemma
2.41
we only focus on
the quantum operator. We need to show that for every event in
\bot
, its mapping through
!
and

Q
is the same. We show it by contradiction. Thus, we need to find an event
e\in\emptyset

such that its mapping through
!
and
Q
is not the same. However, there are no events in

\bot
. Thus the condition holds.∎

Proof of Lemma
4.46

Proof.

Due to Lemma
2.43
we focus on the quantum operator condition.

•

\forall e,e^{\prime}\in E^{\omega}\,,\ e\text{ co }e^{\prime}\Rightarrow[Q^{\omega}(e),Q^{\omega}(e^{\prime})]=0

Let
e,e^{\prime}\in E^{\omega}
such that
e\text{ co }e^{\prime}
. We have two cases:

1.

e,e^{\prime}\in E_{n}

By Definition
4.45
we have
Q^{\omega}(e)=Q_{n}(e)
and
Q^{\omega}(e^{\prime})=Q_{n}(e^{\prime})
and
since
\mathrm{U}_{n}
is a unitary event structure we are done.

2.

e\in E_{n}
and
e^{\prime}\in E_{m}
such that
\mathrm{U}_{n}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}_{m}

By Definition
4.45
we have
Q^{\omega}(e)=Q_{n}(e)
and
Q^{\omega}(e^{\prime})=Q_{m}(e^{\prime})
.
From
\mathrm{U}_{n}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}_{m}
, we have that
Q_{n}(e)=Q_{m}(e)
and since
\mathrm{U}_{m}

is a unitary event structure, then we are done.

•

\mathrel{\!\!\!\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}{\hbox{\kern 17.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\!\!\!\!}
is transitive

We want to show that for
e,e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime}\in E^{\omega}
if
e\mathrel{\!\!\!\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}{\hbox{\kern 17.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\!\!\!\!}e^{\prime}
and

e^{\prime}\mathrel{\!\!\!\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}{\hbox{\kern 17.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\!\!\!\!}e^{\prime\prime}
then
e\mathrel{\!\!\!\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}{\hbox{\kern 17.22636pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\!\!\!\!}e^{\prime\prime}
.
According to Definition
4.45
we have three cases:

1.

e,e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime}\in E_{n}

Then we are done, since
\mathrm{U}_{n}
is a unitary event structure.

2.

e\in E_{n}
and
e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime}\in E_{m}
such that
\mathrm{U}_{n}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}_{m}

From
\mathrm{U}_{n}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}_{m}
we know that
E_{n}\subseteq E_{m}
, hence
e\in E_{m}
.
Since
\mathrm{U}_{m}
is a unitary event structure we are done.

3.

e\in E_{n}
,
e^{\prime}\in E_{m}
, and
e^{\prime\prime}\in E_{k}
such that
\mathrm{U}_{n}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}_{m}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}_{k}

From
\mathrm{U}_{n}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}_{m}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}_{k}
we have that
E_{n}\subseteq E_{m}\subseteq E_{k}
.
Hence,
e,e^{\prime}\in E_{k}
.
Since
\mathrm{U}_{k}
is a unitary event structure we are done.

•

\forall e\in E^{\omega},\ |[e]|>1\Rightarrow\sum_{e^{\prime}\in[e]}Q(e^{\prime})=U

Let
e\in E^{\omega}\,.\,|[e]|>1
. By Definition
4.45
,
\exists n\in\omega

such that
e\in E_{n}
and
Q^{\omega}(e)=Q_{n}(e)
. Since
\mathrm{U}_{n}
is a unitary event structure
we have
\sum_{e^{\prime}\in[e]}Q^{\omega}(e^{\prime})=\sum_{e^{\prime}\in[e]}Q_{n}(e^{\prime})=U
.

∎

Proof of Lemma
4.47

Proof.

Due to Lemma
2.44
we only need to focus on the quantum condition.

•

\mathrm{U}^{\omega}
is an upper bound

\forall n\in\omega
we need to have
\mathrm{U}_{n}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}^{\omega}
. We need to
check that
\forall e\in E_{n}\,.\,Q_{n}(e)=Q^{\omega}(e)
. It follows directly from
Definition
4.45
that
\exists n\in\omega\,.\,e\in E_{n}
and

Q^{\omega}(e)=Q_{n}(e)
.

•

\mathrm{U}^{\omega}
is a least upper bound

Let
\mathrm{U}=(E,\,\leq,\,\#,\,Q)
be an upper bound of the chain. We need to show that if

\mathrm{U}_{n}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}^{\omega}
and
\mathrm{U}_{n}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}
then

\mathrm{U}^{\omega}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}
. From
\mathrm{U}_{n}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}^{\omega}
,

\forall e\in E_{n}\,.\,Q_{n}(e)=Q^{\omega}(e)
. By Definition
4.45
,

\exists n\in\omega\,.\,e\in E_{n}
and
Q^{\omega}(e)=Q_{n}(e)
. From

\mathrm{U}_{n}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}
,
\forall e\in E_{n}\,.\,Q_{n}(e)=Q(e)
. Thus

\forall e\in\mathrm{U}^{\omega},\ \exists n\in\omega\,.\,e\in E_{n}
and

Q^{\omega}(e)=Q_{n}(e)=Q(e)
.

∎

Proof of Lemma
4.48

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}=(E,\,\leq,\,\#,\,Q)

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{1}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1},\,Q_{1})

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2},\,Q_{2})

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}\,;\,\mathrm{U}_{1}=(E^{1},\,\leq^{1},\,\#^{1},\,Q^{1})

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}\,;\,\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E^{2},\,\leq^{2},\,\#^{2},\,Q^{2})

such that
\mathrm{U}_{1}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}_{2}
.

We want to show
\forall e\in E^{1}\,.,\ Q^{1}(e)=Q^{2}(e)
. Due to Lemma
2.46

we focus solely on the quantum condition. Let
e\in E^{1}
. By Definition
4.5
we
have two cases:

1.

e\in E

It follows directly that
Q^{1}(e)=Q(e)=Q^{2}(e)
.

2.

e=(e_{1},x)\in E_{1}\times\mathcal{C}_{\text{max}}(\mathrm{U})

We have
Q^{1}(e)=Q_{1}(e_{1})
.
From
\mathrm{U}_{1}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}_{2}
,
Q_{1}(e_{1})=Q_{2}(e_{1})
.
By Definition
4.5
,
Q_{2}(e_{1})=Q^{2}(e)
.
Thus
Q^{1}(e)=Q^{2}(e)
.

∎

Proof of Lemma
4.49

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{1}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1},\,Q_{1})

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2},\,Q_{2})

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{1}=(E^{\prime}_{1},\,\leq^{\prime}_{1},\,\#^{\prime}_{1},\,Q^{\prime}_{1})

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E^{\prime}_{2},\,\leq^{\prime}_{2},\,\#^{\prime}_{2},\,Q^{\prime}_{2})

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E,\,\leq,\,\#,\,Q)

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{1}\,||\,\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{2}=(E^{\prime},\,\leq^{\prime},\,\#^{\prime},\,Q^{\prime})

such that
\mathrm{U}_{1}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{1}
and
\mathrm{U}_{2}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{2}
.

We want to show that
\forall e\in E\,.\,Q(e)=Q^{\prime}(e)
. Due to Lemma
2.47

we focus solely on the quantum condition. Let
e\in E
. By Definition
4.7
we
have two cases:

1.

e\in E_{1}

We know that
Q(e)=Q_{1}(e)
. Since
\mathrm{U}_{1}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{1}
,
Q_{1}(e)=Q^{\prime}_{1}(e)
.
By Definition
4.7
,
Q^{\prime}_{1}(e)=Q^{\prime}(e)
. Thus
Q(e)=Q^{\prime}(e)
.

2.

e\in E_{2}

Similar to the previous.

∎

Proof of Lemma
4.50

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{1}=(E_{1},\,\leq_{1},\,\#_{1},\,Q_{1})

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E_{2},\,\leq_{2},\,\#_{2},\,Q_{2})

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{1}=(E^{\prime}_{1},\,\leq^{\prime}_{1},\,\#^{\prime}_{1},\,Q^{\prime}_{1})

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{2}=(E^{\prime}_{2},\,\leq^{\prime}_{2},\,\#^{\prime}_{2},\,Q^{\prime}_{2})

\displaystyle M(n,\mathrm{U}_{1},\mathrm{U}_{2})=(E,\,\leq,\,\#,\,Q)

\displaystyle M(n,\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{1},\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{2})=(E^{\prime},\,\leq^{\prime},\,\#^{\prime},\,Q^{\prime})

such that
\mathrm{U}_{1}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{1}
and
\mathrm{U}_{2}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{2}
.

The conditions to check are:

1.

E\subseteq E^{\prime}

2.

\forall e,e^{\prime}\,.\,e\leq e^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow e,e^{\prime}\in E\wedge e\leq^{\prime}e^{\prime}

3.

\forall e,e^{\prime}\,.\,e\#e^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow e,e^{\prime}\in E\wedge e\#^{\prime}e^{\prime}

4.

\forall e\in E\,.\,Q(e)=Q^{\prime}(e)

The first three conditions follow directly from Definition
4.9
. Hence we focus on
the last one.

Let
e\in E
.
We have the four cases:

1.

e=\tau_{0}^{n}

By Definition
4.9
we are done since,
Q(\tau_{0}^{n})=Q^{\prime}(\tau_{0}^{n})
.

2.

e=\tau_{1}^{n}

By Definition
4.9
we are done since,
Q(\tau_{1}^{n})=Q^{\prime}(\tau_{1}^{n})
.

3.

e\in E_{1}

We know that
Q(e)=Q_{1}(e)
.
From
\mathrm{U}_{1}\trianglelefteq\mathrm{U}^{\prime}_{1}
,
Q_{1}(e)=Q^{\prime}_{1}(e)
.
By Definition
4.9
,
Q^{\prime}_{1}(e)=Q^{\prime}(e)
.
Thus
Q(e)=Q^{\prime}(e)
.

4.

e\in E_{2}

Similar to the previous point.

∎

Proof.

Similar to Lemma
2.50
.

Proof.

2.51
.

Proof of Lemma
4.53

Proof.

By Lemma
4.50
, the measurement is monotone. Then we only need to show that
each event of
M(q,\bigsqcup_{n}\mathrm{U}_{n},\bigsqcup_{m}\mathrm{U}_{m})
is an event of

\bigsqcup_{n,m}(M(q,\mathrm{U}_{n},\mathrm{U}_{m}))
. Let
e
be an event of

M(q,\bigsqcup_{n}\mathrm{U}_{n},\bigsqcup_{m}\mathrm{U}_{m})
. We have four cases:

1.

e=\tau_{0}^{n}

It follows directly from Definition
4.9
.

2.

e=\tau_{1}^{n}

It follows directly from Definition
4.9
.

3.

e
is an event of
\bigsqcup_{n}\mathrm{U}_{n}

By Definition
4.45
,
\exists n\in\omega
such that
e
is an event of
\mathrm{U}_{n}
.
By Definition
4.9
,
e
is an event of
M(q,\mathrm{U}_{n},\mathrm{U}_{m})
. By
Definition
4.45
,
e
is an event of
\bigsqcup_{n,m}(M(q,\mathrm{U}_{n},\mathrm{U}_{m}))
.

4.

e
is an event of
\bigsqcup_{m}\mathrm{U}_{m}

Similar to the previous point.

∎

Proof of Lemma
4.55

Proof.

•

while\ M(n,C)\xrightarrow{\tau_{0}^{n}}\checkmark

We know that

\llbracket while\ M(n,C)\rrbracket=\llbracket M(n,\checkmark,C\,;\,while\ M(n,C))\rrbracket

Hence

\llbracket while\ M(n,C)\rrbracket\backslash\tau_{0}^{n}=\llbracket M(n,\checkmark,C\,;\,while\ M(n,C))\rrbracket\backslash\tau_{0}^{n}

By Lemma
4.19
, we have
\llbracket\checkmark\rrbracket
. Hence

\llbracket while\ M(n,C)\rrbracket\backslash\tau_{0}^{n}=\llbracket\checkmark\rrbracket
.

•

while\ M(n,C)\xrightarrow{\tau_{1}^{n}}C\,;\,while\ M(n,C)

We know that

\llbracket while\ M(n,C)\rrbracket=\llbracket M(n,\checkmark,C\,;\,while\ M(n,C))\rrbracket

Hence

\llbracket while\ M(n,C)\rrbracket\backslash\tau_{1}^{n}=\llbracket M(n,\checkmark,C\,;\,while\ M(n,C))\rrbracket\backslash\tau_{1}^{n}

By Lemma
4.19
, we have
\llbracket C\,;\,while\ M(n,C)\rrbracket
. Hence

\llbracket while\ M(n,C)\rrbracket\backslash\tau_{0}^{n}=\llbracket C\,;\,while\ M(n,C)\rrbracket
.

∎

Proof of Lemma
4.56

Proof.

•

l\in\mathcal{I}(\llbracket while\ M(n,C)\rrbracket)

Since
\llbracket while\ M(n,C)\rrbracket=\llbracket M(n,\checkmark,C\,;\,while\ M(n,C))\rrbracket
, we have
that
l=\tau_{0}^{n}
or
l=\tau_{1}^{n}
.
We have two cases:

1.

l=\tau_{0}^{n}

Let
C^{\prime}=\checkmark
. We know that

\llbracket while\ M(n,C)\rrbracket=\llbracket M(n,\checkmark,C\,;\,while\ M(n,C))\rrbracket

Hence

\llbracket M(n,\checkmark,C\,;\,while\ M(n,C))\rrbracket\backslash\tau_{0}^{n}

By Lemma
4.19
, we have
\llbracket\checkmark\rrbracket
. Thus it follows directly that

while\ M(n,C)\xrightarrow{\tau_{0}^{n}}\checkmark
.

2.

l=\tau_{1}^{n}

Let
C^{\prime}=C\,;\,while\ M(n,C)
. We know that

\llbracket while\ M(n,C)\rrbracket=\llbracket M(n,\checkmark,C\,;\,while\ M(n,C))\rrbracket

Hence

\llbracket M(n,\checkmark,C\,;\,while\ M(n,C))\rrbracket\backslash\tau_{1}^{n}

By Lemma
4.19
, we have
\llbracket\checkmark\rrbracket
. Thus it follows directly that

while\ M(n,C)\xrightarrow{\tau_{1}^{n}}C\,;\,while\ M(n,C)
.

∎
